On the Learning Dynamics of RLVR at the Edge of Competence
Yu Huang
Zixin Wen 1 1 footnotemark:

Yuejie Chi

† † thanks: Department of Statistics and Data Science, Wharton School, University of Pennsylvania. † † thanks: Machine Learning Department, Carnegie Mellon University. † † thanks: Department of Statistics and Data Science, Yale University. † † thanks: Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering, The Ohio State University.

August 24, 2026
Abstract
Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has been a main driver of recent breakthroughs in large reasoning models. Yet it remains a mystery how rewards based solely on final outcomes can help overcome the long-horizon barrier to extended reasoning. To understand this, we develop a theory of the training dynamics of RL for transformers on compositional reasoning tasks. Our theory characterizes how the effectiveness of RLVR is governed by the smoothness of the difficulty spectrum. When data contains abrupt discontinuities in difficulty, learning undergoes grokking-type phase transitions, producing prolonged plateaus before progress recurs. In contrast, a smooth difficulty spectrum leads to a relay effect : persistent gradient signals on easier problems elevate the model’s capabilities to the point where harder ones become tractable, resulting in steady and continuous improvement. Our theory explains how RLVR can improve performance at the edge of competence, and suggests that appropriately designed data mixtures can yield scalable gains. As a technical contribution, our analysis develops and adapts tools from Fourier analysis on finite groups to our setting. We validate the predicted mechanisms empirically via synthetic experiments.
1 Introduction
Large language models (LLMs) such as OpenAI-o3 ( OpenAI,, 2024 ) and DeepSeek ( DeepSeek-AI,, 2025 ) have shown striking performance in complex reasoning tasks. A key enabler of this recent progress is reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) ( Shao et al.,, 2024 ; Lambert et al.,, 2024 ; Gao et al.,, 2024 ) , which fine-tunes pre-trained base models via reinforcement learning (RL) using automatically verifiable, outcome-based feedback, such as a binary signal indicating whether the final answer is correct.
This raises an immediate question: if RLVR relies on outcome-based feedback only at the end of a reasoning trajectory, how can such sparse reward mechanism drive effective learning on long-horizon problems? As the horizon grows, RL algorithms encounter an inherent search barrier, because useful signals are buried within an exponentially expanding space of trajectories. While recent studies have sought to understand the mechanism of RLVR ( Yeo et al.,, 2025 ; Wu et al.,, 2025 ; Yue et al.,, 2025 ; Sun et al.,, 2025 ; Yuan et al.,, 2025 ; Wen et al.,, 2025 ) , existing findings often provide mixed and inconclusive results across different tasks and setups. It remains unclear under what conditions the outcome rewards are sufficient to ensure effective RL.
A recent controlled study ( Zhang et al.,, 2025 ) proposed an important insight: RLVR is only effective when training operates near the model’s edge of competence , where the model can solve the problems with non-random accuracy but has not yet mastered them. This suggests a principle for RL data design: one should select problems that are right at the edge of the model’s competence for effective training. Yet, this principle is mainly descriptive: it suggests where RL tends to work, but does not explain why its effectiveness is confined to this regime. This motivates us to ask the following intriguing question:
Why does RLVR primarily improve performance near the edge of the model’s competence?
t t d ​ r d ​ t \dfrac{\mathrm{d}r}{\mathrm{d}t} peak peak plateau plateau
(a) If difficulty ratio R = L k + 1 / L k ≫ 1 R=L_{k+1}/L_{k}\gg 1 , learning exhibits phase transitions in-between difficulty levels.
t t d ​ r d ​ t \dfrac{\mathrm{d}r}{\mathrm{d}t} peak peak peak relay relay relay
(b) If difficulty ratio R = L k + 1 / L k ≲ 1 R=L_{k+1}/L_{k}\lesssim 1 , learning process proceeds smoothly across different difficulty levels from L 1 L_{1} to L max L_{\max} .
t t r ⁡ ( t ) r(t) grokking (a) relay (b)
(c) Reward trajectories r ⁡ ( t ) r(t) induced by different ratio R R in (a) grokking dynamics and (b) smooth relay dynamics.
Figure 1 : Reward-growth dynamics in mixed-difficulty RL.
A schematic illustration of the reward growth rate d ​ r / d ​ t \mathrm{d}r/\mathrm{d}t and r ⁡ ( t ) r(t) for mixed-difficulty RL, demonstrating how the difficulty ratio R = L k + 1 / L k R=L_{k+1}/L_{k} changes the learning dynamics at the edge of model’s competence, which yields either grokking-type phase transitions or smooth relays.
To address this question, we study a multi-step compositional reasoning setting where solving a problem requires sequential steps, yet feedback is provided only through a terminal reward. Our model is a minimal transformer model ( Vaswani et al.,, 2017 ) , which is the backbone architecture of most LLMs. It consists of a softmax-based attention layer followed by a multilayer perceptron (MLP) layer. We fix the MLP to perfectly implement the atomic operation, modeling the regime where the model already possesses the requisite atomic skill and RLVR only needs to learn how to compose these skills ( Yuan et al.,, 2025 ) . We study RL training on this task under outcome-based rewards via the standard policy gradient algorithm REINFORCE ( Williams,, 1992 ) . Within this setting, we track the learning dynamics of the transformer model and identify the factors that govern progress across increasing horizons, thereby shedding insights on when RLVR can (or cannot) scale to long-horizon compositional reasoning. An overview of our main contributions is provided below.
1.
A comparative study between short-horizon learning vs. long-horizon barrier. We first show that with outcome-based rewards, REINFORCE -style policy gradient algorithms provably learn short-horizon compositions. Meanwhile, even if the initial policy achieves non-zero reward, the gradient field at initialization is exponentially flat beyond a critical horizon, indicating an optimization barrier for near-random policies. Alternatively, we show that supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can provably learn beyond the critical horizon by providing intermediate feedback for sequential compositional reasoning.
2.
A theory of phase transitions in RLVR on mixed-difficulty data distribution. On an easy-to-hard mixture over horizons, we establish polynomial-time convergence guarantees for outcome-based RL training. We further show that the shape of the difficulty spectrum in the mixture governs the dynamics: when the difficulty spectrum contains discontinuities, the learning process undergoes long plateaus followed by abrupt improvement, exhibiting grokking -like phase transitions ( Sun et al.,, 2025 ) ; In comparison, a smoother spectrum yields a relay effect that maintains the momentum of reward growth, ensuring steady progress through increasingly harder problems.
3.
Novel techniques from Fourier analysis on groups. We introduce a Fourier analysis ( Terras,, 1999 ) framework that transforms the problem of trajectory-level success conditioning into tractable calculations based on Fourier analysis for convolutions of measures. Our new framework allows us to compute the magnitude of policy gradients in long-horizon group composition problems by resorting to the spectral properties of the group representations, which greatly simplifies the estimation of combinatorial event probabilities.
An optimization perspective for RL at the edge of competence.
Together, our results provide an optimization-based explanation for why RLVR is most effective near the edge of the model’s competence. The key mechanism is the relay effect : when the training distribution includes problems slightly above the model’s current capability, successful solutions generated by the policy provide sufficient gradient signal to bootstrap learning on harder ones, sustaining steady reward improvement. However, this relay breaks down when there is a large gap between the model’s current competence and the next level of difficulty. In this regime, the gradient signal is overwhelmed by noise from rare random successes, and training stalls in prolonged plateaus. Thus, RLVR’s effectiveness is governed not merely by whether the model achieves non-zero reward, but by whether the difficulty landscape provides a smooth curriculum for the relay effect to operate. Figure 1 illustrates the contrast between these two regimes.
2 Related Work
Empirical understanding of RLVR mechanisms.
With RLVR’s recent success and apparent scalability, there has been growing interest in understanding its mechanics, sparking an active debate: what does RLVR actually teach base LLMs beyond pre-training? The existing literature provides mixed evidence. Some works characterize RL primarily as a capability refiner or reranker ( Yeo et al.,, 2025 ; Wu et al.,, 2025 ; Yue et al.,, 2025 ; Zhao et al.,, 2025 ) , whereas others argue that RL can induce substantial reasoning gains beyond the base model ( Sun et al.,, 2025 ; Yuan et al.,, 2025 ; Wen et al.,, 2025 ; Liu et al.,, 2025 ) . Related findings suggest that RLVR can yield notable improvements even from spurious rewards ( Shao et al.,, 2025 ) or extremely limited RL data (e.g., one-shot) ( Wang et al., 2025b, ) . Complementary evidence further highlights an entropy-based mechanism as a potential driver of such gains ( Cui et al.,, 2025 ; Wang et al., 2025a, ) . Recently, Zhang et al., (2025) helps reconcile these views by showing that genuine capability gains arise mainly when there is sufficient headroom beyond pre-training and the RL data are calibrated to the model’s edge of competence.
Theory of RL training for LLM.
The empirical success of RLVR has spurred theoretical studies from various perspectives ( Chen et al., 2025a, ; Chen et al., 2025b, ; Zhu et al.,, 2025 ; Ran-milo et al.,, 2026 ; Rad et al.,, 2026 ; Lyu et al.,, 2025 ; Bu et al.,, 2025 ; Tsilivis et al.,, 2025 ; Davis and Recht,, 2025 ) . Of particular relevance are gradient-based analyses with transformer policies ( Lyu et al.,, 2025 ; Bu et al.,, 2025 ; Ran-milo et al.,, 2026 ) . Bu et al., (2025) formalize the benefits of curriculum-style RL post-training, while Lyu et al., (2025) studies learnability in settings with intermediate supervision. In contrast, we focus on outcome-based RL where no dense feedback is available. A closely related work ( Ran-milo et al.,, 2026 ) shows that outcome-based RL can induce step-by-step reasoning under gradient flow, with asymptotic guarantees under static data conditions. We go beyond this by analyzing realistic gradient dynamics along the full learning trajectory and characterizing phase transitions in both reward and gradient.
Grokking in supervised learning and RL.
Grokking characterizes delayed generalization in supervised learning,
where performance stays flat for long periods before improving abruptly ( Power et al.,, 2022 ) . Similar plateau-to-jump dynamics also appear in RLVR training ( Sun et al.,, 2025 ) , and have been described as “aha moments” in RL systems such as DeepSeek-R1-Zero ( DeepSeek-AI,, 2025 ) . Prior work investigates mechanisms behind such phase changes ( Nanda et al.,, 2023 ; Kumar et al.,, 2024 ; Liu et al., 2022b, ; Tian,, 2025 ) , while our theory on the learning dynamics provides a mechanistic explanation for both the plateau and the subsequent transition in RLVR.
3 Problem Setup
In this section, we formalize our theoretical setting. We first define the compositional reasoning problem, then describe the minimalist transformer architecture and the policy gradient objective used to study RL training dynamics.

3.1 Compositional Reasoning
To study the mechanistic challenges of multi-step reasoning, we consider the state-tracking task ( Liu et al., 2022a, ; Merrill et al.,, 2024 ; Huang et al., 2025b, ) . This setting serves as a simple example of compositional reasoning: while each individual step is computationally simple, the task requires the precise sequential composition of L L transitions in order to compute the final result.
Definition 3.1 ( L L -step compositional reasoning) .
Let 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} be a finite set (the state space ) and 𝒢 \mathcal{G} a finite group,
acting on 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} via ( g , y ) ↦ g ⁡ ( y ) (g,y)\mapsto g(y) . For any initial state y 0 ∈ 𝒴 y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y} and sequence of transitions g 1 , … , g L ∈ 𝒢 g_{1},\dots,g_{L}\in\mathcal{G} ,
we can obtain a trajectory y ℓ = g ℓ ​ ( y ℓ − 1 ) y_{\ell}=g_{\ell}(y_{\ell-1}) where ℓ = 1 , … , L \ell=1,\dots,L . The goal is to predict the final state y L y_{L} given the sequence ( y 0 , g 1 , … , g L ) (y_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{L}) .
We define L L as the length or horizon of the problem.
Our analysis requires some structural assumptions on the group action of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} on 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , which we present below.
Assumption 3.1 (Group structure and action) .
We assume 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is a finite non-abelian simple group that acts simply transitively on the set 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , which implies that there is a bijective correspondence between group elements and states, such that | 𝒢 | = | 𝒴 | = d |\mathcal{G}|=|\mathcal{Y}|=d . We focus on the asymptotic regime where the state space scales ( d → ∞ d\rightarrow\infty ).
Remark 3.1 .
Due to the inherent mixing properties of non-abelian groups ( Larsen and Shalev,, 2007 ) , different sequences of operations rarely lead to the same state unless the sequences are identical. However, in abelian groups, composing the same operations in different orders yields the same result. Requiring the group to be non-abelian reduces this type of shortcut solutions and leads to cleaner analysis.
Next, we specify the format of reasoning data and the distribution over such instances.
Definition 3.2 (Reasoning problems) .
Fix a set of positional identifiers 𝒳 ⊂ ℝ d \mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} consisting of mutually
orthogonal vectors.
We encode each token as a position–symbol pair ( x , s ) (x,s) where
x ∈ 𝒳 x\in\mathcal{X} and s ∈ 𝒢 ∪ 𝒴 s\in\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{Y} . A length- L L reasoning instance is Z L = ( Z p L , Z a L ) Z^{L}=(Z_{p}^{L},Z_{a}^{L}) consisting of:
•
problem description (prompt): a sequence of L L transition tokens:
Z p L = ( ( x p , 1 , g 1 ) , ( x p , 2 , g 2 ) , … , ( x p , L , g L ) ) , Z_{p}^{L}=\big((x_{p,1},g_{1}),(x_{p,2},g_{2}),\dots,(x_{p,L},g_{L})\big),
where x p , ℓ ∈ 𝒳 x_{p,\ell}\in\mathcal{X} are distinct and g ℓ ∈ 𝒢 g_{\ell}\in\mathcal{G} .
•
compositional solution: a sequence of
( L + 1 ) (L+1) state tokens:
Z a L = ( ( x a , 0 , y 0 ) , ( x a , 1 , y 1 ) , … , ( x a , L , y L ) ) , Z_{a}^{L}=\big((x_{a,0},y_{0}),(x_{a,1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{a,L},y_{L})\big),
where y ℓ = g ℓ ​ ( y ℓ − 1 ) y_{\ell}=g_{\ell}(y_{\ell-1}) for all ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] .
•
position alignment: the prompt and solution positions are related
by a fixed unknown permutation 𝔰 : 𝒳 → 𝒳 \mathfrak{s}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X} such that x a , ℓ − 1 = 𝔰 ⁡ ( x p , ℓ ) x_{a,\ell-1}=\mathfrak{s}(x_{p,\ell}) , ∀ ℓ ∈ [ L ] \forall\ell\in[L] .
Now we are ready to define the data distribution for the reasoning problems.
Definition 3.3 (Data distribution 𝒟 L \mathcal{D}^{L} ) .
Given a problem length L L , we sample a reasoning instance Z L = ( Z p L , Z a L ) Z^{L}=(Z_{p}^{L},Z_{a}^{L}) as in Definition 3.2 by the following process:
1.
g 1 , … , g L g_{1},\dots,g_{L} is sampled from 𝒢 \mathcal{G} uniformly without replacement;
2.
the initial state y 0 y_{0} is sampled uniformly at random from the set 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} ;
3.
we sample distinct prompt identifiers { x p , 1 , … , x p , L } \{x_{p,1},\dots,x_{p,L}\}
uniformly from 𝒳 \mathcal{X} ;
4.
Set x a , ℓ − 1 = 𝔰 ⁡ ( x p , ℓ ) , ∀ ℓ ∈ { 1 , … , L } . x_{a,\ell-1}=\mathfrak{s}(x_{p,\ell}),\ \forall\ell\in\{1,\dots,L\}. Additionally, x a , L x_{a,L} is sampled from 𝒳 ∖ { x a , k } k = 0 L − 1 \mathcal{X}\setminus\{x_{a,k}\}_{k=0}^{L-1} .
5.
the intermediate states are computed via the group action: y ℓ = g ℓ ​ ( y ℓ − 1 ) y_{\ell}=g_{\ell}(y_{\ell-1}) .
We say Z L ≜ ( Z p L , Z a L ) Z^{L}\triangleq(Z_{p}^{L},Z_{a}^{L}) is sampled from 𝒟 L \mathcal{D}^{L} . Since both { g ℓ } ℓ = 1 L \{g_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L} and { x p , ℓ } ℓ = 1 L \{x_{p,\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L} are sampled
without replacement, the length is bounded by
L ≤ min ⁡ { | 𝒳 | − 1 , d } L\leq\min\{|\mathcal{X}|-1,d\} .
Assumption 3.2 .
We assume | 𝒳 | = Θ ⁡ ( d c x ) |\mathcal{X}|=\Theta(d^{c_{x}}) for some constant c x ∈ ( 0.1 , 1 ) c_{x}\in(0.1,1) . We denote L max = | 𝒳 | − 1 L_{\max}=|\mathcal{X}|-1 as the unique symbols in 𝒳 \mathcal{X} defines the maximum problem length.
Embeddings and tokenizer.
We embed each symbol s ∈ 𝒢 ∪ 𝒴 s\in\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{Y} into ℝ 2 ​ d \mathbb{R}^{2d} via an orthonormal map
𝖾𝗆𝖻 : 𝒢 ∪ 𝒴 → ℝ 2 ​ d \mathsf{emb}:\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d} .
With a slight abuse of notation, whenever a symbol s s is used as a model input
(or appears inside network computations), we write s s to mean its embedding
𝖾𝗆𝖻 ⁡ ( s ) ∈ ℝ 2 ​ d \mathsf{emb}(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d} . We also fix a bijective tokenizer τ : 𝒴 → [ d ] \tau:\mathcal{Y}\to[d] to index states for the
next-state prediction objective, so that predicting y ∈ 𝒴 y\in\mathcal{Y} is equivalent to
predicting the class label τ ⁡ ( y ) ∈ [ d ] \tau(y)\in[d] .
3.2 Transformer Architecture
Building on the reasoning format in
Definition 3.2 and 3.3 , we now define a simple transformer that predicts the next state in the solution trace given the prompt and the current state token.
Definition 3.4 (One-layer transformer ( Vaswani et al.,, 2017 ) ) .
We consider a simplified transformer consisting of a single attention module followed by a one-hidden-layer MLP. For a prompt
Z p L = ( ( x p , ℓ , g ℓ ) ) ℓ = 1 L Z_{p}^{L}=((x_{p,\ell},g_{\ell}))_{\ell=1}^{L}
and the current solution token
Z a , k = ( x a , k , y k ) Z_{a,k}=(x_{a,k},y_{k}) , the transformer with parameter θ = ( W , Q ) \theta=(W,Q) outputs an (unnormalized) score vector over the next-state index in [ d ] [d] :
𝖳𝖥 θ ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) = 𝖬𝖫𝖯 W ​ ( 𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇 Q ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) ) ∈ ℝ d . \displaystyle\mathsf{TF}_{\theta}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L})=\mathsf{MLP}_{W}\left(\mathsf{Attn}_{Q}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L})\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Attention Layer.
The attention module uses the current solution position x a , k x_{a,k} to form weights
over the prompt positions { x p , ℓ } ℓ = 1 L \{x_{p,\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L} , and returns a vector that aggregates the transition embeddings. Specifically, given a query weight Q ∈ ℝ d × d Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} , we define:
𝖠𝗍𝗍𝗇 Q ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) ≜ 1 2 ​ ( y k + ∑ ℓ = 1 L 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , k → p , ℓ ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) ⋅ g ℓ ) ∈ ℝ 2 ​ d , \displaystyle\textstyle\mathsf{Attn}_{Q}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L})\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\Big(y_{k}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbf{Attn}_{a,k\to p,\ell}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L})\cdot g_{\ell}\Big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},
(1)
where the attention weight 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , k → p , ℓ ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) \mathbf{Attn}_{a,k\to p,\ell}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L}) is obtained by softmax-normalizing the inner
products ⟨ Q ​ x a , k , x p , ℓ ⟩ \langle Qx_{a,k},x_{p,\ell}\rangle :
𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑 ​ ( ( , , , , , ) ) ℓ . \displaystyle\mathsf{softmax}\Big((\langle Qx_{a,k},x_{p,1}\rangle,\dots,\langle Qx_{a,k},x_{p,L}\rangle)\Big)_{\ell}.
(2)
Here for any u ∈ ℝ n u\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , 𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑 ​ ( u ) i ≜ exp ⁡ ( u i ) ∑ j = 1 n exp ⁡ ( u j ) \mathsf{softmax}(u)_{i}\triangleq\frac{\exp(u_i)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\exp(u_j)} . In standard transformer architectures, the score typically takes the form
⟨ W Q ​ x a , k , W K ​ x p , ℓ ⟩ \langle W_{Q}x_{a,k},W_{K}x_{p,\ell}\rangle rather than using a single matrix Q Q .
We adopt the equivalent reparameterization commonly used in theoretical works to simplify analysis without
changing expressivity ( Huang et al.,, 2023 ; Yang et al.,, 2024 ; Zhang et al.,, 2024 ) . The factor 1 2 \frac{1}{2} normalizes the combined contribution of the residual term y k y_{k} and the attention output.
Remark 3.2 .
We compute attention scores using only positional identifiers
x a , k , x p , ℓ x_{a,k},x_{p,\ell} , a standard simplification in theoretical analyses of transformers ( Jelassi et al.,, 2022 ; Huang et al., 2025a, ; Wen et al.,, 2024 ; Kim and Suzuki,, 2024 ; Cheng et al.,, 2026 ) . This decoupling separates token association from reasoning with vector embeddings (states and transitions), which will be carried out by the subsequent MLP computation. Since the attention scores depend only on ( x a , k , x p , 1 : L ) (x_{a,k},x_{p,1:L}) , we suppress the arguments and write 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , k → p , ℓ \mathbf{Attn}_{a,k\to p,\ell} instead of 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , k → p , ℓ ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) \mathbf{Attn}_{a,k\to p,\ell}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L}) when clear from context.
MLP layer.
Given the attention output in ℝ 2 ​ d \mathbb{R}^{2d} , the MLP maps it to logits in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}
for next-state prediction (indexed by τ : 𝒴 → [ d ] \tau:\mathcal{Y}\to[d] ). With m m hidden units and ReLU activation σ ⁡ ( z ) = max ⁡ { 0 , z } \sigma(z)=\max\{0,z\} , for each j ∈ [ d ] j\in[d] ,
[ 𝖬𝖫𝖯 W ​ ( Z ) ] j = ∑ r = 1 m σ ⁡ ( ⟨ W j , r , Z ⟩ ) , W j , r ∈ ℝ 2 ​ d . \textstyle\bigl[\mathsf{MLP}_{W}(Z)\bigr]_{j}=\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sigma\left(\langle W_{j,r},Z\rangle\right),\quad W_{j,r}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.
In our study, we will keep W W fixed and assume the MLP has already acquired pre-trained atomic skills for one-step transitions: given a state y y and a transition g g , it implements the map y ↦ g ⁡ ( y ) y\mapsto g(y) . Our focus is therefore on how the attention module supports long-horizon composition once the model already possesses the one-step atomic skill, which has been actively studied in recent RLVR exploration ( Yuan et al.,, 2025 ; Park et al.,, 2025 ) .
Induced next-state distribution.
Given the transformer’s output
𝖳𝖥 θ ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L}) , we define
the induced next-state distribution (policy) by softmax normalization:
π θ ​ ( j ∣ Z a , k , Z p L ) ≜ 𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑 ​ ( 𝖳𝖥 θ ​ ( Z a , k , Z p L ) ) j , \displaystyle\pi_{\theta}\big(j\mid Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L}\big)\triangleq\mathsf{softmax}\Big(\mathsf{TF}_{\theta}(Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L})\Big)_{j},
(3)
where j ∈ [ d ] j\in[d] . We equivalently write for y ∈ 𝒴 y\in\mathcal{Y} ,
π θ ​ ( y ∣ Z a , k , Z p L ) ≜ π θ ​ ( τ ⁡ ( y ) ∣ Z a , k , Z p L ) \pi_{\theta}(y\mid Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L})\triangleq\pi_{\theta}(\tau(y)\mid Z_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L}) .
In our reasoning format, the answer positions ( x a , 1 , … , x a , L ) (x_{a,1},\dots,x_{a,L}) are part
of the instance and are not predicted; the model only predicts the next state
symbol at each step.
Starting from the prefix Z L , 0 = [ Z a , 0 , Z p L ] Z^{L,0}=[Z_{a,0},Z_{p}^{L}] , the induced distribution
over the generated state sequence y ^ L = ( y ^ 1 , … , y ^ L ) \widehat{y}^{L}=(\widehat{y}_{1},\dots,\widehat{y}_{L})
factorizes autoregressively as
π θ ​ ( y ^ L ∣ Z L , 0 ) \displaystyle\pi_{\theta}(\widehat{y}^{L}\mid Z^{L,0})
= ∏ k = 0 L − 1 π θ ​ ( y ^ k + 1 ∣ Z ^ a , k , Z p L ) , \displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{L-1}\pi_{\theta}(\widehat{y}_{k+1}\mid\widehat{Z}_{a,k},Z_{p}^{L}),
(4)
where
Z ^ a , k = ( x a , k , y ^ k ) , y ^ 0 = y 0 . \displaystyle\widehat{Z}_{a,k}=(x_{a,k},\widehat{y}_{k}),\ \widehat{y}_{0}=y_{0}.
When no confusion arises, we abbreviate the conditioning as ( y 0 , G L ) (y_{0},G^{L}) , where
G L = ( g 1 , … , g L ) G^{L}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{L}) .
Formally, π θ L ( ⋅ ∣ y 0 , G L ) \pi_{\theta}^{L}(\cdot\mid y_{0},G^{L}) still conditions on the full
instance Z L Z^{L} (including ( x p , 1 : L , x a , 0 : L ) (x_{p,1:L},x_{a,0:L}) and the fixed permutation π \pi ); we simply suppress these positional variables in the notation.
3.3 Pretrained Atomic Skills
We assume that the MLP module provides a pre-trained atomic skill for single-step transitions, and we keep its parameter W W fixed throughout RL training. This allows us to focus on long-horizon composition in the attention dynamics.
For each output index j ∈ [ d ] j\in[d] and hidden neuron r ∈ [ m ] r\in[m] , let us define the feature magnitude
V j , r ​ ( s ) ≜ ⟨ W j , r , s ⟩ , s ∈ 𝒢 ∪ 𝒴 . V_{j,r}(s)\triangleq\langle W_{j,r},s\rangle,\qquad s\in\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{Y}.
For each pair ( g , y ) (g,y) , let j = τ ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( y ) ) j=\tau(g(y)) be the correct next-state index. Within the neuron group { W j , r } r ∈ [ m ] \{W_{j,r}\}_{r\in[m]} , we designate a unique neuron r g ⋅ y ∈ [ m ] r_{g\cdot y}\in[m] associated with this pair 1 1
1
Our theory can also accommodate the setting where, for each feature, there is a non-overlapping group of neurons
r 1 , r 2 , … , r k ∈ [ m ] r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{k}\in[m] that jointly satisfies the same activation pattern (e.g.,
∑ i = 1 k σ ⁡ ( V j , r i ​ ( g ) + V j , r i ​ ( y ) ) = 2 ​ B \sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma\!\big(V_{j,r_{i}}(g)+V_{j,r_{i}}(y)\big)=2B ).
We do not adopt this setting to simplify the proof. .
We define B = C B ​ log ⁡ d B=C_{B}\log d with sufficiently large integer C B = O ⁡ ( 1 ) C_{B}=O(1) and σ 0 = d − 1 / 2 \sigma_{0}=d^{-1/2} , and assume the features satisfy:
V j , r g ⋅ y ​ ( g ) = B , V j , r g ⋅ y ​ ( y ) = B + 2 ​ σ 0 ; \displaystyle V_{j,r_{g\cdot y}}(g)=B,\qquad V_{j,r_{g\cdot y}}(y)=B+2\sigma_{0};
(5a)
V j , r g ⋅ y ​ ( s ) = − B , ∀ s ∈ ( 𝒢 ∪ 𝒴 ) ∖ { g , y } ; \displaystyle V_{j,r_{g\cdot y}}(s)=-B,\quad\forall s\in(\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{Y})\setminus\{g,y\};
(5b)

(5c)
Proposition 3.1 .
Under Assumption 3.1 - 3.2 , if the MLP weight W W satisfies ( 5a )–( 5c ), given any Q Q , for any y 0 ∈ 𝒴 y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y} and G 1 = ( g 1 ) G^{1}=(g_{1}) with g 1 ∈ 𝒢 g_{1}\in\mathcal{G} , we have
π θ ​ ( g 1 ​ ( y 0 ) | y 0 , G 1 ) = 1 − 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 ​ d . \displaystyle\pi_{\theta}\big(g_{1}(y_{0})\big|y_{0},G^{1}\big)=1-\frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}d}.
Note that for L = 1 L=1 , the model necessarily attends to the only prompt Z p , 1 Z_{p,1} . Combined with the residual connection, the MLP receives an aggregate input
1 2 ​ ( g 1 + y 0 ) \frac{1}{2}(g_{1}+y_{0}) . Proposition 3.1 thus guarantees that an MLP equipped with the above structural properties can perfectly implement the atomic group action.
Remark 3.3 .
At a high level, when the MLP input contains the correct pair ( g , y ) (g,y) , a unique activated neuron creates a large positive margin for the correct logit, while mismatched symbols
produce canceling (negative) contributions.
Consequently, the MLP predicts g ⁡ ( y ) g(y) with near-perfect accuracy. Prior analysis ( Huang et al., 2025b, ) shows that an MLP can learn such a
feature-separated structure under supervised training with suitable
initialization.
Therefore, we abstract this one-step capability as the following structural assumption on W W .
Assumption 3.3 (Pretrained MLP) .
The MLP weight W W is fixed and satisfies
( 5a )–( 5c ).
How does the transformer reason sequentially?
Solving the L L -step state-tracking task reduces to carrying out the single-step operation L L times: retrieve the required transition g ℓ g_{\ell} from the prompt and apply it to the current predicted state y ^ ℓ − 1 \widehat{y}_{\ell-1} . Under the above assumption, the pre-trained MLP contains all the atomic skills for one-step transitions, and the remaining challenge is association : the attention layer must find the correct g ℓ g_{\ell} for the current reasoning step, which shall be learned in RL or SFT.
3.4 Outcome-based RL Objective
We train the induced policy π θ \pi_{\theta} using an outcome-based RL objective with a terminal reward.
Given an instance Z L ∼ 𝒟 L Z^{L}\sim\mathcal{D}^{L} (equivalently, ( y 0 , G L ) (y_{0},G^{L}) ), we generate a state sequence
y ^ L = ( y ^ 1 , … , y ^ L ) \widehat{y}^{L}=(\widehat{y}_{1},\dots,\widehat{y}_{L}) with the policy π θ \pi_{\theta} via ( 4 ). We assign reward 1 1 if the final prediction y ^ L \hat{y}_{L} matches the true final state y L y_{L} , and 0 0 otherwise:
r ( y ^ L ∣ y 0 , G L ) ≜ 𝟙 { y ^ L = y L } , r(\widehat{y}^{L}\mid y_{0},G^{L})\triangleq\mathds{1}\big\{\widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L}\big\},
where y L = g L ( ⋯ g 1 ( y 0 ) ) y_{L}=g_{L}(\cdots g_{1}(y_{0})) . The RL objective is defined as the expected terminal reward:
𝒥 L ( θ ) = 𝔼 Z L [ 𝔼 y ^ L ∼ π θ L ( ⋅ ∣ y 0 , G L ) [ r ( y ^ L ∣ y 0 , G L ) ] ] . \mathcal{J}_{L}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{y}^{L}\sim\pi_{\theta}^{L}(\cdot\mid y_{0},G^{L})}\big[r(\widehat{y}^{L}\mid y_{0},G^{L})\big]\Big].
(6)
For comparison, we also consider an SFT-type objective ( Chu et al.,, 2025 ) . Unlike the outcome-based RL objective, which provides a terminal reward only after the full rollout, this supervised objective uses teacher forcing ( Huang et al., 2025b, ; Kim and Suzuki,, 2024 ; Wen et al.,, 2024 ; Yang et al.,, 2025 ) : at each step k k we condition on the ground-truth current state y k − 1 y_{k-1} and apply immediate supervision to the next state y k y_{k} .
Formally, the SFT objective is written as
𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L ( θ ) ≜ 𝔼 Z L [ 1 L ∑ k = 1 L − log π θ ( y k ∣ y k − 1 , G L ) ] . \mathsf{Loss}_{L}(\theta)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\Big[\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k=1}^{L}-\log\pi_{\theta}(y_{k}\mid y_{k-1},G^{L})\Big].
(7)
Learning algorithm.
We consider the REINFORCE algorithm of policy gradient ( Williams,, 1992 ) :
∇ 𝒥 L ( θ ) = 𝔼 Z L , y ^ L [ r ( y ^ L ∣ y 0 , G L ) ∇ log π θ L ( y ^ L ∣ y 0 , G L ) ] . \displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{J}_{L}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L},\widehat{y}^{L}}\Big[r(\widehat{y}^{L}\mid y_{0},G^{L})\nabla\log\pi_{\theta}^{L}(\widehat{y}^{L}\mid y_{0},G^{L})\Big].
Since we keep W W fixed, we study the gradient ascent on Q Q with length-normalized ( He et al.,, 2025 ; Gao et al.,, 2024 ) policy gradient:
Q ( t + 1 ) = Q ( t ) + η ​ ∇ Q 𝒥 ~ L ​ ( θ ( t ) ) , Q^{(t+1)}=Q^{(t)}+\eta\nabla_{Q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}\big(\theta^{(t)}\big),
(8)
where 𝒥 ~ L ​ ( θ ( t ) ) = 1 L ​ 𝒥 L ​ ( θ ( t ) ) \widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}\big(\theta^{(t)}\big)=\frac{1}{L}\mathcal{J}_{L}\big(\theta^{(t)}\big) and η > 0 \eta>0 is the step size. Similarly, we consider the gradient descent for optimizing the SFT loss:
Q ( t + 1 ) = Q ( t ) − η ​ ∇ Q 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L ​ ( θ ( t ) ) . Q^{(t+1)}=Q^{(t)}-\eta\nabla_{Q}\mathsf{Loss}_{L}\big(\theta^{(t)}\big).
(9)
Assumption 3.4 (Initialization) .
At t = 0 t=0 , Q ( 0 ) Q^{(0)} is initialized to be zero matrix 𝟎 d × d \mathbf{0}_{d\times d} .
For simplicity, in the following dicsussions, we let A ( t ) A^{(t)} represent the value of A A at iteration t t , dropping the explicit dependence on the θ ( t ) \theta^{(t)} or Q ( t ) Q^{(t)} where the context allows.
4 Learning Short-horizon Compositional Reasoning
In this section, we examine the RL dynamics of transformers on short-horizon compositional tasks. We prove that RL successfully learns compositional reasoning up to a critical horizon, beyond which a flat-gradient barrier emerges due to the nature of sparse, outcome-based rewards. In contrast, we demonstrate that SFT can overcome this limitation by leveraging immediate supervision.
4.1 RL for Short-horizon Compositions
Following the setup in Section 3.3 , the transformer
𝖳𝖥 θ ( 0 ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta^{(0)}} at initialization executes the atomic
one-step skill, yet its attention is approximately uniform at t = 0 t=0 ,
and thus the induced policy does not reliably implement multi-step
compositions. Our first result shows that, for any short horizon L ≤ C B = O ⁡ ( 1 ) L\leq C_{B}=O(1) (determined by the pretrained MLP parameters),
policy-gradient RL learns the L L -step composition and yields the intended
attention concentration pattern.
Theorem 4.1 (RL for short-horizon problems) .
Suppose Assumption 3.1 - 3.3 hold and assume that 𝖳𝖥 θ ( 0 ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta^{(0)}} is initialized according to Assumption 3.4 . Then for any L ∈ [ 2 , C B ] L\in[2,C_{B}] , η = 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⁡ ( d ) \eta=\frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}(d)} and ϵ ∈ ( 1 log Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ⁡ ( d ) , 1 4 ) \epsilon\in\big(\frac{1}{\log^{\Omega(1)}(d)},\frac{1}{4}\big) , the transformer 𝖳𝖥 θ ( t ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta^{(t)}} trained via ( 8 ) on the objective 𝒥 L \mathcal{J}_{L} after T L , ϵ = O ⁡ ( L max ​ log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) η ​ log ⁡ d ⋅ d ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B − 1 ) T_{L,\epsilon}=O\big(\frac{L_{\max}\log({L}/{\epsilon})}{\eta\log d}\cdot d^{(1-\epsilon)C_{B}-1}\big) iterations attains:
1.
Reward optimality: At t = T L , ϵ t=T_{L,\epsilon} , the reward is optimal:
𝒥 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − O ​ ( 1 d C B ​ ( 1 − ϵ ) − 1 ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{L}^{(t)}\geq 1-O\quantity(\frac{1}{d^{C_B(1-\epsilon) - 1}}).
2.
Optimal short-horizon attention: At t = T L , ϵ t=T_{L,\epsilon} , for any ℓ ≤ L \ell\leq L , we have
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ . \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell}^{(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon.
Significance of the result.
Theorem 4.1 provides the first provable guarantee that a
transformer can learn multi-step compositional reasoning via
outcome-only policy gradients, when the initialization reliably implements only the atomic one-step skill.
The guarantee exhibits an explicit short-horizon regime ( L ≤ C B L\leq C_{B} ) in
which learning is efficient. Beyond reward optimality, the theorem also identifies the emergent
attention concentration pattern, giving a mechanistic characterization of how the
learned transformer implements the L L -step composition.
4.2 Critical Horizon and Exponentially Flat Region
When L L exceeds the critical threshold C B C_{B} , the near-uniform attention at initialization yields little useful signal to the MLP. As a consequence, the model behaves almost randomly on long compositional instances. With only
outcome-based rewards, the resulting policy-gradient signal becomes exponentially small, even when the model receives a nonzero reward, as shown in the result below.
Proposition 4.1 (Exponentially flat region) .
Suppose Assumption 3.1 - 3.3 hold and assume that the 𝖳𝖥 θ ( 0 ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta^{(0)}} is initialized according to Assumption 3.4 . Then for any horizon L > 2 ​ C B L>2C_{B} , whenever the feature magnitude
max x , x ′ ∈ 𝒳 ⁡ ⟨ Q ​ x , x ′ ⟩ ≤ 0.01 \max_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\langle Qx,x^{\prime}\rangle\leq 0.01 , we have 𝒥 L ( t ) = 1 d ​ ( 1 ± o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) \mathcal{J}_{L}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{d}(1\pm o(1)) , and
max x , x ′ ∈ 𝒳 ⁡ | ⟨ [ ∇ Q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) ] ​ x , x ′ ⟩ | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 L max ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L ) . \displaystyle\max_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\bigg|\Big\langle\big[\nabla_{Q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}\big]x,x^{\prime}\Big\rangle\bigg|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{L_{\max}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L)}.
Why is the landscape flat for RL initially?
Conceptually, the initial training horizon controls the concentration of signals the model could learn from each sample, which dilutes due to the O ⁡ ( d L ) O(d^{L}) possible trajectories if the model uniformly traverses the actions specified by the problem instance. In this case, outcome-based reward, due to the lack of process feedback , makes it extremely difficult to pick up sufficient signals from policy gradients.
4.3 SFT Succeeds Beyond the Critical Horizon
As a complementary comparison, we consider an SFT objective as in ( 7 ). Since SFT provides intermediate supervision rather than only an outcome-based reward, it remains effective even for long-horizon problems.
Theorem 4.2 (SFT provably escapes initial flat region) .
Suppose Assumption 3.1 - 3.3 hold and assume that 𝖳𝖥 θ ( 0 ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta^{(0)}} is initialized according to Assumption 3.4 . Then for any length 2 ≤ L ≤ 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀 ​ d 2\leq L\leq\mathsf{polylog}d , η = 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⁡ ( d ) \eta=\frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}(d)} and ϵ ∈ ( 1 log Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ⁡ ( d ) , 1 4 ) \epsilon\in(\frac{1}{\log^{\Omega(1)}(d)},\frac{1}{4}) , the transformer 𝖳𝖥 θ ( t ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta^{(t)}} trained via ( 9 ) on the objective 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L \mathsf{Loss}_{L} for
T L , ϵ = O ⁡ ( L max ​ log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) ​ d ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B − 1 η ​ ϵ ​ log ⁡ d + L max ​ L η ​ log ⁡ d ) \displaystyle T_{L,\epsilon}=O\Big(\frac{L_{\max}\log({L}/{\epsilon})d^{(1-\epsilon)C_{B}-1}}{\eta\epsilon\log d}+\frac{L_{\max}L}{\eta\log d}\Big)
iterations, satisfies:
1.
Loss convergence: At t = T L , ϵ t=T_{L,\epsilon} , the loss converges:
𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L ( t ) ≤ O ​ ( 1 d C B ​ ( 1 − ϵ ) − 1 ) . \displaystyle\mathsf{Loss}_{L}^{(t)}\leq O\quantity(\frac{1}{d^{C_B(1-\epsilon) - 1}}).
2.
Optimal attention: For any ℓ ≤ L \ell\leq L , we shall have
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ . \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell}^{(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon.
Thus Theorem 4.2 has shown that SFT can successfully train the transformer to solve the composition reasoning beyond the critical horizon.
5 Learning Dynamics of Mixed-difficulty RL
In practice, RL datasets typically contain instances of mixed complexity, either due to data collection or explicit curriculum strategies ( Zeng et al.,, 2025 ; Parashar et al.,, 2025 ; Chen et al., 2025c, ) ,
which can fundamentally alter optimization dynamics.
Motivated by this potential, we study policy-gradient training under mixed-difficulty distributions and show that large difficulty gaps
lead to grokking-like phase transitions, whereas smoother mixtures yield
a relay effect that carries progress from easier to harder horizons.
5.1 Easy-to-Hard Mixture
To model mixed difficulty, we consider a mixture over multiple reasoning horizons.
Let us choose difficulty ratio R > 1 R>1 and fix a starting horizon L 1 ≥ 2 L_{1}\geq 2 .
Define a set of horizons ℒ R = { L 1 , L 2 , … , L K } \mathcal{L}_{R}=\{L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{K}\} by the recursion:
L k = min ⁡ { ⌈ R ​ L k − 1 ⌉ , L max } , 2 ≤ k ≤ K , L_{k}=\min\{\lceil RL_{k-1}\rceil,L_{\max}\},\qquad 2\leq k\leq K,
where K = ⌈ log R ⁡ ( L max / L 1 ) ⌉ K=\lceil\log_{R}(L_{\max}/L_{1})\rceil so that L K = L max L_{K}=L_{\max} .
We then define the mixed-difficulty objective as the uniform mixture
𝒥 mix , R ​ ( θ ) = 𝔼 L ∼ Unif ⁡ ( ℒ R ) ​ [ 𝒥 L ​ ( θ ) ] . \displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{mix},R}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{L\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{L}_{R})}\big[\mathcal{J}_{L}(\theta)\big].
(10)
To optimize 𝒥 mix , R \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{mix},R} , we consider a length-normalized update:
Q ( t + 1 ) = Q ( t ) + η ​ ∇ Q 𝒥 ~ mix , R ​ ( θ ( t ) ) , \displaystyle Q^{(t+1)}=Q^{(t)}+\eta\nabla_{Q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}(\theta^{(t)}),
(11)
where ∇ Q 𝒥 ~ mix , R ​ ( θ ) = 𝔼 L ∼ Unif ⁡ ( ℒ R ) ​ [ ∇ Q 𝒥 ~ L ​ ( θ ) ] \nabla_{Q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{L\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{L}_{R})}[\nabla_{Q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}(\theta)] , and recall 𝒥 ~ L ​ ( θ ) = 1 L ​ 𝒥 L ​ ( θ ) \widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}\big(\theta\big)=\frac{1}{L}\mathcal{J}_{L}\big(\theta\big) .
5.2 Grokking Dynamics Under Large Difficulty Ratios
Intuitively, under mixed-difficulty training, the shorter-horizon tasks are simpler and thus are expected to be solved first, and the learning progress then gradually extends to longer horizons. When the difficulty spectrum is coarse, i.e., when the mixture ratio R R is large, this inter-horizon progression need not be smooth: the policy can spend an extended period with near-zero reward on the next horizon
before making visible gains.
This plateau-and-jump pattern resembles grokking , an empirical phase-transition phenomenon ( Power et al.,, 2022 ; Sun et al.,, 2025 ) : after an extended period of receiving near-zero reward, the policy abruptly climbs to near-perfect accuracy.
Our next theorem formalizes this behavior in the mixed-horizon setting by quantitatively characterizing
the length of the plateau and the subsequent transition.
To state the result, we introduce two observable states for each horizon that capture
(i) when progress first becomes visible and (ii) when the horizon is essentially solved.
Stopping times for mastery and visible states.
For any horizon L k ∈ ℒ R L_{k}\in\mathcal{L}_{R} , we say that the task at horizon L k L_{k} has visible return at iteration t t if 𝒥 L k ( t ) ≥ 0.01 \mathcal{J}_{L_{k}}^{(t)}\geq 0.01 :
T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k ≜ min ⁡ { t : 𝒥 L k ( t ) ≥ 0.01 } . T_{\mathsf{vis},k}\triangleq\min\{t:\mathcal{J}_{L_{k}}^{(t)}\geq 0.01\}.
(12)
We say the horizon L k L_{k} is mastered at iteration t t if 𝒥 L k ( t ) ≥ 0.99 \mathcal{J}_{L_{k}}^{(t)}\geq 0.99 :
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k ≜ min ⁡ { t : 𝒥 L k ( t ) ≥ 0.99 } . T_{\mathsf{mas},k}\triangleq\min\{t:\mathcal{J}_{L_{k}}^{(t)}\geq 0.99\}.
(13)
Theorem 5.1 (Grokking dynamics) .
Let 𝒥 mix , R \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{mix},R} be the mixed-difficulty objective with ratio ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ≤ R ≤ L max 2 ​ C B \omega(1)\leq R\leq\frac{L_{\max}}{2C_{B}} and starting horizon L 1 = C B L_{1}=C_{B} . Under Assumptions 3.1 – 3.4 , consider the RL training process under the length-normalized update ( 11 ) with step size
η = 1 / 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⁡ ( d ) \eta=1/\mathsf{poly}(d) . Then for each 1 ≤ k ≤ K − 2 1\leq k\leq K-2 , the following hold:
1.
Long inter-difficulty plateaus.
Before the next horizon L k + 1 L_{k+1} makes noticeable progress (i.e., before it enters the visible-return state), the inter-horizon plateau length satisfies
T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k = Θ ~ ​ ( L max η ) ⋅ d C B − 1 . \displaystyle T_{\mathsf{vis},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},k}=\widetilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{L_{\max}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot d^{C_{B}-1}.
(14)
2.
Grokking-like phase transitions. Once L k + 1 L_{k+1} enters the visible reward state, it reaches mastery quickly: T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k + 1 ≤ O ~ ​ ( L max η ) ⋅ L k + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{vis},k+1}\leq\widetilde{O}\big(\frac{L_{\max}}{\eta}\big)\cdot L_{k+1} .
Theorem 5.1 shows that each transition L k → L k + 1 L_{k}\!\to\!L_{k+1} consists of a long near-zero-return plateau followed by a rapid rise to mastery once return becomes visible. Aggregating these transitions yields a time-to-mastery bound for the longest horizon, in which the total runtime is dominated by the plateaus.
Corollary 5.1 .
Under the assumptions of Theorem 5.1 , suppose c x < C B − 2 C B + 2 c_{x}<\frac{C_{B}-2}{C_{B}+2} , the first time the longest horizon
L max L_{\max} reaches mastery satisfies
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K = Θ ~ ​ ( L max η ) ⋅ d C B − 1 ≜ 𝒯 𝗉𝗅𝖺𝗍 . \displaystyle T_{\mathsf{mas},K}=\widetilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{L_{\max}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot d^{C_{B}-1}\triangleq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{plat}}.
(15)
Why does grokking happen in RL?
For long-horizon tasks, reward can either come from fully correct traces or from rare lucky guesses that reach the correct final answer despite intermediate mistakes. Before the policy can reliably generate correct traces at long horizons, the reward stays near-zero, and the gradient signal mainly consists of those from the lucky guesses, which are random and uninformative. Meanwhile, gradient updates from shorter horizons keep sharpening the internal features long after their rewards have saturated. This hidden progress would eventually lead to a non-trivial success rate at long horizons, resulting in a rapid improvement of rewards within a few iterations. Our analysis in Lemma 6.2 makes this mechanism explicit by tracking how the hidden feature sharpening translates into the long-horizon policy improvement.
5.3 Relay Dynamics under Moderate Difficulty Ratios
The grokking dynamics above highlight that when the mixture is highly discontinuous, training can stall at each new horizon: even after L k L_{k} is mastered,
the next horizon L k + 1 L_{k+1} may remain in the near-zero-reward regime for a long plateau
before its reward becomes visible. We now show that, when the difficulty spectrum is sufficiently smooth ( R R is a moderate constant), training exhibits a relay effect: progress on easier horizons continuously supports the next harder horizon, preventing prolonged plateaus.
Our next theorem formalizes the relay regime by providing an upper bound on T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k T_{\mathsf{vis},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},k} , which will be significantly smaller than the long plateaus in the large difficulty regime.
Theorem 5.2 (Relay dynamics) .
Let 𝒥 mix , R \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{mix},R} be the mixed-difficulty objective with ratio 2 ≤ R ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 ) 2\leq R\leq O(1)
and starting horizon L 1 = C B L_{1}=C_{B} . Under Assumptions 3.1 – 3.4 ,
consider the RL training process under the length-normalized update ( 11 )
with step size η = 1 / 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 ⁡ ( d ) \eta=1/\mathsf{poly}(d) . Then for each k ≤ K − 2 k\leq K-2 ,
before L k + 1 L_{k+1} enters the visible-return state, the inter-horizon plateau length satisfies
T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k ≤ O ~ ​ ( L max η ) ⋅ d ( 1 − C B C B + R ) ​ C B − 1 . T_{\mathsf{vis},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},k}\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{L_{\max}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot d^{(1-\frac{C_{B}}{C_{B}+R})C_{B}-1}.
Moreover, once L k + 1 L_{k+1} enters the visible-return state, it reaches mastery rapidly: T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k + 1 ≤ O ~ ​ ( L max η ) ⋅ L k + 1 . T_{\mathsf{mas},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{vis},k+1}\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{L_{\max}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot L_{k+1}.
Compared with the grokking regime, Theorem 5.2 shortens each inter-horizon plateau by a factor d Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) d^{\Theta(1)} . Although a smoother spectrum induces more horizons, we have K = O ⁡ ( log R ⁡ ( L max / C B ) ) ≤ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) K=O(\log_{R}(L_{\max}/C_{B}))\leq O(\log d) . Therefore, the total time to reach mastery at the longest horizon is still governed by the (much shorter) relay plateaus, leading to a strictly faster overall convergence bound than in the large-ratio regime.
Corollary 5.2 .
Under the assumptions of Theorem 5.2 , suppose c x < C B − 2 C B + 2 c_{x}<\frac{C_{B}-2}{C_{B}+2} , the first time the longest horizon
L max L_{\max} reaches mastery satisfies T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K ≤ 𝒯 𝗋𝖾𝗅𝖺𝗒 T_{\mathsf{mas},K}\leq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{relay}} , where
𝒯 𝗋𝖾𝗅𝖺𝗒 ≤ O ~ ​ ( L max η ) ⋅ d ( 1 − C B C B + R ) ​ C B − 1 ≤ O ~ ​ ( d − C B 2 C B + R ) ​ 𝒯 𝗉𝗅𝖺𝗍 . {\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{relay}}\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{L_{\max}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot d^{(1-\frac{C_{B}}{C_{B}+R})C_{B}-1}\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(d^{-\frac{C_{B}^{2}}{C_{B}+R}}\Big)\,{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{plat}}.
Relay learning near the edge of competence.
Theorem 5.1 and Theorem 5.2 show that the mixed-horizon dynamics are governed by
how quickly training can move from mastering L k L_{k} to making visible progress on L k + 1 L_{k+1} .
When R R is large, this handoff is delayed: after L k L_{k} is mastered, the policy can remain stuck for a long
period with near-zero return on L k + 1 L_{k+1} , until a sharp, grokking-like jump makes the next horizon suddenly solvable.
When R = O ⁡ ( 1 ) R=O(1) , the handoff becomes smooth: progress on L k L_{k} starts benefiting L k + 1 L_{k+1} before L k L_{k} fully saturates, so the next horizon gains a visible return much sooner and training relays steadily across horizons. This behavior can be interpreted as learning at the edge of competence ( Zhang et al.,, 2025 ) . In the moderate- R R regime, the policy becomes “just competent enough” on L k L_{k} that success on L k + 1 L_{k+1} is no longer purely random, yet L k L_{k} still provides a strong learning signal; the two horizons therefore improve in tandem. In the large- R R regime, the policy must become overwhelmingly competent on L k L_{k} before L k + 1 L_{k+1} ceases to be random, creating the long plateau and the subsequent phase transition. See Section 6.2 for a more detailed, gradient-level mechanism explanation.
6 Proof Overview
This section explains the main proof idea behind our learning-dynamic results.
The central technical ingredient is a characterization of the
policy-gradient signal as a function of a step-wise probability , which
reveals an explicit long-horizon thresholding mechanism.
6.1 Technical Preliminaries
We first map our reasoning mechanism to some step-invariant quantities. At step ℓ \ell , attention weights over prompt tokens are given by a softmax of
scores ⟨ Q ​ x a , ℓ − 1 , x p , ℓ ′ ⟩ \langle Qx_{a,\ell-1},x_{p,\ell^{\prime}}\rangle . Since
x a , ℓ − 1 = 𝔰 ⁡ ( x p , ℓ ) x_{a,\ell-1}=\mathfrak{s}(x_{p,\ell}) , correct retrieval means that the aligned
prompt token x p , ℓ x_{p,\ell} receives a strictly larger score than all misaligned tokens x p , ℓ ′ x_{p,\ell^{\prime}} with
ℓ ′ ≠ ℓ \ell^{\prime}\neq\ell .
With initialization Q ( 0 ) = 𝟎 Q^{(0)}=\mathbf{0} and the symmetry of 𝒟 L \mathcal{D}^{L} , the expected policy-gradient update preserves a two-level score
structure: for all x ∈ 𝒳 x\in\mathcal{X} and x ′ ≠ x x^{\prime}\neq x ,
⟨ Q ( t ) ​ 𝔰 ​ ( x ) , x ⟩ = q ( t ) , ⟨ Q ( t ) ​ 𝔰 ​ ( x ) , x ′ ⟩ = r ( t ) . \displaystyle\big\langle Q^{(t)}\mathfrak{s}(x),x\big\rangle=q^{(t)},\quad\big\langle Q^{(t)}\mathfrak{s}(x),x^{\prime}\big\rangle=r^{(t)}.
Consequently, the attention weight is step-invariant: 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ( t ) ≡ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell}\equiv\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)} . The following lemma translates 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)} into the corresponding three classes of step-invariant next-state probabilities.
Lemma 6.1 (Step-invariant probability) .
Given 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)} , with a fixed MLP in Assumption 3.3 , the next-state distribution assigns step-invariant probability masses to:
•
Target: p L , 1 ( t ) ≜ π θ ( t ) ​ ( g ℓ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) p_{L,1}^{(t)}\triangleq\pi_{\theta^{(t)}}\big(g_{\ell}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}\big) ;
•
Context
distractor: p L , 2 ( t ) ≜ π θ ( t ) ​ ( g ℓ ′ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) | y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) p_{L,2}^{(t)}\triangleq\pi_{\theta^{(t)}}\big(g_{\ell^{\prime}}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})\big|\ \widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}\big) for any ℓ ′ ≠ ℓ \ell^{\prime}\neq\ell ;
•
Other distractor: p L , 3 ( t ) ≜ π θ ( t ) ​ ( y | y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) p_{L,3}^{(t)}\triangleq\pi_{\theta^{(t)}}\big(y\ \big|\ \widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}\big) for any y ∉ { g ℓ ′ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) ∣ ℓ ′ ∈ [ L ] } y\notin\{g_{\ell^{\prime}}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})\mid\ell^{\prime}\in[L]\} .
Moreover, p L , 1 ( t ) ∝ d C B ​ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) p_{L,1}^{(t)}\propto d^{C_{B}\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}} . For later use, define the effective probability margin:
Δ L ( t ) := p L , 1 ( t ) − p L , 3 ( t ) \Delta^{(t)}_{L}:=p^{(t)}_{L,1}-p^{(t)}_{L,3} and
δ L ( t ) := p L , 2 ( t ) − p L , 3 ( t ) \delta^{(t)}_{L}:=p^{(t)}_{L,2}-p^{(t)}_{L,3} .
6.2 Key Lemma for Gradient Estimation
The preceding discussion reduces the model’s one-step behavior to two scalars ( q ( t ) , r ( t ) ) (q^{(t)},r^{(t)}) . We now state the main technical result: for length- L L tasks, the policy-gradient
signal in the ( q ( t ) , r ( t ) ) (q^{(t)},r^{(t)}) coordinates admits an explicit characterization
in terms of the step-level margin Δ L ( t ) \Delta_{L}^{(t)} .
This turns the learning dynamics into an effective one-dimensional evolution of
attention concentration.
Lemma 6.2 (Gradient characterization) .
Throughout the mixed-training process, given L ∈ ℒ R L\in\mathcal{L}_{R} , if the step-wise probability satisfies δ L ( t ) Δ L ( t ) ≪ 1 L 2 ​ ( 1 − Δ L ( t ) ) \frac{\delta^{(t)}_{L}}{\Delta^{(t)}_{L}}\ll\frac{1}{L^{2}}(1-\Delta^{(t)}_{L}) and p L , 2 ( t ) p L , 1 ( t ) ≪ 1 − p L , 1 ( t ) \frac{p^{(t)}_{L,2}}{p^{(t)}_{L,1}}\ll 1-p_{L,1}^{(t)} , then we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}^{(t)}_{L}
∝ ( Δ L ( t ) ) L ​ ( 1 − Δ L ( t ) ) , \displaystyle\propto(\Delta^{(t)}_{L})^{L}(1-\Delta^{(t)}_{L}),
(16)
𝒥 L ( t ) \displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{(t)}_{L}
= 1 d + ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 ± o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ⋅ ( Δ L ( t ) ) L . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}+\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{d}\Bigr)(1\pm o(1))\cdot(\Delta^{(t)}_{L})^{L}.
(17)
Moreover, | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 L max ) ​ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) |\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}^{(t)}_{L}|\leq O(\frac{1}{L_{\max}})\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}^{(t)}_{L} .
Remark 6.1 .
Lemma 6.2 shows that the policy gradient is essentially
driven by the q q -direction, with | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L | |\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}| being lower-order.
Its magnitude exhibits two regimes: when Δ L \Delta_{L} is small, the
long-horizon factor ( Δ L ) L (\Delta_{L})^{L} exponentially suppresses the
gradient; when Δ L ≈ 1 \Delta_{L}\approx 1 , the update is in a convergence regime
and is controlled by the shrinking term 1 − Δ L 1-\Delta_{L} .
Since larger q q increases the target attention weight 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L \mathbf{Attn}_{L} and
hence the margin Δ L \Delta_{L} via Lemma 6.1 , the
takeaway is that length- L L learning is negligible until q q is large enough to make Δ L \Delta_{L} moderate, after which progress slows again as Δ L → 1 \Delta_{L}\to 1 .
Two alternating phases in mixed-horizon training.
Under mixed-horizon training, learning typically proceeds from shorter to longer horizons: shorter tasks suffer weaker attention dilution, so they contribute usable gradient earlier and drive up q q ; as q q grows, longer horizons gradually become gradient-effective and then start to show visible return. Consequently, learning alternates between
(i) a gradient transfer phase, where ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k}} dominates while
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k + 1 \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k+1}} remains negligible, and (ii) a reward-emergence
phase, where ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k + 1 \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k+1}} becomes substantial and L k + 1 L_{k+1} quickly reaches mastery.
Regime comparison: grokking vs. edge-of-competence relay.
The key difference is whether ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k + 1 \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k+1}} becomes non-negligible
before ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k}} has decayed to a saturated signal. Guided by Lemmas 6.2 and 6.1 , activating L k + 1 L_{k+1} in the first place requires its target attention 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L k + 1 \mathbf{Attn}_{L_{k+1}} to reach a constant-level regime so that the gate
( Δ L k + 1 ) L k + 1 (\Delta_{L_{k+1}})^{L_{k+1}} is not exponentially suppressed; the handoff is then controlled by when
( Δ L k + 1 ) L k + 1 ≈ 1 − Δ L k . (\Delta_{L_{k+1}})^{L_{k+1}}\approx 1-\Delta_{L_{k}}.
For large R = ω ⁡ ( 1 ) R=\omega(1) , reaching this regime for L k + 1 L_{k+1} forces q q so large that L k L_{k} is already driven to
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L k = 1 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathbf{Attn}_{L_{k}}=1-o(1) , making 1 − Δ L k 1-\Delta_{L_{k}} small (on the order of d − ( C B − 1 ) d^{-(C_{B}-1)} ). Thus
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k + 1 \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k+1}} stays negligible over a long plateau, and reward emerges via a
grokking-style jump. For moderate R = O ⁡ ( 1 ) R=O(1) , the same catch-up happens while L k L_{k} is still away from full saturation (edge of competence), so ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k}} and ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L k + 1 \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{k+1}} overlap and jointly drive progress, yielding a smooth relay.
6.3 Proof of Lemma 6.2 : Fourier Analysis on Groups
We begin by discussing the central technical challenge in analyzing long-horizon policy gradients, and then introduce our Fourier-based techniques for tackling the challenges.
Key technical challenges.
The starting point is to express the policy gradient in terms of the one-step action distribution on the group.
By simple transitivity, each transition y ^ ℓ − 1 → y ^ ℓ \widehat{y}_{\ell-1}\to\widehat{y}_{\ell} corresponds to a unique group element
u ℓ ∈ 𝒢 u_{\ell}\in\mathcal{G} such that y ^ ℓ = u ℓ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) \widehat{y}_{\ell}=u_{\ell}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}) .
Let μ ℓ \mu_{\ell} denote the one-step action law of u ℓ u_{\ell} on 𝒢 \mathcal{G} (under the current policy), and write μ ℓ ​ ( g ) = ℙ ⁡ ( u ℓ = g ) \mu_{\ell}(g)\;=\;\mathbb{P}(u_{\ell}=g) . With this notation, the step- ℓ \ell gradient reduces to a posterior-vs-prior gap for the target action:
∇ q 𝒥 L ∝ ∑ ℓ ∈ [ L ] ( ℙ ⁡ ( u ℓ = g ℓ ∣ y ^ L = y L ) − μ ℓ ​ ( g ℓ ) ) . \textstyle\nabla_{q}\mathcal{J}_{L}\propto\sum_{\ell\in[L]}\Big(\mathbb{P}(u_{\ell}=g_{\ell}\mid\widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L})-\mu_{\ell}(g_{\ell})\Big).
Let G ∗ = g L ∘ ⋯ ∘ g 1 G_{\ast}=g_{L}\circ\cdots\circ g_{1} .
Simple transitivity also implies that terminal success is exactly the group-product constraint
y ^ L = y L ⟺ u L ∘ ⋯ ∘ u 1 = G ∗ . \textstyle\widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad u_{L}\circ\cdots\circ u_{1}=G_{\ast}.
Hence the success probability and the numerator in the posterior can be written as point-evaluations of convolution products, for instance: 2 2
2
see
Section B.2 for formal definitions of convolution ∗ \ast and Fourier transforms.
ℙ ( y ^ L = y L ) = ( μ L ∗ ⋯ ∗ μ 1 ) ( G ∗ ) . \textstyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L})\;=\;(\mu_{L}\ast\cdots\ast\mu_{1})(G_{\ast}).
The challenge is that these are high-order convolutions evaluated at a specific group element.
When L L is large, a direct expansion in the group domain involves exponentially many mixed terms and offers no clean control:
conditioning on u L ∘ ⋯ ∘ u 1 = G ∗ u_{L}\circ\cdots\circ u_{1}=G_{\ast} couples all steps, so the posterior
ℙ ( u ℓ = ⋅ ∣ y ^ L = y L ) \mathbb{P}(u_{\ell}=\cdot\mid\widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L}) is inherently trajectory-level and does not factorize into per-step statistics.
Fourier analysis to estimate the dominant signal.
To make these convolution powers tractable, we pass to the Fourier domain on 𝒢 \mathcal{G} ( Kondor,, 2008 ; Terras,, 1999 ) ,
where convolution becomes multiplication, turning the L L -fold convolution into a structured product of Fourier operators:
∏ ℓ ∈ [ L ] μ ^ ℓ ​ ( λ ) \prod_{\ell\in[L]}\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\lambda) .
Here μ ^ ℓ ​ ( λ ) \widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\lambda) is the Fourier transform of μ ℓ \mu_{\ell} at an irreducible unitary representation λ \lambda † † footnotemark:
.
Notice that the step-invariant three-way partition of next-token outcomes in Lemma 6.1 is equivalently a three-way partition of u ℓ u_{\ell} , and thus
μ ℓ ( g ) = p L , 1 𝟙 { g = g ℓ } + p L , 2 𝟙 { g ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } } + p L , 3 𝟙 { g ∈ 𝒢 ∖ G L } . \mu_{\ell}(g)=p_{L,1}\mathds{1}\{g=g_{\ell}\}+p_{L,2}\mathds{1}\{g\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}\}+p_{L,3}\mathds{1}\{g\in\mathcal{G}\setminus G^{L}\}.
Exploiting this structure, we obtain
μ ^ ℓ ​ ( λ ) = Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g ℓ ) + δ L ​ ∑ g ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } λ ⁡ ( g ) , \widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\lambda)=\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{\ell})+\delta_{L}\sum_{g\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}}\lambda(g),
which splits into a target-aligned contribution and a residual contribution from context distractors. Taking products across L L steps, the leading contribution arises from selecting the aligned component at each step (under mild separation conditions on Δ L \Delta_{L} and δ L \delta_{L} ), which yields the characteristic ( Δ L ) L ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) (\Delta_{L})^{L}(1-\Delta_{L}) structure in the resulting posterior deviation.
Prior use of group representations in machine learning.
Group representation theory has been widely explored in machine learning to model and exploit symmetry ( Esteves,, 2020 ; Marchetti et al.,, 2024 ) and to analyze structured distributions ( Chen et al.,, 2020 ; Kondor,, 2008 ) .
Our use is different in both setting and goal: we bring Fourier analysis into the study of
long-horizon, compositional RL objectives, where the terminal success event couples all steps and makes policy-gradient estimation inherently trajectory-dependent. The group structure turns this global conditioning into an L L -fold convolution object that can be controlled spectrally.
7 Experiments
In this section, we provide empirical support for our theoretical analysis through synthetic experiments.
7.1 General Setup
The experiment setting is designed to mirror our theoretical framework and assumptions in Section 3 . We consider a cyclic group action over ℤ 96 \mathbb{Z}_{96} . We study two training paradigms:
•
fixed-length training with reasoning lengths L ∈ { 5 , 15 , 45 } L\in\{5,15,45\} ;
•
mixed-length training: ℒ 3 = { 5 , 15 , 45 } \mathcal{L}_{3}=\{5,15,45\} and ℒ 7 = { 5 , 35 } \mathcal{L}_{7}=\{5,35\} . The reasoning depth L L is stochastically assigned within each training batch ( N = 512 N=512 ), where the problem length of each individual sample is uniformly drawn from ℒ R \mathcal{L}_{R} .
We use an abelian cyclic action for experimental convenience; for the lengths we consider ( L ≥ 5 L\geq 5 ), the commutativity-induced shortcut effects discussed in Remark 3.1 are mild.
Model and training settings.
We employ a one-layer detached attention layer paired with a fixed MLP transition head. First, the transition MLP is pretrained via supervised learning to master the one-step group operation ( y , g ) ↦ y ⋅ g (y,g)\mapsto y\cdot g , after which its parameters are frozen. The attention parameters Q Q are subsequently trained using the REINFORCE algorithm to maximize the terminal reward. We utilize an exponential moving-average baseline (momentum 0.95) and an entropy penalty ( 10 − 3 10^{-3} ) to facilitate stable policy gradients.
Evaluation.
We periodically compute the per-length average success rate by running greedy rollouts and calculating the fraction of episodes where the model correctly predicts the entire trajectory { y ^ L = y L } \{\widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L}\} over 30 batches of size 512. We additionally report an attention-alignment metric, peak attention-hit rate, defined as the fraction of steps where the argmax attention weight selects the unique prompt position corresponding to the current action token, i.e., the prompt index 𝔰 − 1 ​ ( x a , k ) \mathfrak{s}^{-1}(x_{a,k}) within the sampled set { x p , ℓ } ℓ = 1 L \{x_{p,\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L} . A higher hit rate indicates that the attention layer recovers the underlying permutation 𝔰 \mathfrak{s} by locating the correct prompt position at each step, consistent with attention concentration ( 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L → 1 \mathbf{Attn}_{L}\to 1 ).
(a) RL rapidly learns short-horizon compositions, whereas longer horizons exhibit a near-flat reward plateau.
(b) Attention concentrates for short horizons, but saturates at a lower level for longer horizons.
Figure 2 : Average reward and peak attention hit rate during fixed-length RL training.
(a) R = 3 R=3 : a smoother difficulty spectrum facilitates relay dynamics across horizons.
(b) R = 7 R=7 : a larger difficulty ratio yields a prolonged plateau at longer horizons.
Figure 3 : Average reward during mixed-length RL training under different difficulty ratios.
Results for fixed-length training.
The average reward and peak attention hit rate under fixed-length training are reported in Figure 2 . We overlay three curves corresponding to training runs at different lengths in the same plot. We observe that the short-horizon setting ( L 1 = 5 L_{1}=5 ) achieves nearly optimal reward together with strong attention concentration, whereas longer horizons exhibit near-flat plateaus in both reward and attention. This behavior is consistent with the predictions of Theorem 4.1 and Proposition 4.1 .
Results for mixed-length training.
For mixed-length training, we consider two difficulty-ratio regimes: moderate ( R = 3 R=3 ) and large ( R = 7 R=7 ). As shown in Figure 3 , successful learning curves exhibit a grokking-style plateau-then-jump behavior. Moreover, Figure 3(a) indicates that a suitable difficulty ratio shortens the plateau between consecutive horizons, leading to efficient relay dynamics. In contrast, Figure 3(b) shows that when the difficulty ratio is too large, the longer horizon ( L = 35 L=35 ) remains at near-zero reward throughout training, whereas under a suitable R R (as in Figure 3(a) ), L = 45 L=45 can already reach near-optimal reward. Together, these observations validate the predictions from Theorem 5.1 and Theorem 5.2 in the mixed-difficulty regime.
8 Conclusions
In this work, we analyze the training dynamics of RLVR on a multi-step compositional reasoning task. To the best of our knowledge, we provide the first end-to-end learning process analysis for outcome-based RL with transformer-based policies, together with explicit convergence guarantees. Our theory captures both grokking-like phase transitions and the relay effect under mixed difficulty, explaining how RLVR learns at the edge of competence and identifying data mixing as a scalable lever for accelerating progress to harder tasks. Technically, we introduce a novel Fourier analysis on groups that makes long-horizon conditioning and compositional structure tractable. We also provide evidence from synthetic experiments that corroborates our theoretical results.
Limitations and future directions.
One major limitation of our study is that we focus on a single dimension of distribution diversity: we control for all other factors and only scale the length of compositions in the data distribution. This simplification allows us to study the optimization dynamics of RL in a clean, abstracted setting. However, this design choice also limits the generality of our conclusions. In real world reasoning problems, hard reasoning problems may be different from easy problems in many aspects. For example, they may require more long-tailed distributed atomic skills than easy problems, and they may sit in very different semantic contexts than easy problems, both of which we cannot study theoretically yet. There are also other reasoning patterns, such as planning and search, which we cannot analyze in the current setting. We hope our work can inspire future research into these topics.

Appendix: Complete Proofs
Appendix A Preliminaries
In this section, we will introduce some useful notations and derive several preliminary policy gradient lemmas, which will be repeatedly used in the subsequent training-dynamics analysis. Throughout the proof, we use d 𝗉 d_{\mathsf{p}} to denote | 𝒳 | |\mathcal{X}| .
A.1 Gradient Computations
Notations for gradient expressions.
Consider a problem instance of length L L , we denote a full answer trajectory with initial prefix Z a , 0 Z_{a,0} by
Z ^ a L ≜ ( Z a , 0 , Z ^ a , 1 , … , Z ^ a , L ) . \widehat{Z}_{a}^{L}\triangleq\bigl(Z_{a,0},\widehat{Z}_{a,1},\ldots,\widehat{Z}_{a,L}\bigr).
For each 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ L 1\leq\ell\leq L , we denote the partial trajectory up to step ℓ \ell (including the initial prefix) by
Z ^ a L , ℓ ≜ ( Z a , 0 , Z ^ a , 1 , … , Z ^ a , ℓ ) . \widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell}\triangleq\bigl(Z_{a,0},\widehat{Z}_{a,1},\ldots,\widehat{Z}_{a,\ell}\bigr).
We introduce the following shorthand notations (for j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y}) , r ∈ [ m ] r\in[m] , and ℓ , k ∈ [ L ] \ell,k\in[L] ):
ℰ j ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{j}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})
≜ 𝟙 τ ⁡ ( y ^ ℓ ) = j − π θ ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) , \displaystyle\triangleq\mathds{1}_{\tau(\widehat{y}_{\ell})=j}-\pi_{\theta}(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}),
(18a)
Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) \displaystyle\Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell-1}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})
≜ 1 2 ​ ( ⟨ W j , r , Z ^ a , k − 1 ⟩ + ∑ k ∈ [ L ] 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ⋅ ⟨ W j , r , Z p , k ⟩ ) . \displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\Big(\big\langle W_{j,r},\widehat{Z}_{a,k-1}\big\rangle+\sum_{k\in[L]}\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,k}\cdot\big\langle W_{j,r},Z_{p,k}\big\rangle\Big).
(18b)
Ξ ℓ , k ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) \displaystyle\Xi_{\ell,k}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})
≜ 1 2 ​ ∑ j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) ℰ j ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) ​ ∑ r ∈ [ m ] σ ′ ​ ( Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) ) ​ ⟨ W j , r , Z p , k ⟩ . \displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y})}\mathcal{E}_{j}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\sum_{r\in[m]}\sigma^{\prime}\big(\Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell-1}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\big)\langle W_{j,r},Z_{p,k}\rangle.
(18c)
Here, π θ ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) = 𝗌𝗈𝖿𝗍𝗆𝖺𝗑 ​ ( 𝖳𝖥 θ ​ ( Z ^ a , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) ) j \pi_{\theta}(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})=\mathsf{softmax}\left(\mathsf{TF}_{\theta}\left(\widehat{Z}_{a,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L}\right)\right)_{j} .
Fact A.1 (Gradients of Q) .
Given a problem length L L , we have the following expression for the policy gradient w.r.t. the attention matrix Q Q :
∇ Q 𝒥 ~ L = 1 L 𝔼 Z L , y ^ L [ 𝟙 y ^ L = y L ∑ ℓ = 1 L ∇ Q log π θ ( y ^ ℓ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) ] , \displaystyle\nabla_{Q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}=\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L},\widehat{y}^{L}}\left[\mathds{1}_{\widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L}}\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\nabla_{Q}\log\pi_{\theta}(\widehat{y}_{\ell}\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})\right],
where
∇ Q log π θ ( y ^ ℓ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) \displaystyle\nabla_{Q}\log\pi_{\theta}(\widehat{y}_{\ell}\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})
= ∑ k ∈ [ L ] 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ⋅ ( Ξ ℓ , k ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) − ∑ k ′ ∈ [ L ] 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ Ξ ℓ , k ′ ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) ) ​ x a , ℓ − 1 ​ x p , k ⊤ . \displaystyle=\sum_{{k}\in[L]}\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,k}\cdot\left(\Xi_{\ell,k}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})-\sum_{{k}^{\prime}\in[L]}\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,k}\Xi_{\ell,k^{\prime}}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\right)x_{a,\ell-1}x_{p,k}^{\top}.
Moreover, the gradient of Q Q for the supervised loss 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L \mathsf{Loss}_{L} can be written as:
− ∇ Q 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L \displaystyle-\nabla_{Q}\mathsf{Loss}_{L}
= 1 L ​ 𝔼 Z L ​ [ ∑ ℓ = 1 L ∑ k = 1 L 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ⋅ ( Ξ ℓ , k ​ ( Z a L , ℓ , Z p L ) − ∑ k ′ ∈ [ L ] 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ Ξ ℓ , k ′ ​ ( Z a L , ℓ , Z p L ) ) ​ x a , ℓ − 1 ​ x p , k ⊤ ] . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\Big[\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,k}\cdot\left(\Xi_{\ell,k}({Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})-\sum_{{k}^{\prime}\in[L]}\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,k}\Xi_{\ell,k^{\prime}}({Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\right)x_{a,\ell-1}x_{p,k}^{\top}\Big].
Then we consider the gradient of x ⊤ ​ ∇ Q ​ log ⁡ π θ ​ ( y ^ ℓ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) ​ x ′ {x}^{\top}\nabla_{Q}\log\pi_{\theta}(\widehat{y}_{\ell}\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})x^{\prime} for x , x ′ ∈ 𝒳 x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X} .
Lemma A.1 .
Given x , x ′ ∈ 𝒳 x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X} , if x = 𝔰 ⁡ ( x ′ ) x=\mathfrak{s}(x^{\prime}) , when x a , ℓ − 1 = x x_{a,\ell-1}=x for ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , then we have
x ⊤ ​ ∇ Q ​ log ⁡ π θ ​ ( y ^ ℓ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) ​ x ′ \displaystyle{x}^{\top}\nabla_{Q}\log\pi_{\theta}(\widehat{y}_{\ell}\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})x^{\prime}
= 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ⋅ ( Ξ ℓ , ℓ ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) − ∑ k ′ ∈ [ L ] 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ′ ​ Ξ ℓ , k ′ ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) ) \displaystyle=\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,\ell}\cdot\left(\Xi_{\ell,\ell}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})-\sum_{{k}^{\prime}\in[L]}\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,k^{\prime}}\Xi_{\ell,k^{\prime}}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\right)
= 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ⋅ ( ∑ j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) ℰ j ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) ​ ∑ r ∈ [ m ] σ ′ ​ ( Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) ) ⋅ ( ⟨ W j , r , Z p , ℓ ⟩ − Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) ) ) . \displaystyle=\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,\ell}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y})}\mathcal{E}_{j}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\sum_{r\in[m]}\sigma^{\prime}\big(\Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell-1}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\big)\cdot\Big(\langle W_{j,r},Z_{p,\ell}\rangle-\Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell-1}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\Big)\bigg).
Lemma A.2 .
Given x , x ′ ∈ 𝒳 x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X} , if x ≠ 𝔰 ⁡ ( x ′ ) x\neq\mathfrak{s}(x^{\prime}) , when x p , ℓ ′ = x ′ x_{p,\ell^{\prime}}=x^{\prime} and x a , ℓ − 1 = x x_{a,\ell-1}=x for ℓ , ℓ ′ ∈ [ L ] \ell,\ell^{\prime}\in[L] , noticing that ℓ ′ ≠ ℓ \ell^{\prime}\neq\ell , then we have
x ⊤ ​ ∇ Q ​ log ⁡ π θ ​ ( y ^ ℓ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) ​ x ′ \displaystyle{x}^{\top}\nabla_{Q}\log\pi_{\theta}(\widehat{y}_{\ell}\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})x^{\prime}
= 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ′ ⋅ ( Ξ ℓ , ℓ ′ ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) − ∑ k ′ ∈ [ L ] 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ′ ​ Ξ ℓ , k ′ ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) ) \displaystyle=\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\left(\Xi_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})-\sum_{{k}^{\prime}\in[L]}\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,k^{\prime}}\Xi_{\ell,k^{\prime}}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\right)
= 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ′ ⋅ ( ∑ j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) ℰ j ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) ​ ∑ r ∈ [ m ] σ ′ ​ ( Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) ) ⋅ ( ⟨ W j , r , Z p , ℓ ′ ⟩ − Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) ) ) . \displaystyle=\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y})}\mathcal{E}_{j}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\sum_{r\in[m]}\sigma^{\prime}\big(\Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell-1}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\big)\cdot\Big(\langle W_{j,r},Z_{p,\ell^{\prime}}\rangle-\Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell-1}_{a},Z_{p}^{L})\Big)\bigg).
Observe that there are d L − 1 d^{L-1} intermediate-state sequences
( y ^ 1 , … , y ^ L − 1 ) ∈ 𝒴 L − 1 (\widehat{y}_{1},\dots,\widehat{y}_{L-1})\in\mathcal{Y}^{L-1}
that lead to y ^ L = y L \widehat{y}_{L}=y_{L} .
Only these trajectories yield a nonzero terminal reward.
Hence, it suffices to restrict our attention to their contributions.
Combining this with the preceding lemmas, a direct calculation gives
Lemma A.3 .
Given x , x ′ ∈ 𝒳 x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X} , we have
•
if x = 𝔰 ⁡ ( x ′ ) x=\mathfrak{s}(x^{\prime}) , then

Proof.
The two results are obtained by invoking Lemma A.1 and Lemma A.2 , respectively. Specifically, for a given ℓ \ell , the event { x a , ℓ − 1 = x } \{x_{a,\ell-1}=x\} occurs with probability 1 / d 𝗉 1/d_{\mathsf{p}} , while for k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell , the event { x p , ℓ − 1 = 𝔰 − 1 ​ ( x ) , x p , k = x ′ , x ′ ≠ 𝔰 − 1 ​ ( x ) } \{x_{p,\ell-1}=\mathfrak{s}^{-1}(x),x_{p,k}=x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\neq\mathfrak{s}^{-1}(x)\} occurs with probability 1 d 𝗉 ​ ( d 𝗉 − 1 ) \frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}(d_{\mathsf{p}}-1)} . The remaining calculation details are omitted here for brevity.
∎

Notations for scalarized attention dynamics.
Based on the gradient update, the quantity ⟨ x , Q ​ x ′ ⟩ \langle x,Qx^{\prime}\rangle takes only two possible values,
depending on whether x = 𝔰 ⁡ ( x ′ ) x=\mathfrak{s}(x^{\prime}) (the matched position) or x ≠ 𝔰 ⁡ ( x ′ ) x\neq\mathfrak{s}(x^{\prime}) (a mismatched position).
Accordingly, we define the (unnormalized) target and non-target attention scores as
q \displaystyle q
≜ ⟨ Q ​ 𝔰 ​ ( x ) , x ⟩ , x ∈ 𝒳 , \displaystyle\triangleq\left\langle Q\,\mathfrak{s}(x),\,x\right\rangle,\qquad x\in\mathcal{X},
(21a)
r \displaystyle r
≜ ⟨ Q ​ 𝔰 ​ ( x ) , x ′ ⟩ , x ′ ∈ 𝒳 ∖ { x } . \displaystyle\triangleq\left\langle Q\,\mathfrak{s}(x),\,x^{\prime}\right\rangle,\qquad x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\}.
(21b)
With this notation, Lemma A.3 can be viewed as a policy-gradient update on ( q , r ) (q,r) .
Hence, it suffices to track the dynamics of these two scalars in the sequel. Thus, following ( 19 ), we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L = 1 2 ​ L ​ d 𝗉 ​ 𝔼 Z L ​ [ 𝔍 ⁡ ( y 0 , G L ) ] . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}=\frac{1}{2Ld_{\mathsf{p}}}\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\left[\mathfrak{J}(y_{0},G^{L})\right].
(22)
Furthermore, under this reduction, for a fixed problem length L L ,
the attention weights 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ′ \mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell^{\prime}} (for ℓ , ℓ ′ ∈ [ L ] \ell,\ell^{\prime}\in[L] )
take only two distinct values depending on whether the prompt position matches:
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell}
= e q e q + ( L − 1 ) ​ e r , \displaystyle=\frac{e^{q}}{e^{q}+(L-1)e^{r}},
(23a)
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ′ \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell^{\prime}}
= e r e q + ( L − 1 ) ​ e r , ℓ ′ ≠ ℓ . \displaystyle=\frac{e^{r}}{e^{q}+(L-1)e^{r}},\qquad\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell.
(23b)
When the context is clear, we denote the target attention weight
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ \mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell} by 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L \mathbf{Attn}_{L} for brevity.
A.2 Some Useful Bounds
Notations for activated neurons.
Fix an output index j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y}) , define the fiber 𝔉 j ≜ { ( g , y ) ∈ 𝒢 × 𝒴 : τ ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( y ) ) = j } \mathfrak{F}_{j}\triangleq\{(g,y)\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{Y}:\tau(g(y))=j\} , i.e., the set of transition–state pairs whose next state is tokenized as j j .
For each ( g , y ) ∈ 𝔉 j (g,y)\in\mathfrak{F}_{j} , let r g ⋅ y r_{g\cdot y} denote the (unique) neuron in the
pre-trained MLP that is activated for predicting j = τ ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( y ) ) j=\tau(g(y)) as defined in ( 5 ). We further define the set of all activated neurons
𝔄 ≜ ∪ j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) 𝔄 j , where 𝔄 j ≜ { r ∣ ∃ ( g , y ) ∈ 𝔉 j , r = r g ⋅ y } . \displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\triangleq\cup_{j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y})}\mathfrak{A}_{j},\text{ where }\mathfrak{A}_{j}\triangleq\{r\mid\exists(g,y)\in\mathfrak{F}_{j},r=r_{g\cdot y}\}.
Equivalently, 𝔄 \mathfrak{A} collects the activated neurons across all fibers { 𝔉 j } j ∈ [ n Y ] \{\mathfrak{F}_{j}\}_{j\in[n_{Y}]} .
Substituting the conditions from ( 5 ) yields the following characterizations of Λ j , r \Lambda_{j,r} .
Lemma A.5 (Characterizations of Λ \Lambda ) .
Given ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] and ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) (\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L}) .
Let { 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k } k = 1 L \{\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}\}_{k=1}^{L} denote the attention weights
from the answer token at step ℓ − 1 \ell-1 to the L L prompt tokens.
Then we have:
1.
For any j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y}) and any activated neuron r ∈ 𝔄 j r\in\mathfrak{A}_{j} ,
Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) = 1 2 ​ ( V j , r ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) + ∑ k = 1 L 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ V j , r ​ ( g k ) ) . \Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})=\frac{1}{2}\Big(V_{j,r}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})+\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}V_{j,r}(g_{k})\Big).
2.
For any j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y}) and any non-activated neuron r ∉ 𝔄 j r\notin\mathfrak{A}_{j} ,
Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) = 0 . \Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})=0.
Lemma A.6 (Values of Λ \Lambda at step ℓ \ell ) .
Fix ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] and an input ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) (\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L}) .
Let { 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k } k = 1 L \{\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}\}_{k=1}^{L} denote the attention weights.
Then the following properties hold.
1.
target transition g ℓ g_{\ell} .
Let j ≔ τ ⁡ ( g ℓ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ) j\coloneqq\tau\big(g_{\ell}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}\big) .
Then
Λ j , r g ℓ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) = 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ ​ B + σ 0 , \Lambda_{j,\,r_{g_{\ell}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})=\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,\ell}\,B+\sigma_{0},
and for any r ∈ 𝔄 j ∖ { r g ℓ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 } r\in\mathfrak{A}_{j}\setminus\{r_{g_{\ell}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}\} ,
Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) < 0 . \Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})<0.
