in-context distractor g k g_{k} , k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell .
Fix k ∈ [ L ] ∖ { ℓ } k\in[L]\setminus\{\ell\} and let
j ≔ τ ⁡ ( g k ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ) j\coloneqq\tau\big(g_{k}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}\big) .
Then
Λ j , r g k ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) = 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ B + σ 0 , \Lambda_{j,\,r_{g_{k}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})=\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}\,B+\sigma_{0},
and for any r ∈ 𝔄 j ∖ { r g k ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 } r\in\mathfrak{A}_{j}\setminus\{r_{g_{k}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}\} ,
Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) < 0 . \Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})<0.
3.
vocabulary distractor g g .
For any g ∈ 𝒢 ∖ G L g\in\mathcal{G}\setminus G^{L} and
j ≔ τ ⁡ ( g ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ) j\coloneqq\tau\big(g\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}\big) ,
Λ j , r g ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) = σ 0 , \Lambda_{j,\,r_{g\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})=\sigma_{0},
and for any r ∈ 𝔄 j ∖ { r g ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 } r\in\mathfrak{A}_{j}\setminus\{r_{g\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}\} ,
Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) < 0 . \Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})<0.
Proof.
By Lemma A.5 , for any j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y}) and any neuron r ∈ 𝔄 j r\in\mathfrak{A}_{j} , we have
Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) = 1 2 ​ ( V j , r ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) + ∑ k = 1 L 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ V j , r ​ ( g k ) ) . \Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})=\frac{1}{2}\Big(V_{j,r}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})+\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}V_{j,r}(g_{k})\Big).
By the simple transitivity assumption, there exists a unique g ⋆ ∈ 𝒢 g^{\star}\in\mathcal{G} such that
τ ⁡ ( g ⋆ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) ) = j \tau\!\big(g^{\star}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})\big)=j .
Invoking ( 5 ), we have
V j , r g ⋆ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) = B + 2 ​ σ 0 . V_{j,r_{g^{\star}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})=B+2\sigma_{0}.
If g ⋆ = g k ∈ G L g^{\star}=g_{k}\in G^{L} for some k ∈ [ L ] k\in[L] , then
V j , r g ⋆ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( g k ) = B V_{j,r_{g^{\star}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(g_{k})=B
and
V j , r g ⋆ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( g k ′ ) = − B V_{j,r_{g^{\star}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(g_{k^{\prime}})=-B for all k ′ ≠ k k^{\prime}\neq k .
Therefore,
2 ​ Λ j , r g ⋆ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) \displaystyle 2\Lambda_{j,r_{g^{\star}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})
= 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ B + ∑ k ′ ≠ k 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ′ ​ ( − B ) + ( B + 2 ​ σ 0 ) \displaystyle=\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}B+\sum_{k^{\prime}\neq k}\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k^{\prime}}(-B)+(B+2\sigma_{0})
= 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ B − ( 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ) ​ B + B + 2 ​ σ 0 \displaystyle=\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}B-(1-\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k})B+B+2\sigma_{0}
= 2 ​ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ B + 2 ​ σ 0 . \displaystyle=2\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}B+2\sigma_{0}.
Otherwise, if g ⋆ ∉ G L g^{\star}\notin G^{L} , then V j , r g ⋆ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( g k ) = − B V_{j,r_{g^{\star}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(g_{k})=-B for all k ∈ [ L ] k\in[L] ,
and hence
2 ​ Λ j , r g ⋆ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) = ∑ k = 1 L 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ​ ( − B ) + ( B + 2 ​ σ 0 ) = 2 ​ σ 0 . 2\Lambda_{j,r_{g^{\star}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}}}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})=\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}(-B)+(B+2\sigma_{0})=2\sigma_{0}.
Finally, consider any other pair ( g , y ) (g,y) such that τ ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( y ) ) = j \tau(g(y))=j but y ≠ y ^ ℓ − 1 y\neq\widehat{y}_{\ell-1} .
For the corresponding neuron r g ⋅ y ∈ 𝔄 j r_{g\cdot y}\in\mathfrak{A}_{j} , we have V j , r g ⋅ y ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) = − B V_{j,r_{g\cdot y}}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})=-B .
Moreover, among { g k } k = 1 L \{g_{k}\}_{k=1}^{L} , at most one index can contribute + B +B and the remaining contribute − B -B ,
so
2 ​ Λ j , r g ⋅ y ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) \displaystyle 2\Lambda_{j,r_{g\cdot y}}(\widehat{Z}_{a}^{L,\ell-1},Z_{p}^{L})
≤ ( 2 ​ max k ∈ [ L ] ​ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k − 1 ) ​ B − B \displaystyle\leq\Bigl(2\max_{k\in[L]}\mathbf{Attn}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}-1\Bigr)B-B
< 0 . \displaystyle<0.
This concludes the proof.
∎
Throughout the following analysis, we suppress the dependence on the underlying instance.
When the context is clear, we abbreviate Λ j , r ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ − 1 , Z p L ) \Lambda_{j,r}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell-1}_{a},Z_{p}^{L}) as Λ j , r \Lambda_{j,r} and ℰ j ​ ( Z ^ a L , ℓ , Z p L ) \mathcal{E}_{j}(\widehat{Z}^{L,\ell}_{a},Z_{p}^{L}) as ℰ j \mathcal{E}_{j} .
Hence, combining the reduced attention pattern in ( 23 ), namely, one target receiving weight 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)} and L − 1 L-1 symmetric non-targets, the above characterization of the activations Λ j , r \Lambda_{j,r} implies a step-invariant, context-level structure for the next-state
distribution π θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) \pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}) . In particular, the candidates decompose into three groups: (i) the target g ℓ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 g_{\ell}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1} ; (ii) the L − 1 L-1 symmetric non-targets { g ℓ ′ ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 : ℓ ′ ≠ ℓ } \{g_{\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}:\ \ell^{\prime}\neq\ell\} ; and (iii) the remaining d − L d-L states outside the context induced set (i.e., vocabulary distractors). We formalize this decomposition in the following lemma.
Lemma A.7 .
At step ℓ \ell , conditioning on y ^ ℓ − 1 \widehat{y}_{\ell-1} and G L G^{L} , the policy
π θ ( t ) ( ⋅ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) \pi^{(t)}_{\theta}(\cdot\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}) satisfies:
1.
For j = τ ⁡ ( g ℓ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) ) j=\tau\big(g_{\ell}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})\big) ,
π θ ( t ) ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) = d 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B d 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B + ( L − 1 ) ​ d 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) L − 1 ​ C B + ( d − L ) ≜ p L , 1 ( t ) . \displaystyle\pi^{(t)}_{\theta}(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})=\frac{d^{\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}}}{d^{\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}}+(L-1)d^{\frac{1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}}{L-1}C_{B}}+(d-L)}\triangleq p^{(t)}_{L,1}.
(24)
2.
For j ∈ τ ( { g ⋅ y ^ ℓ − 1 : g ∈ G L , g ≠ g ℓ } ) j\in\tau\Big(\big\{g\cdot\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}:g\in G^{L},g\neq g_{\ell}\big\}\Big) ,
π θ ( t ) ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) = d 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) L − 1 ​ C B d 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B + ( L − 1 ) ​ d 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) L − 1 ​ C B + ( d − L ) ≜ p L , 2 ( t ) . \displaystyle\pi^{(t)}_{\theta}(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})=\frac{d^{\frac{1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}}{L-1}C_{B}}}{d^{\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}}+(L-1)d^{\frac{1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}}{L-1}C_{B}}+(d-L)}\triangleq p^{(t)}_{L,2}.
(25)
3.
For any other j ∈ τ ⁡ ( 𝒴 ) j\in\tau(\mathcal{Y}) ,
π θ ( t ) ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) = 1 d 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B + ( L − 1 ) ​ d 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) L − 1 ​ C B + ( d − L ) ≜ p L , 3 ( t ) . \displaystyle\pi^{(t)}_{\theta}(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L})=\frac{1}{d^{\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}}+(L-1)d^{\frac{1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}}{L-1}C_{B}}+(d-L)}\triangleq p^{(t)}_{L,3}.
(26)
Moreover, π θ ( t ) ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) \pi^{(t)}_{\theta}(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}) does not depend on ℓ \ell .
Hence, we suppress the index ℓ \ell and write p L , 1 ( t ) p^{(t)}_{L,1} , p L , 2 ( t ) p^{(t)}_{L,2} ,
and p L , 3 ( t ) p^{(t)}_{L,3} for brevity.
Probabilistic Event.
We conclude this subsection by introducing a probabilistic event that characterizes the potential for path collisions , where an incorrect sequence of operations inadvertently leads to the same outcome as the intended compositional path. Such an event serves as a key tool for bounding the interference from distracting trajectories in our subsequent analysis:
𝔈 L ≜ { ∃ ( g ^ 1 , … , g ^ L ) , g ^ ℓ ∈ { g 1 , … , g L } for all ℓ ∈ [ L ] , s.t. g ^ L ∘ ⋯ g ^ 1 ( y 0 ) = y L } . \displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{L}\triangleq\Big\{\exists(\widehat{g}_{1},\dots,\widehat{g}_{L}),\ \widehat{g}_{\ell}\in\{g_{1},\dots,g_{L}\}\text{ for all }\ell\in[L],\ \text{s.t. }\widehat{g}_{L}\circ\cdots\widehat{g}_{1}(y_{0})=y_{L}\Big\}.
Lemma A.8 (Probability of Trajectory Collision) .
Under Assumption 3.1 , let 𝒢 \mathcal{G} be a finite non-abelian simple group of order d d . For a target state y L ∈ 𝒴 y_{L}\in\mathcal{Y} reached by a specific sequence of L L actions, the probability that any alternative sequence of L L actions (formed by the same set of available operators) hits y L y_{L} is bounded by:
ℙ ⁡ ( 𝔈 L ) = O ⁡ ( L L d ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{E}_{L})=O\Big(\frac{L^{L}}{d}\Big).
(27)
Proof.
To establish the bound, we consider the total number of possible compositional paths and the collision probability associated with each. In our setting, there are L L choices for each of the L L steps in a trajectory, resulting in at most L L L^{L} possible sequences within the set { g 1 , … , g L } L \{g_{1},\dots,g_{L}\}^{L} . According to the mixing properties of non-abelian simple groups ( Larsen and Shalev,, 2007 ) , any sequence that is not algebraically identical to the correct path ( g ^ L ∘ ⋯ ∘ g ^ 1 ) (\widehat{g}_{L}\circ\dots\circ\widehat{g}_{1}) induces a near-uniform distribution over 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} . Consequently, for any single incorrect trajectory, the probability of it hitting the specific target state y L y_{L} is 1 / d + o ⁡ ( 1 / d ) 1/d+o(1/d) . This asymptotic uniformity holds generally across the group, including the case where the target y L y_{L} is the initial state y 0 y_{0} . By applying the union bound over the collection of all L L L^{L} possible paths, we find that the total probability of an accidental collision is at most L L ⋅ ( 1 / d + o ⁡ ( 1 / d ) ) L^{L}\cdot(1/d+o(1/d)) . As the state space size d → ∞ d\to\infty , the probability of an accidental activation via an incorrect compositional path becomes negligible, yielding ℙ ⁡ ( 𝔈 L ) = O ⁡ ( L L / d ) \mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{E}_{L})=O(L^{L}/d) .
∎
Appendix B Gradient Characterization for General Length
In this section, we use spectral analysis to derive explicit gradient formulas for q ( t ) q^{(t)} and r ( t ) r^{(t)} under the step-invariant constraints in Lemma A.7 . For each fixed problem length L L , these characterizations provide the analytical foundation for our subsequent study of training dynamics across different training settings in later sections.
Action distribution induced by π θ \pi_{\theta} .
We introduce an action variable u ℓ ∈ 𝒢 u_{\ell}\in\mathcal{G} as the unique group element applied at step ℓ \ell .
By the simply-transitive action of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} on 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , each transition y ^ ℓ − 1 → y ^ ℓ \widehat{y}_{\ell-1}\to\widehat{y}_{\ell}
corresponds to a unique u ℓ u_{\ell} such that y ^ ℓ = u ℓ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) \widehat{y}_{\ell}=u_{\ell}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}) .
With this notation, Lemma A.7 can be equivalently stated as a step-invariant action distribution.
Lemma B.1 .
Fix a step ℓ \ell and condition on ( y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) (\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}) .
Then the policy π θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) \pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}) can be partitioned into the following three classes:

target action ( p L , 1 p_{L,1} ): applying the correct in-context rule g ℓ g_{\ell} ,
p L , 1 \displaystyle p_{L,1}
: = π θ ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) , j = τ ⁡ ( g ℓ ​ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) ) . \displaystyle:=\pi_{\theta}\left(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}\right),\qquad j=\tau\big(g_{\ell}(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1})\big).
(28a)
•
in-context distractor actions ( p L , 2 p_{L,2} ): applying an incorrect rule from the context,
p L , 2 \displaystyle p_{L,2}
: = π θ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) , j ∈ τ ( { g ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) : g ∈ G L , g ≠ g ℓ } ) . \displaystyle:=\pi_{\theta}\left(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}\right),\qquad j\in\tau\Big(\big\{g(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}):g\in G^{L},g\neq g_{\ell}\big\}\Big).
(28b)
•
vocabulary distractor actions ( p L , 3 p_{L,3} ): any other token not corresponding to an
in-context transition,
p L , 3 \displaystyle p_{L,3}
: = π θ ​ ( j ∣ y ^ ℓ − 1 , G L ) , j ∈ τ ⁡ ( { g ⁡ ( y ^ ℓ − 1 ) : g ∉ G L } ) . \displaystyle:=\pi_{\theta}\left(j\mid\widehat{y}_{\ell-1},G^{L}\right),\qquad j\in\tau\Big(\big\{g(\widehat{y}_{\ell-1}):g\notin G^{L}\big\}\Big).
(28c)

Intuitively, the gradient is driven by the gap between the posterior action probabilities
conditioned on success and their unconditional counterparts. Therefore, controlling the gradient reduces to estimating the posterior probabilities
ρ ℓ , 1 \rho_{\ell,1} and ρ ℓ , 2 \rho_{\ell,2} as functions of the probability tuple
( p L , 1 , p L , 2 , p L , 3 ) (p_{L,1},p_{L,2},p_{L,3}) .
In what follows, we suppress the dependence on ( θ , ( y 0 , G L ) ) (\theta,(y_{0},G^{L})) whenever the context is clear.
B.2 Preliminaries: Harmonic Analysis on 𝒢 \mathcal{G}
The trajectory measure introduced in the previous part involves cumulative products of group actions,
which correspond to repeated convolutions of measures on the underlying group.
A direct combinatorial analysis of these convolutions is often intractable.
To address this, we work in the Fourier domain via the irreducible representations of the group.
In this section, we briefly review the basic facts of harmonic analysis on finite
groups and collect the spectral tools we will use to decouple these convolution operations.
For background and more detailed treatments, see Serre, (1977) ; Kondor, (2008) .
Definition B.1 (Irreducible Representations) .
Let 𝒢 \mathcal{G} be a finite group of order | 𝒢 | = N |\mathcal{G}|=N . Let Λ \Lambda denote the set of equivalence classes of irreducible unitary representations of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . For each λ ∈ Λ \lambda\in\Lambda , λ : 𝒢 → U ⁡ ( d λ ) \lambda:\mathcal{G}\to U(d_{\lambda}) is homomorphism, where U ⁡ ( d λ ) U(d_{\lambda}) is the group of d λ × d λ d_{\lambda}\times d_{\lambda} unitary matrices.
•
The trivial representation is denoted by 𝟏 \mathbf{1} , with d 𝟏 = 1 d_{\mathbf{1}}=1 and 𝟏 ​ ( g ) = 1 , ∀ g \mathbf{1}(g)=1,\forall g .
•
Orthogonality Relations: For λ , η ∈ Λ \lambda,\eta\in\Lambda :
⟨ λ i ​ j , η k ​ l ⟩ = ∑ g ∈ 𝒢 λ i ​ j ​ ( g ) ​ η k ​ l ​ ( g ) ¯ = N d λ ​ δ λ ​ η ​ δ i ​ k ​ δ j ​ l \langle\lambda_{ij},\eta_{kl}\rangle=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\lambda_{ij}(g)\overline{\eta_{kl}(g)}=\frac{N}{d_{\lambda}}\delta_{\lambda\eta}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}
Specifically, for λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} , ∑ g ∈ 𝒢 λ ⁡ ( g ) = 𝟎 d λ × d λ \sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\lambda(g)=\mathbf{0}_{d_{\lambda}\times d_{\lambda}} .
Definition B.2 (Fourier Transform) .
Let Λ \Lambda be the set of irreducible unitary representations of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . For any λ ∈ Λ \lambda\in\Lambda , let d λ d_{\lambda} be its dimension. For a function f : 𝒢 → ℂ f:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{C} , the Fourier transform is:
f ^ ​ ( λ ) ≜ ∑ h ∈ 𝒢 f ⁡ ( h ) ​ λ ​ ( h ) . \widehat{f}(\lambda)\triangleq\sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}}f(h)\lambda(h).
Definition B.3 (Convolution) .
The convolution of two functions f , ν : 𝒢 → ℂ f,\nu:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{C} is defined as:
( f ∗ ν ) ​ ( g ) := ∑ h ∈ 𝒢 f ⁡ ( g ​ h − 1 ) ​ ν ​ ( h ) . (f*\nu)(g):=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}}f(gh^{-1})\nu(h).
The Fourier transform maps convolution to matrix multiplication:
f ∗ ν ^ ​ ( g ) = f ^ ​ ( g ) ​ ν ^ ​ ( g ) . \widehat{f*\nu}(g)=\widehat{f}(g)\widehat{\nu}(g).
Lemma B.2 (Fourier Inversion Formula & Plancherel Identity) .
Function f : 𝒢 → ℂ f:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{C} can be reconstructed from its Fourier coefficients:
f ⁡ ( g ) = 1 N ​ ∑ λ ∈ Λ d λ ​ Tr ​ ( f ^ ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( g ) − 1 ) . f(g)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\left(\widehat{f}(\lambda)\lambda(g)^{-1}\right).
Using the inversion formula at g = e g=e (identity), we have the identity:
∑ λ ∈ Λ d λ 2 = N . \sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}d_{\lambda}^{2}=N.
We distinguish the trivial representation 𝟏 \mathbf{1} (where λ ⁡ ( h ) = 1 \lambda(h)=1 ) from non-trivial representations λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} . Note that ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ 2 = N − 1 \sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}^{2}=N-1 .
Definition B.4 (Character Value & Spectral Decay Factor) .
Let 𝒢 \mathcal{G} be a finite group and let Λ \Lambda denote the set of its irreducible unitary representations. The character of a representation λ ∈ Λ \lambda\in\Lambda , denoted by χ λ : 𝒢 → ℂ \chi_{\lambda}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{C} , is defined as the trace of the linear operator λ ⁡ ( g ) \lambda(g) for each g ∈ 𝒢 g\in\mathcal{G} :
χ λ ​ ( g ) := Tr ⁡ ( λ ⁡ ( g ) ) . \chi_{\lambda}(g):=\mathrm{Tr}(\lambda(g)).
The scalar χ λ ​ ( g ) \chi_{\lambda}(g) is referred to as the character value of the element g g corresponding to λ \lambda . Furthermore, we define the spectral decay factor , denoted by γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) \gamma(\mathcal{G}) , as the maximum normalized character value over all non-trivial representations and non-identity elements:
γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) := max λ ∈ Λ , λ ≠ 𝟏 g ∈ 𝒢 , g ≠ e ⁡ | χ λ ​ ( g ) | d λ , \gamma(\mathcal{G}):=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda,\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}\\
g\in\mathcal{G},g\neq e\end{subarray}}\frac{|\chi_{\lambda}(g)|}{d_{\lambda}},
where d λ d_{\lambda} denotes the dimension of the representation λ \lambda .
Remark B.1 (Magnitude of γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) \gamma(\mathcal{G}) ) .
For any finite group 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , it holds that 0 ≤ γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ≤ 1 0\leq\gamma(\mathcal{G})\leq 1 . Specifically, if 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is abelian or has a non-trivial center Z ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ≠ { e } Z(\mathcal{G})\neq\{e\} , then γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) = 1 \gamma(\mathcal{G})=1 . If 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is a non-abelian simple group, then γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) < 1 \gamma(\mathcal{G})<1 . Furthermore, for many sequences of simple groups (e.g., P ​ S ​ L 2 ​ ( q ) PSL_{2}(q) ), γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) → 0 \gamma(\mathcal{G})\to 0 as | 𝒢 | → ∞ |\mathcal{G}|\to\infty , indicating rapid spectral decay.
B.3 Spectral Decomposition of the Step Measure
With the harmonic analysis framework in place, we translate conditional expectations under the
trajectory measure induced by 𝖳𝖥 θ \mathsf{TF}_{\theta} into convolution equations on the group.
Reduction to Group Actions.
Since 𝒢 \mathcal{G} acts simply transitively on 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , for any trajectory 𝒗 \bm{v} with fixed v 0 = y 0 v_{0}=y_{0} there
exists a unique sequence of group actions ( u 1 , … , u L ) ∈ 𝒢 L (u_{1},\ldots,u_{L})\in\mathcal{G}^{L} such that
v ℓ = u ℓ ​ ( v ℓ − 1 ) v_{\ell}=u_{\ell}(v_{\ell-1}) for ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] .
Consequently,
v L = ( u L ⋯ u 1 ) ( y 0 ) . v_{L}=(u_{L}\cdots u_{1})(y_{0}).
Let the target path be given by ( g 1 , … , g L ) (g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}) so that y L = ( g L ⋯ g 1 ) ( y 0 ) y_{L}=(g_{L}\cdots g_{1})(y_{0}) , and define the
target composition G ∗ ≜ g L ⋯ g 1 G_{\ast}\triangleq g_{L}\cdots g_{1} .
Then the endpoint constraint is equivalent to the group equation
v L = y L ⟺ u L ⋯ u 1 = G ∗ . v_{L}=y_{L}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad u_{L}\cdots u_{1}=G_{\ast}.
Under this representation, the posteriors of interest can be written as
ρ ℓ , 1 = ℙ ~ ( u ℓ = g ℓ | u L ⋯ u 1 = G ∗ ) , ρ ℓ , 2 = ℙ ~ ( u ℓ ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } | u L ⋯ u 1 = G ∗ ) . \rho_{\ell,1}=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(u_{\ell}=g_{\ell}\middle|u_{L}\cdots u_{1}=G_{\ast}\right),\qquad\rho_{\ell,2}=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(u_{\ell}\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}\middle|u_{L}\cdots u_{1}=G_{\ast}\right).
Definition B.5 (One-step measure) .
For each step ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , define a probability measure μ ℓ \mu_{\ell} on 𝒢 \mathcal{G} by
μ ℓ ​ ( h ) ≜ ℙ ~ ​ ( u ℓ = h ) = { p L , 1 , h = g ℓ , p L , 2 , h ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } , p L , 3 , h ∈ 𝒢 ∖ G L . \mu_{\ell}(h)\triangleq\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(u_{\ell}=h)=\begin{cases}p_{L,1},&h=g_{\ell},\\
p_{L,2},&h\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\},\\
p_{L,3},&h\in\mathcal{G}\setminus G^{L}.\end{cases}
Equivalently,
μ ℓ = p L , 1 ​ δ g ℓ + p L , 2 ​ δ G L ∖ { g ℓ } + p L , 3 ​ δ 𝒢 ∖ G L , \mu_{\ell}=p_{L,1}\delta_{g_{\ell}}+p_{L,2}\delta_{G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}}+p_{L,3}\delta_{\mathcal{G}\setminus G^{L}},
where δ S \delta_{S} denotes the (unnormalized) uniform measure on a set S ⊆ 𝒢 S\subseteq\mathcal{G} .
Moreover, the marginal probability of the endpoint is given by
ℙ ~ ( v L = y L ) = ( μ L ∗ ⋯ ∗ μ 1 ) ( G ∗ ) . \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(v_{L}=y_{L})=(\mu_{L}*\cdots*\mu_{1})(G_{\ast}).
We now compute the Fourier transform μ ^ ℓ ​ ( λ ) \widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\lambda) for a nontrivial irreducible
representation λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} .
Definition B.6 (Spectral objects and effective parameters) .
Given ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , for an irreducible representation λ \lambda of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , define the sample operator
W ℓ ​ ( λ ) ≜ ∑ h ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } λ ⁡ ( h ) . W_{\ell}(\lambda)\triangleq\sum_{h\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}}\lambda(h).
We also define the effective parameters
Δ L ≜ p L , 1 − p L , 3 , δ L ≜ p L , 2 − p L , 3 , σ G L ≜ max λ ≠ 𝟏 ⁡ max ℓ ∈ [ L ] ​ ‖ W ℓ ​ ( λ ) ‖ op , \Delta_{L}\triangleq p_{L,1}-p_{L,3},\qquad\delta_{L}\triangleq p_{L,2}-p_{L,3},\qquad\sigma_{G^{L}}\triangleq\max_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}\max_{\ell\in[L]}\|W_{\ell}(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}},
where ∥ ⋅ ∥ op \|\cdot\|_{\text{op}} denotes the operator norm induced by ∥ ⋅ ∥ 2 \|\cdot\|_{2} , i.e., ‖ A ‖ op ≔ sup ‖ x ‖ 2 = 1 ‖ A ​ x ‖ 2 \|A\|_{\text{op}}\coloneqq\sup_{\|x\|_{2}=1}\|Ax\|_{2} .
Lemma B.3 (Fourier transform of the one-step measure) .
Let μ ℓ \mu_{\ell} be the one-step measure in Definition B.5 .
For any nontrivial irreducible representation λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} ,
μ ^ ℓ ​ ( λ ) = Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g ℓ ) + δ L ​ W ℓ ​ ( λ ) . \widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\lambda)=\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{\ell})+\delta_{L}W_{\ell}(\lambda).
Proof.
By definition of μ ℓ \mu_{\ell} ,
μ ^ ℓ ​ ( λ ) = p L , 1 ​ λ ​ ( g ℓ ) + p L , 2 ​ ∑ h ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } λ ⁡ ( h ) + p L , 3 ​ ∑ h ∈ 𝒢 ∖ G L λ ⁡ ( h ) . \widehat{\mu}_{\ell}(\lambda)=p_{L,1}\lambda(g_{\ell})+p_{L,2}\sum_{h\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}}\lambda(h)+p_{L,3}\sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}\setminus G^{L}}\lambda(h).
For λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} , by Lemma B.2 , we have ∑ h ∈ 𝒢 λ ⁡ ( h ) = 0 \sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}}\lambda(h)=0 , hence
∑ h ∈ 𝒢 ∖ G L λ ⁡ ( h ) = − ( λ ⁡ ( g ℓ ) + W ℓ ​ ( λ ) ) \sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}\setminus G^{L}}\lambda(h)=-(\lambda(g_{\ell})+W_{\ell}(\lambda)) .
Substituting and collecting terms yields the claim.
∎
B.4 Posterior Estimation
Building on the operator decomposition, we compute the posterior probabilities by evaluating traces
of the resulting spectral products.
For notational convenience, we define the events
E ≜ { v L = y L } , A ℓ ≜ { u ℓ = g ℓ } , B ℓ ≜ { u ℓ ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } } . E\triangleq\{v_{L}=y_{L}\},\qquad A_{\ell}\triangleq\{u_{\ell}=g_{\ell}\},\qquad B_{\ell}\triangleq\{u_{\ell}\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}\}.
Thus, the main task is to control ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E) as well as the joint probabilities
ℙ ~ ​ ( A ℓ ∩ E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A_{\ell}\cap E) and ℙ ~ ​ ( B ℓ ∩ E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(B_{\ell}\cap E) .
Lemma B.4 .
With E , A ℓ , B ℓ E,A_{\ell},B_{\ell} defined above, we have the expansions
ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)
= 1 d + ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ Δ L L + ℛ E , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\Delta_{L}^{L}+\mathcal{R}_{E},
(36a)
ℙ ~ ​ ( A ℓ ∩ E ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A_{\ell}\cap E)
= p L , 1 d + ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 + ℛ A , \displaystyle=\frac{p_{L,1}}{d}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}+\mathcal{R}_{A},
(36b)
ℙ ~ ​ ( B ℓ ∩ E ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(B_{\ell}\cap E)
= ( L − 1 ) ​ p L , 2 d + ℛ B . \displaystyle=\frac{(L-1)p_{L,2}}{d}+\mathcal{R}_{B}.
(36c)
Moreover, the remainders satisfy
| ℛ E | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{E}|
≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ [ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − Δ L L − L ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 + ( L − 1 ) ​ L ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ] , \displaystyle\leq\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\Big[(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L}-\Delta_{L}^{L}-L\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}+(L-1)L\gamma(\mathcal{G})\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big],
(37a)
| ℛ A | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{A}|
≤ p L , 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ [ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 − ( L − 1 ) ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 2 + ( L − 1 ) 2 ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 2 ] , \displaystyle\leq p_{L,1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\Big[(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}-(L-1)\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-2}+(L-1)^{2}\gamma(\mathcal{G})\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-2}\Big],
(37b)
| ℛ B | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{B}|
≤ p L , 2 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ [ σ G L ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 ) + ( L − 1 ) ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ Δ L L − 1 ] . \displaystyle\leq p_{L,2}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\Big[\sigma_{G^{L}}\big((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\big)+(L-1)\gamma(\mathcal{G})\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big].
(37c)
Proof.
By Lemma B.2 and the convolution theorem, each quantity
ℙ ~ ​ ( ⋅ ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot) can be written as a sum of traces of products of Fourier operators.
We isolate the trivial-representation contribution and bound the remaining terms using the
decomposition μ ^ k ​ ( λ ) = Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) + δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) \widehat{\mu}_{k}(\lambda)=\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k})+\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda) .
We spell out the details for ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E) ; the bounds for
ℙ ~ ​ ( A ℓ ∩ E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A_{\ell}\cap E) and ℙ ~ ​ ( B ℓ ∩ E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(B_{\ell}\cap E) follow analogously.
Estimation of ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E) .
Let us start with ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E) . By Lemma B.2 , we have
ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)
= ( μ L ∗ ⋯ ∗ μ 1 ) ( G ∗ ) \displaystyle=(\mu_{L}\ast\cdots\ast\mu_{1})(G_{\ast})
= 1 d ​ ∑ λ ∈ Λ d λ ​ Tr ​ ( μ L ∗ ⋯ ∗ μ 1 ^ ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\left(\widehat{{\mu_{L}\ast\cdots\ast\mu_{1}}}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\right)
= 1 d ∑ λ ∈ Λ d λ Tr ( [ ∏ k = L 1 μ ^ k ​ ( λ ) ] ⏟ = : Π ⁡ ( λ ) λ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\Bigg(\underbrace{\left[\prod_{k=L}^{1}\widehat{\mu}_{k}(\lambda)\right]}_{=:\Pi(\lambda)}\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\Bigg).
•
For λ = 𝟏 \lambda=\mathbf{1} : μ ^ k ​ ( 𝟏 ) = 1 \widehat{\mu}_{k}(\mathbf{1})=1 . Hence Π ⁡ ( λ ) = 1 \Pi(\lambda)=1 , and we can obtain
1 d ​ d λ ​ Tr ​ ( Π ⁡ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) = 1 d ​ d 𝟏 ⋅ d 𝟏 = 1 d . \displaystyle\frac{1}{d}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\left(\Pi(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\right)=\frac{1}{d}d_{\mathbf{1}}\cdot d_{\mathbf{1}}=\frac{1}{d}.
•
For λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} :
by the decomposition μ ^ k ​ ( λ ) = Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) + δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) \widehat{\mu}_{k}(\lambda)=\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k})+\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda) from Lemma B.3 , we have
Π ⁡ ( λ ) \displaystyle\Pi(\lambda)
= ∏ k = L 1 ( Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) + δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) ) = Δ L L ​ ∏ k = L 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) + T res ​ ( λ ) . \displaystyle=\prod_{k=L}^{1}\big(\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k})+\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\big)=\Delta_{L}^{L}\prod_{k=L}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})+T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda).
Then the trace contribution by the first term is:
Tr ​ ( Δ L L ​ ( ∏ k = L 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) \displaystyle\text{Tr}\bigg(\Delta_{L}^{L}\big(\prod_{k=L}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\big)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)
= ( Δ L ) L ​ Tr ​ ( λ ⁡ ( ∏ k = L 1 g k ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) = d λ ​ ( Δ L ) L . \displaystyle=(\Delta_{L})^{L}\text{Tr}(\lambda(\prod_{k=L}^{1}g_{k})\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1})=d_{\lambda}(\Delta_{L})^{L}.
Summing this over all λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} , we obtain
1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ​ Tr ​ ( Δ L L ​ ( ∏ k = L 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) \displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\bigg(\Delta_{L}^{L}\big(\prod_{k=L}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\big)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)
= 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ⋅ d λ ​ ( Δ L ) L \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\cdot d_{\lambda}(\Delta_{L})^{L}
= ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( Δ L ) L , \displaystyle=\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(\Delta_{L})^{L},
where the last equality holds since ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ 2 = d − 1 \sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}^{2}=d-1 by Lemma B.2 .
Therefore, it suffices to control the operator norm of the residual term T res ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda) .
Notice that T res ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda) can be expanded into 2 L − 1 2^{L}-1 terms, each of the form M L ⋯ M 1 M_{L}\cdots M_{1} ,
where for each k k ,
M k ∈ { Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) , δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) } , M_{k}\in\left\{\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k}),\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\right\},
and at least one factor M k M_{k} equals δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) \delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda) .
We further decompose T res ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda) into two parts,
T res ​ ( λ ) = T res , 1 ​ ( λ ) + T res , 2 ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)=T_{\mathrm{res},1}(\lambda)+T_{\mathrm{res},2}(\lambda) :
–
T res , 1 ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res},1}(\lambda) consists of the terms for which there exists a unique k ∗ ∈ [ L ] k^{\ast}\in[L]
such that M k ∗ = δ L ​ W k ∗ ​ ( λ ) M_{k^{\ast}}=\delta_{L}W_{k^{\ast}}(\lambda) . In this case,
| Tr ​ ( δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( ∏ k = L k ∗ + 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ W k ∗ ​ ( λ ) ​ ( ∏ k = k ∗ − 1 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) | \displaystyle\Big|\text{Tr}\bigg(\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(\prod_{k=L}^{k^{\ast}+1}\lambda(g_{k})\Big)W_{k^{\ast}}(\lambda)\Big(\prod_{k=k^{\ast}-1}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\Big)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)\Big|
= | δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ Tr ​ ( W k ∗ ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( g k ∗ ) − 1 ) | \displaystyle=\Big|\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\text{Tr}(W_{k^{\ast}}(\lambda)\lambda(g_{k^{\ast}})^{-1})\Big|
= | δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ∑ g ∈ G L ∖ { g k ∗ } Tr ​ ( λ ⁡ ( g ​ g k ∗ − 1 ) ) | \displaystyle=\Big|\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\sum_{g\in{G^{L}}\setminus\{g_{k^{\ast}}\}}\text{Tr}(\lambda(gg_{k^{\ast}}^{-1}))\Big|
= δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ∑ g ∈ G L ∖ { g k ∗ } | χ λ ​ ( g ​ g k ∗ − 1 ) | \displaystyle=\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\sum_{g\in{G^{L}}\setminus\{g_{k^{\ast}}\}}\Big|\chi_{\lambda}(gg_{k^{\ast}}^{-1})\Big|
≤ δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( L − 1 ) ⋅ d λ ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) , \displaystyle\leq\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(L-1)\cdot d_{\lambda}\gamma(\mathcal{G}),
(38)
where the last inequality uses g ​ g k ∗ − 1 ≠ e gg_{k^{\ast}}^{-1}\neq e (here e e denotes the identity element of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} ) and the definition of γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) \gamma(\mathcal{G}) .
Since there are L L such terms in T res , 1 ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res},1}(\lambda) , we obtain
| 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ​ Tr ​ ( T res , 1 ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) | ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( L − 1 ) ​ L ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) . \displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\bigg(T_{\mathrm{res},1}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)\Big|\leq\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(L-1)L\gamma(\mathcal{G}).
–
T res , 2 ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res},2}(\lambda) collects the remaining 2 L − 1 − L 2^{L}-1-L terms, i.e., those for which at least
two factors M k M_{k} equal δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) \delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda) . Then
‖ T res , 2 ​ ( λ ) ‖ op \displaystyle\|T_{\mathrm{res},2}(\lambda)\|_{\text{op}}
= ∥ ∑ M k ∈ { Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) , δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) } , ∑ k = 1 L 𝟙 M k = δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) ≥ 2 M L ⋅ , ⋯ , ⋅ M 1 ∥ op \displaystyle=\Bigg\|\sum_{M_{k}\in\{\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k}),\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\},\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathds{1}_{M_{k}=\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)}\geq 2}M_{L}\cdot,\cdots,\cdot M_{1}\Bigg\|_{\text{op}}
≤ ∑ M k ∈ { Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) , δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) } , ∑ k = 1 L 𝟙 M k = δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) ≥ 2 ∏ k = L 1 ‖ M k ‖ op \displaystyle\leq\sum_{M_{k}\in\{\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k}),\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\},\sum_{k=1}^{L}\mathds{1}_{M_{k}=\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)}\geq 2}\prod_{k=L}^{1}\|M_{k}\|_{\text{op}}
≤ ( a ) ​ ∑ i = 2 L ( L i ) ​ Δ L L − i ​ ( σ G L ​ δ L ) i = ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − Δ L L − L ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 , \displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\sum_{i=2}^{L}\binom{L}{i}\Delta_{L}^{L-i}(\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L})^{i}=(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L})^{L}-\Delta_{L}^{L}-L\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1},
Here ( a ) (a) holds since ‖ Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) ‖ op ≤ Δ L \|\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k})\|_{\text{op}}\leq\Delta_{L} ,
‖ δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) ‖ op ≤ σ G L ​ δ L \|\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\|_{\text{op}}\leq\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L} , and there are exactly
( L i ) \binom{L}{i} choices of indices for which i i different M k M_{k} ’s equal δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) \delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda) .
Consequently,
| 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ​ Tr ​ ( T res , 2 ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) | ≤ 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ⋅ d λ ​ ‖ T res , 2 ​ ( λ ) ‖ op ​ ‖ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ‖ op \displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\bigg(T_{\mathrm{res},2}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)\Big|\leq\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\cdot d_{\lambda}\|T_{\mathrm{res},2}(\lambda)\|_{\text{op}}\|\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\text{op}}
= ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − Δ L L − L ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ) . \displaystyle=\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\bigg((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L})^{L}-\Delta_{L}^{L}-L\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\bigg).
Putting everything together, we obtain
ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) = 1 d + ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ Δ L L + ℛ E , \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)=\frac{1}{d}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\Delta_{L}^{L}+\mathcal{R}_{E},
where the remainder term ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} satisfies
| ℛ E | ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − Δ L L − L ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 + δ L ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( L − 1 ) ​ L ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ) . |\mathcal{R}_{E}|\leq\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\bigg((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L}-\Delta_{L}^{L}-L\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}+\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(L-1)L\gamma(\mathcal{G})\bigg).
Estimation of ℙ ~ ​ ( A ℓ ∩ E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A_{\ell}\cap E) .
The analysis is similar to that of ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E) .
The key difference is that we replace the measure at step ℓ \ell by the Dirac measure
p L , 1 ​ δ g ℓ p_{L,1}\delta_{g_{\ell}} , since the ℓ \ell -th step takes the action g ℓ g_{\ell} .
Correspondingly, its Fourier transform becomes p L , 1 ​ λ ​ ( g ℓ ) p_{L,1}\lambda(g_{\ell}) .
Hence,
ℙ ~ ​ ( A ℓ ∩ E ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A_{\ell}\cap E)
= ( μ L ∗ ⋯ p L , 1 δ g ℓ ⋯ ∗ μ 1 ) ( G ∗ ) \displaystyle=(\mu_{L}\ast\cdots p_{L,1}\delta_{g_{\ell}}\cdots\ast\mu_{1})(G_{\ast})
= 1 d ∑ λ ∈ Λ d λ Tr ( [ μ ^ L ( λ ) ⋯ p L , 1 λ ( g ℓ ) ⋯ μ ^ 1 ( λ ) ] ⏟ = : Π A ℓ ​ ( λ ) λ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\Big(\underbrace{\left[\widehat{\mu}_{L}(\lambda)\cdots p_{L,1}\lambda(g_{\ell})\cdots\widehat{\mu}_{1}(\lambda)\right]}_{=:\Pi_{A_{\ell}}(\lambda)}\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\Big).
•
For λ = 𝟏 \lambda=\mathbf{1} : since μ ^ k ​ ( 𝟏 ) = 1 \widehat{\mu}_{k}(\mathbf{1})=1 , we have Π A ℓ ​ ( 𝟏 ) = p L , 1 \Pi_{A_{\ell}}(\mathbf{1})=p_{L,1} and thus
1 d ​ d λ ​ Tr ​ ( Π A ℓ ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) = p L , 1 d . \displaystyle\frac{1}{d}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\left(\Pi_{A_{\ell}}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\right)=\frac{p_{L,1}}{d}.
•
For λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} : we can write
Π A ℓ ​ ( λ ) \displaystyle\Pi_{A_{\ell}}(\lambda)
= ( ∏ k = L ℓ + 1 ( Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) + δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) ) ) ​ ( p L , 1 ​ λ ​ ( g ℓ ) ) ​ ( ∏ k = ℓ − 1 1 ( Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) + δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) ) ) \displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{k=L}^{\ell+1}\big(\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k})+\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\big)\Big)\Big(p_{L,1}\lambda(g_{\ell})\Big)\Big(\prod_{k=\ell-1}^{1}\big(\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k})+\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\big)\Big)
= p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( ∏ k = L 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ λ ​ ( g ℓ ) ​ ( ∏ k = L 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) + T res , A ℓ ​ ( λ ) \displaystyle=p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(\prod_{k=L}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\Big)\lambda(g_{\ell})\Big(\prod_{k=L}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\Big)+T_{\mathrm{res},A_{\ell}}(\lambda)
The trace contribution of the leading term is
Tr ​ ( p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( ∏ k = L 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) \displaystyle\text{Tr}\bigg(p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\big(\prod_{k=L}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\big)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)
= p L , 1 ​ ( Δ L ) L − 1 ​ Tr ​ ( λ ⁡ ( ∏ k = L 1 g k ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) \displaystyle=p_{L,1}(\Delta_{L})^{L-1}\text{Tr}(\lambda(\prod_{k=L}^{1}g_{k})\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1})
= d λ ​ p L , 1 ​ ( Δ L ) L − 1 . \displaystyle=d_{\lambda}p_{L,1}(\Delta_{L})^{L-1}.
Summing over all λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} yields
1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ​ Tr ​ ( p L , 1 ​ ( Δ L ) L − 1 ​ ( ∏ k = L 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) \displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\bigg(p_{L,1}(\Delta_{L})^{L-1}\big(\prod_{k=L}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\big)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)
= 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ⋅ d λ ​ p L , 1 ​ ( Δ L ) L − 1 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\cdot d_{\lambda}p_{L,1}(\Delta_{L})^{L-1}
= ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 1 ​ ( Δ L ) L − 1 . \displaystyle=\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)p_{L,1}(\Delta_{L})^{L-1}.
The residual term T res , A ℓ ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res},A_{\ell}}(\lambda) can be controlled exactly as in the analysis of
T res ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda) , which gives
| 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ​ Tr ​ ( T res , A ℓ ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) | \displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\bigg(T_{\mathrm{res},A_{\ell}}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)\Big|
≤ p L , 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 − ( L − 1 ) ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 2 + δ L ​ Δ L L − 2 ​ ( L − 1 ) 2 ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ) . \displaystyle\leq p_{L,1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\bigg((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}-(L-1)\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-2}+\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-2}(L-1)^{2}\gamma(\mathcal{G})\bigg).
Putting the above bounds together, we conclude that
ℙ ~ ​ ( A ℓ ∩ E ) = p L , 1 d + ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 + ℛ A , \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A_{\ell}\cap E)=\frac{p_{L,1}}{d}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}+\mathcal{R}_{A},
where
| ℛ A | ≤ p L , 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 − ( L − 1 ) ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 2 + δ L ​ Δ L L − 2 ​ ( L − 1 ) 2 ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ) . |\mathcal{R}_{A}|\leq p_{L,1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\bigg((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}-(L-1)\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-2}+\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-2}(L-1)^{2}\gamma(\mathcal{G})\bigg).
Estimation of ℙ ~ ​ ( B ℓ ∩ E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(B_{\ell}\cap E) .
For B ℓ ∩ E B_{\ell}\cap E , at step ℓ \ell we use the measure p L , 2 ​ δ G L ∖ { g ℓ } p_{L,2}\delta_{G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}} ,
whose Fourier operator is
μ ^ B ℓ ​ ( λ ) = p L , 2 ​ ∑ g ∈ G L ∖ { g ℓ } λ ⁡ ( g ) = p L , 2 ​ W ℓ ​ ( λ ) . \widehat{\mu}_{B_{\ell}}(\lambda)=p_{L,2}\sum_{g\in G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}}\lambda(g)=p_{L,2}W_{\ell}(\lambda).
Hence,
ℙ ~ ​ ( B ℓ ∩ E ) \displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(B_{\ell}\cap E)
= ( μ L ∗ ⋯ p L , 2 δ G L ∖ { g ℓ } ⋯ ∗ μ 1 ) ( G ∗ ) \displaystyle=(\mu_{L}\ast\cdots p_{L,2}\delta_{G^{L}\setminus\{g_{\ell}\}}\cdots\ast\mu_{1})(G_{\ast})
= 1 d ∑ λ ∈ Λ d λ Tr ( [ μ ^ L ( λ ) ⋯ μ ^ B ℓ ( λ ) ⋯ μ ^ 1 ( λ ) ] ⏟ = : Π B ℓ ​ ( λ ) λ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\Big(\underbrace{\left[\widehat{\mu}_{L}(\lambda)\cdots\widehat{\mu}_{B_{\ell}}(\lambda)\cdots\widehat{\mu}_{1}(\lambda)\right]}_{=:\Pi_{B_{\ell}}(\lambda)}\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\Big).
•
For λ = 𝟏 \lambda=\mathbf{1} : since μ ^ k ​ ( 𝟏 ) = 1 \widehat{\mu}_{k}(\mathbf{1})=1 , we have Π B ℓ ​ ( 𝟏 ) = p L , 2 ​ ( L − 1 ) \Pi_{B_{\ell}}(\mathbf{1})=p_{L,2}(L-1) , and thus
1 d ​ d λ ​ Tr ​ ( Π B ℓ ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) = ( L − 1 ) ⋅ p L , 2 d . \displaystyle\frac{1}{d}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\left(\Pi_{B_{\ell}}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\right)=\frac{(L-1)\cdot p_{L,2}}{d}.
•
For λ ≠ 𝟏 \lambda\neq\mathbf{1} : analogous to the decomposition of T res ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda) , the operator Π B ℓ ​ ( λ ) \Pi_{B_{\ell}}(\lambda) can be
expanded into 2 L − 1 2^{L-1} terms of the form M L ⋯ ( p L , 2 W ℓ ( λ ) ) ⋯ M 1 M_{L}\cdots\big(p_{L,2}W_{\ell}(\lambda)\big)\cdots M_{1} , where, for each k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell ,
M k ∈ { Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) , δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) } . M_{k}\in\{\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k}),\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\}.
We further split Π B ℓ ​ ( λ ) \Pi_{B_{\ell}}(\lambda) into two parts,
Π B ℓ ​ ( λ ) = T B ℓ , 1 ​ ( λ ) + T B ℓ , 2 ​ ( λ ) . \Pi_{B_{\ell}}(\lambda)=T_{B_{\ell},1}(\lambda)+T_{B_{\ell},2}(\lambda).
–
T B ℓ , 1 T_{B_{\ell},1} consists of the unique term for which M k = Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) M_{k}=\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k}) for all k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell .
In this case,
| Tr ​ ( Δ L L − 1 ​ ( ∏ k = L k ∗ + 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ p L , 2 ​ W ℓ ​ ( λ ) ​ ( ∏ k = k ∗ − 1 1 λ ⁡ ( g k ) ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) | \displaystyle\Big|\text{Tr}\bigg(\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(\prod_{k=L}^{k^{\ast}+1}\lambda(g_{k})\Big)p_{L,2}W_{\ell}(\lambda)\Big(\prod_{k=k^{\ast}-1}^{1}\lambda(g_{k})\Big)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)\Big|
≤ p L , 2 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( L − 1 ) ⋅ d λ ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) , \displaystyle\leq p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(L-1)\cdot d_{\lambda}\gamma(\mathcal{G}),
where the inequality follows by an argument analogous to ( 38 ).
Therefore, we have:
| 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ​ Tr ​ ( T B ℓ , 1 ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) | ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 2 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( L − 1 ) ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) . \displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\bigg(T_{B_{\ell},1}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)\Big|\leq\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(L-1)\gamma(\mathcal{G}).
–
T B ℓ , 2 T_{B_{\ell},2} collects the remaining terms, i.e., those for which at least one index k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell
satisfies M k = δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) M_{k}=\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda) . Then
‖ T res , 2 ​ ( λ ) ‖ op \displaystyle\|T_{\mathrm{res},2}(\lambda)\|_{\text{op}}
= ∥ ∑ M k ∈ { Δ L ​ λ ​ ( g k ) , δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) } ​ for ​ k ≠ ℓ , ∑ k ≠ ℓ 𝟙 M k = δ L ​ W k ​ ( λ ) ≥ 1 M L ⋯ p L , 2 W ℓ ( λ ) ⋯ M 1 ∥ op \displaystyle=\Bigg\|\sum_{M_{k}\in\{\Delta_{L}\lambda(g_{k}),\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)\}\text{ for }k\neq\ell,\sum_{k\neq\ell}\mathds{1}_{M_{k}=\delta_{L}W_{k}(\lambda)}\geq 1}M_{L}\cdots p_{L,2}W_{\ell}(\lambda)\cdots M_{1}\Bigg\|_{\text{op}}
≤ p L , 2 ​ σ G L ​ ∑ i = 1 L ( L i ) ​ Δ L L − i ​ ( σ G L ​ δ L ) i \displaystyle~~~~\leq p_{L,2}\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\binom{L}{i}\Delta_{L}^{L-i}(\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L})^{i}
= p L , 2 ​ σ G L ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 ) , \displaystyle~~~~=p_{L,2}\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\Big((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big),
which can be shown by the same argument as in the bound for T res , 2 T_{\mathrm{res},2} .
Consequently,
| 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ​ Tr ​ ( T B ℓ , 2 ​ ( λ ) ​ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ) | \displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\text{Tr}\bigg(T_{B_{\ell},2}(\lambda)\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\bigg)\Big|
≤ 1 d ​ ∑ λ ≠ 𝟏 d λ ⋅ d λ ​ ‖ T B ℓ , 2 ‖ op ​ ‖ λ ​ ( G ∗ ) − 1 ‖ op \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda\neq\mathbf{1}}d_{\lambda}\cdot d_{\lambda}\|T_{B_{\ell},2}\|_{\text{op}}\|\lambda(G_{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\text{op}}
= ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 2 ​ σ G L ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 ) . \displaystyle=\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)p_{L,2}\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\Big((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{{G^{L}}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big).
Putting everything together, we obtain
ℙ ~ ​ ( B ℓ ∩ E ) = ( L − 1 ) ​ p L , 2 d + ℛ B , \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(B_{\ell}\cap E)=\frac{(L-1)p_{L,2}}{d}+\mathcal{R}_{B},
where the remainder term ℛ B \mathcal{R}_{B} satisfies
| ℛ B | ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 2 ​ ( σ G L ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 ) + Δ L L − 1 ​ ( L − 1 ) ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ) . |\mathcal{R}_{B}|\leq\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)p_{L,2}\bigg(\sigma_{G^{L}}\Big((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big)+\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(L-1)\gamma(\mathcal{G})\bigg).
∎
The expansions in Lemma B.4 immediately imply the following deviations of the
posterior probabilities ρ ℓ , 1 \rho_{\ell,1} and ρ ℓ , 2 \rho_{\ell,2} from their corresponding priors.
Proposition B.1 (Posterior deviation and dominant term) .
The posterior deviations admit the exact identities
ρ ℓ , 1 − p L , 1 \displaystyle\rho_{\ell,1}-p_{L,1}
= p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) + ℛ A − p L , 1 ​ ℛ E ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) , \displaystyle=\frac{p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(1-\Delta_{L})\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)+\mathcal{R}_{A}-p_{L,1}\mathcal{R}_{E}}{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)},
(39a)
p L , 2 − ρ ℓ , 2 L − 1 \displaystyle p_{L,2}-\frac{\rho_{\ell,2}}{L-1}
= p L , 2 ​ Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) + p L , 2 ​ ℛ E − ℛ B L − 1 ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) . \displaystyle=\frac{p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)+p_{L,2}\mathcal{R}_{E}-\frac{\mathcal{R}_{B}}{L-1}}{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)}.
(39b)
Moreover, if
σ G L ​ δ L Δ L ≪ 1 L , \frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}\ll\frac{1}{L},
(40)
then the remainder terms satisfy
| ℛ A | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{A}|
≤ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( O ⁡ ( σ G L 2 ​ δ L 2 Δ L 2 ) + ( L − 1 ) 2 ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ δ L Δ L ) , \displaystyle\leq p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\left(O\left(\frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}^{2}}{\Delta_{L}^{2}}\right)+\frac{(L-1)^{2}\gamma(\mathcal{G})\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}\right),
(41a)
| ℛ E | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{E}|
≤ Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( O ⁡ ( σ G L 2 ​ δ L 2 Δ L 2 ) + ( L − 1 ) ​ L ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ δ L Δ L ) , \displaystyle\leq\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\left(O\left(\frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}^{2}}{\Delta_{L}^{2}}\right)+\frac{(L-1)L\gamma(\mathcal{G})\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}\right),
(41b)
| ℛ B | L − 1 \displaystyle\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{B}|}{L-1}
≤ p L , 2 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( O ⁡ ( σ G L 2 ​ δ L ( L − 1 ) ​ Δ L ) + γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ) . \displaystyle\leq p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\left(O\left(\frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}}{(L-1)\Delta_{L}}\right)+\gamma(\mathcal{G})\right).
(41c)
Proof.
For ρ ℓ , 1 − p L , 1 \rho_{\ell,1}-p_{L,1} , by Lemma B.4 we have
ρ ℓ , 1 − p L , 1 \displaystyle\rho_{\ell,1}-p_{L,1}
= ℙ ~ ​ ( A ℓ ∩ E ) − p L , 1 ​ ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \displaystyle=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A_{\ell}\cap E)-p_{L,1}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)}{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)}
= p L , 1 d + p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) + ℛ A − p L , 1 ​ ( 1 d + Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) + ℛ E ) ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \displaystyle=\frac{\frac{p_{L,1}}{d}+p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)+\mathcal{R}_{A}-p_{L,1}\Big(\frac{1}{d}+\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)+\mathcal{R}_{E}\Big)}{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)}
= p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) + ℛ A − p L , 1 ​ ℛ E ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) , \displaystyle=\frac{p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}(1-\Delta_{L})\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)+\mathcal{R}_{A}-p_{L,1}\mathcal{R}_{E}}{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)},
which gives ( 39a ).
For p L , 2 − ρ ℓ , 2 L − 1 p_{L,2}-\frac{\rho_{\ell,2}}{L-1} , we similarly obtain
p L , 2 − ρ ℓ , 2 L − 1 \displaystyle p_{L,2}-\frac{\rho_{\ell,2}}{L-1}
= p L , 2 ​ ( L − 1 ) ​ ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) − ℙ ~ ​ ( B ℓ ∩ E ) ( L − 1 ) ​ ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \displaystyle=\frac{p_{L,2}(L-1)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)-\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(B_{\ell}\cap E)}{(L-1)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)}
= p L , 2 ​ ( L − 1 ) ​ ( 1 d + Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) + ℛ E ) − ( L − 1 ) ​ p L , 2 d − ℛ B ( L − 1 ) ​ ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \displaystyle=\frac{p_{L,2}(L-1)\Big(\frac{1}{d}+\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)+\mathcal{R}_{E}\Big)-\frac{(L-1)p_{L,2}}{d}-\mathcal{R}_{B}}{(L-1)\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)}
= p L , 2 ​ Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) + p L , 2 ​ ℛ E − ℛ B / ( L − 1 ) ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) , \displaystyle=\frac{p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)+p_{L,2}\mathcal{R}_{E}-\mathcal{R}_{B}/(L-1)}{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)},
which gives ( 39b ).
It remains to bound ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} , ℛ A \mathcal{R}_{A} , and ℛ B \mathcal{R}_{B} under ( 40 ).
For notational simplicity, let x ≜ σ G L ​ δ L / Δ L x\triangleq\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}/\Delta_{L} . Then
( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) k = Δ L k ​ ( 1 + x ) k . (\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{k}=\Delta_{L}^{k}(1+x)^{k}.
Bounds for ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} and ℛ A \mathcal{R}_{A} .
The expressions in the brackets for ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} and ℛ A \mathcal{R}_{A} contain
( 1 + x ) k − 1 − k ​ x , k ∈ { L , L − 1 } . (1+x)^{k}-1-kx,\qquad k\in\{L,L-1\}.
Under x ≪ 1 / L x\ll 1/L , the second-order Taylor remainder gives ( 1 + x ) k − 1 − k ​ x = O ⁡ ( k 2 ​ x 2 ) (1+x)^{k}-1-kx=O(k^{2}x^{2}) , which implies
( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) k − Δ L k − k ​ σ G L ​ δ L ​ Δ L k − 1 = Δ L k ⋅ O ⁡ ( k 2 ​ x 2 ) = O ⁡ ( k 2 ​ σ G L 2 ​ δ L 2 ​ Δ L k − 2 ) . (\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{k}-\Delta_{L}^{k}-k\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{k-1}=\Delta_{L}^{k}\cdot O(k^{2}x^{2})=O\big(k^{2}\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}^{2}\Delta_{L}^{k-2}\big).
Substituting this estimate into the displayed bounds for ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} and ℛ A \mathcal{R}_{A} yields the claimed
controls for | ℛ E | |\mathcal{R}_{E}| and | ℛ A | |\mathcal{R}_{A}| .
Bound for ℛ B \mathcal{R}_{B} .
Here the bracket contains ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 = Δ L L − 1 ​ ( ( 1 + x ) L − 1 − 1 ) (\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}=\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\big((1+x)^{L-1}-1\big) .
Under x ≪ 1 / L x\ll 1/L , the first-order estimate gives ( 1 + x ) L − 1 − 1 = O ⁡ ( ( L − 1 ) ​ x ) (1+x)^{L-1}-1=O((L-1)x) , hence
σ G L ​ ( ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 − Δ L L − 1 ) = σ G L ​ Δ L L − 1 ⋅ O ⁡ ( ( L − 1 ) ​ σ G L ​ δ L / Δ L ) = O ⁡ ( ( L − 1 ) ​ σ G L 2 ​ δ L ​ Δ L L − 2 ) . \sigma_{G^{L}}\big((\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}-\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\big)=\sigma_{G^{L}}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\cdot O((L-1)\sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}/\Delta_{L})=O\big((L-1)\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}\Delta_{L}^{L-2}\big).
Plugging this into the displayed bound for ℛ B \mathcal{R}_{B} yields the stated control on | ℛ B | |\mathcal{R}_{B}| .
∎
B.5 Gradient Characterization: Proof of Lemma 6.2
Combining the posterior deviations in Proposition B.1 with
( 35 ), we obtain the following characterization of the gradient, which is a formal version of Lemma 6.2 .
Proposition B.2 (Gradient characterization) .
Given problem length L L , suppose that
L 2 ​ δ L Δ L = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) and p L , 2 p L , 1 = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) . \frac{L^{2}\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{p_{L,2}}{p_{L,1}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}).
Then
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}
= Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ d / d 𝗉 ) ⋅ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) , \displaystyle=\Theta(\log d/d_{\mathsf{p}})\cdot p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L}),
| ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L | \displaystyle|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}|
= O ⁡ ( 1 / d 𝗉 ) ⋅ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L . \displaystyle=O(1/d_{\mathsf{p}})\cdot\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}.
Proof.
Recall that
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L = 1 2 ​ L ​ d 𝗉 𝔼 Z L [ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ⋅ B ⋅ ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) ​ ∑ ℓ = 1 L ( ( ρ ℓ , 1 − p L , 1 ) ​ ( 2 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ) + ( p L , 2 − ρ ℓ , 2 L − 1 ) ​ ( 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ) ) ⏟ = : J gap ] . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}=\frac{1}{2Ld_{\mathsf{p}}}\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\Big[\mathbf{Attn}_{L}\cdot B\cdot\underbrace{\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\Big((\rho_{\ell,1}-p_{L,1})(2-\mathbf{Attn}_{L})+\Big(p_{L,2}-\frac{\rho_{\ell,2}}{L-1}\Big)(1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L})\Big)}_{=:J_{\text{gap}}}\Big].
(42)
Note that 2 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ≥ 1 2-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}\geq 1 and 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) 1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}\in(0,1) . Under the stated assumptions, since σ G L ≤ L − 1 \sigma_{G^{L}}\leq L-1 , the condition
σ G L ​ δ L / Δ L ≪ 1 / L \sigma_{G^{L}}\delta_{L}/\Delta_{L}\ll 1/L holds. Hence Proposition B.1 applies.
We now verify that for J gap J_{\text{gap}} , all remainder contributions are negligible compared to the leading term
p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\big(1-\frac{1}{d}\big)(1-\Delta_{L}) .
•
Bounding ℛ A \mathcal{R}_{A} .
By ( 41a ) in Proposition B.1 ,
| ℛ A | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{A}|
≤ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( O ⁡ ( σ G L 2 ​ δ L 2 Δ L 2 ) + ( L − 1 ) 2 ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ δ L Δ L ) . \displaystyle\leq p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\left(O\left(\frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}^{2}}{\Delta_{L}^{2}}\right)+\frac{(L-1)^{2}\gamma(\mathcal{G})\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}\right).
Using σ G L ≤ L − 1 \sigma_{G^{L}}\leq L-1 and L 2 ​ δ L Δ L = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) \frac{L^{2}\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}) ,
we have
σ G L 2 ​ δ L 2 Δ L 2 ≤ L 2 ​ δ L Δ L ⋅ δ L Δ L = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) ⋅ δ L Δ L = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) , \frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}^{2}}{\Delta_{L}^{2}}\leq\frac{L^{2}\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}\cdot\frac{\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L})\cdot\frac{\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}),
and similarly
( L − 1 ) 2 ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ δ L Δ L ≤ γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ⋅ o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) . \frac{(L-1)^{2}\gamma(\mathcal{G})\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}\leq\gamma(\mathcal{G})\cdot o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}).
Therefore,
| ℛ A | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{A}|
≤ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) ​ ( o ⁡ ( 1 ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ) ≪ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) . \displaystyle\leq p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L})\big(o(1)+o(1)\gamma(\mathcal{G})\big)\ll p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L}).
•
Bounding ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} .
By ( 41b ) in Proposition B.1 ,
| ℛ E | \displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{E}|
≤ Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( O ⁡ ( σ G L 2 ​ δ L 2 Δ L 2 ) + ( L − 1 ) ​ L ​ γ ​ ( 𝒢 ) ​ δ L Δ L ) . \displaystyle\leq\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\left(O\left(\frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}^{2}}{\Delta_{L}^{2}}\right)+\frac{(L-1)L\gamma(\mathcal{G})\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}\right).
Using the same estimates as above and Δ L ≤ 1 \Delta_{L}\leq 1 , we obtain
| ℛ E | ≪ Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) . |\mathcal{R}_{E}|\ll\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L}).
Consequently, p L , 1 ​ | ℛ E | p_{L,1}|\mathcal{R}_{E}| is dominated by p L , 1 ​ Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L}) .
•
Bounding ℛ B \mathcal{R}_{B} .
By ( 41c ) in Proposition B.1 ,
| ℛ B | L − 1 \displaystyle\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{B}|}{L-1}
≤ p L , 2 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( O ⁡ ( σ G L 2 ​ δ L ( L − 1 ) ​ Δ L ) + γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ) . \displaystyle\leq p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\left(O\left(\frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}}{(L-1)\Delta_{L}}\right)+\gamma(\mathcal{G})\right).
Using σ G L ≤ L − 1 \sigma_{G^{L}}\leq L-1 and L 2 ​ δ L Δ L = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) \frac{L^{2}\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}) ,
we have
σ G L 2 ​ δ L ( L − 1 ) ​ Δ L ≤ L 2 ​ δ L Δ L = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) . \frac{\sigma_{G^{L}}^{2}\delta_{L}}{(L-1)\Delta_{L}}\leq\frac{L^{2}\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}).
Moreover, p L , 2 p L , 1 = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) \frac{p_{L,2}}{p_{L,1}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}) implies
p L , 2 ​ Δ L L − 1 ≤ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ⋅ o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) . p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\leq p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\cdot o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}).
Thus,
| ℛ B | L − 1 \displaystyle\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{B}|}{L-1}
≤ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) ​ ( o ⁡ ( 1 ) + γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) ⋅ o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ≪ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) . \displaystyle\leq p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L})\Big(o(1)+\gamma(\mathcal{G})\cdot o(1)\Big)\ll p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L}).
Finally, the same assumption p L , 2 p L , 1 = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) \frac{p_{L,2}}{p_{L,1}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}) also yields
p L , 2 ​ Δ L L ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ≪ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) , p_{L,2}\Delta_{L}^{L}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)\ll p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L}),
so the contribution of the second posterior deviation term is dominated by the first term.
Plugging the above bounds into the expression for ∇ q 𝒥 L \nabla_{q}\mathcal{J}_{L} , and using that
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathbf{Attn}_{L}=\Theta(1) , which is implied by p L , 2 p L , 1 = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) \frac{p_{L,2}}{p_{L,1}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}) , we conclude that
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L = Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ d / d 𝗉 ) ⋅ p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ) . \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}=\Theta(\log d/d_{\mathsf{p}})\cdot p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{d}\Big)(1-\Delta_{L}).
The analysis for | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L | |\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}| is similar. Alternatively, we may invoke the direct comparison
bound in Lemma A.3 to obtain
| ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L | = O ⁡ ( 1 / d 𝗉 ) ⋅ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L |\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}|=O(1/d_{\mathsf{p}})\cdot\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L} .
∎
B.6 Exponentially Flat Region for Long-Horizon Tasks: Proof of Proposition 4.1
Following the same decomposition underlying Lemma B.4 ,
we show that when the step-invariant probability tuple ( p L , 1 , p L , 2 , p L , 3 ) (p_{L,1},p_{L,2},p_{L,3})
has small effective margins
Δ L := p L , 1 − p L , 3 \Delta_{L}:=p_{L,1}-p_{L,3} and δ L := p L , 2 − p L , 3 \delta_{L}:=p_{L,2}-p_{L,3} , the resulting policy gradient is upper bounded by a quantity that decays
exponentially in the horizon length L L .
We then specialize this general exponential barrier to our concrete setting,
which immediately yields Proposition 4.1 .
Proposition B.3 .
Under Assumptions 3.1 – 3.3 ,
for any 2 ≤ L ≤ L max 2\leq L\leq L_{\max} , suppose the step-invariant probability tuple
( p L , 1 , p L , 2 , p L , 3 ) (p_{L,1},p_{L,2},p_{L,3}) satisfies, with
Δ L := p L , 1 − p L , 3 \Delta_{L}:=p_{L,1}-p_{L,3} and δ L := p L , 2 − p L , 3 \delta_{L}:=p_{L,2}-p_{L,3} ,
Δ L + L ​ δ L ≤ O ~ ​ ( d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ) , p L , i ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) for ​ i ∈ [ 3 ] . \displaystyle\Delta_{L}+L\delta_{L}\leq\widetilde{O}\left(d^{-\Omega(1)}\right),\qquad p_{L,i}\leq d^{-\Omega(1)}\ \ \text{for }i\in[3].
(43)
Then,
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L ) , | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 2 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L ) . \left|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}\right|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L)},\qquad\left|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}\right|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L)}.
(44)
Proof.
A key takeaway from Lemma B.4 is that,
when bounding the remainder contributions (e.g., ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} ),
we decompose the remainder term T res ​ ( λ ) T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda) into several parts.
Independent of this finer decomposition, its operator norm admits the crude bound
‖ T res ​ ( λ ) ‖ op ≤ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − Δ L L . \|T_{\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G_{L}}\delta_{L})^{L}-\Delta_{L}^{L}.
Using this bound directly gives
ℛ E ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L . \mathcal{R}_{E}\leq\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{d}\Bigr)(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G_{L}}\delta_{L})^{L}.
The same argument applies to ℛ A \mathcal{R}_{A} and ℛ B \mathcal{R}_{B} , yielding
ℛ A ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 1 ​ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 , ℛ B ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ p L , 2 ​ σ G L ​ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 . \mathcal{R}_{A}\leq\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{d}\Bigr)p_{L,1}(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G_{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{B}\leq\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{d}\Bigr)p_{L,2}\sigma_{G_{L}}(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G_{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}.
Invoking ( 39 ) from Proposition B.1 and substituting the above bounds into
( 42 ), we obtain
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L | \displaystyle\left|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}\right|
≤ O ~ ​ ( B d 𝗉 ) ​ ( p L , 1 ​ Δ L L − 1 + p L , 1 ​ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 + p L , 2 ​ σ G L L − 1 ​ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L − 1 ) \displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{B}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\Bigl(p_{L,1}\Delta_{L}^{L-1}+p_{L,1}(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G_{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}+p_{L,2}\frac{\sigma_{G_{L}}}{L-1}(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G_{L}}\delta_{L})^{L-1}\Bigr)
≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L ) , \displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L)},
where in the last step we use σ G L ≤ L − 1 \sigma_{G_{L}}\leq L-1 together with the assumptions
Δ L + L ​ δ L ≤ O ~ ​ ( d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ) \Delta_{L}+L\delta_{L}\leq\widetilde{O}\left(d^{-\Omega(1)}\right) and
p L , i ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) p_{L,i}\leq d^{-\Omega(1)} for i ∈ [ 3 ] i\in[3] .
The bound for | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L | \left|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}\right| follows by the same reasoning and is omitted.
∎
Proposition B.4 ( Proposition 4.1 restated) .
Under Assumptions 3.1 – 3.3 ,
suppose 𝖳𝖥 θ ( 0 ) \mathsf{TF}_{\theta^{(0)}} is initialized according to Assumption 3.4 .
Then for any horizon L > 2 ​ C B L>2C_{B} , whenever the feature magnitudes satisfy
max ⁡ { | r ( t ) | , | q ( t ) | } ≤ 0.01 \max\{|r^{(t)}|,|q^{(t)}|\}\leq 0.01 , we have 𝒥 L ( t ) = 1 d ​ ( 1 ± o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) \mathcal{J}_{L}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{d}(1\pm o(1)) , and
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L ) , | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 2 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L ) . \big|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L)},\qquad\big|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L)}.
Proof.
Since max ⁡ { | r ( t ) | , | q ( t ) | } ≤ 0.01 \max\{|r^{(t)}|,|q^{(t)}|\}\leq 0.01 , the attention weights satisfy
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B ≤ C B ​ e 0.02 e 0.02 + L − 1 < 1 , 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) L − 1 ​ C B ≤ C B e − 0.02 + L − 1 < 1 . \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L}C_{B}\leq\frac{C_{B}e^{0.02}}{e^{0.02}+L-1}<1,\qquad\frac{1-\mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L}}{L-1}C_{B}\leq\frac{C_{B}}{e^{-0.02}+L-1}<1.
In particular, this implies p L , i ( t ) ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) p_{L,i}^{(t)}\leq d^{-\Omega(1)} for all i ∈ [ 3 ] i\in[3] .
It remains to bound Δ L ( t ) + L ​ δ L ( t ) \Delta_{L}^{(t)}+L\delta_{L}^{(t)} , which we do by considering two regimes.
Case 1: L < d 0.01 L<d^{0.01} .
By Lemma A.7 ,
p L , 1 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 L + d 1 − C B ​ e 0.02 e 0.02 + L − 1 ) = d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) , p L , 2 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 L + d 1 − C B e − 0.02 + L − 1 ) ≤ d − 0.5 . p_{L,1}^{(t)}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{L+d^{1-\frac{C_{B}e^{0.02}}{e^{0.02}+L-1}}}\right)=d^{-\Omega(1)},\qquad p_{L,2}^{(t)}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{L+d^{1-\frac{C_{B}}{e^{-0.02}+L-1}}}\right)\leq d^{-0.5}.
Therefore,
Δ L ( t ) + L ​ δ L ( t ) ≤ p L , 1 ( t ) + L ​ p L , 2 ( t ) ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) . \Delta_{L}^{(t)}+L\delta_{L}^{(t)}\leq p_{L,1}^{(t)}+Lp_{L,2}^{(t)}\leq d^{-\Omega(1)}.
Case 2: L ≥ d 0.01 L\geq d^{0.01} .
In this regime, we bound Δ L ( t ) \Delta_{L}^{(t)} and δ L ( t ) \delta_{L}^{(t)} directly.
In particular,
Δ L ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( e C B ​ e 0.02 e 0.02 + L − 1 ​ log ⁡ d − 1 d ) ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 L ​ d ) , \Delta_{L}^{(t)}\leq O\left(\frac{e^{\frac{C_{B}e^{0.02}}{e^{0.02}+L-1}\log d}-1}{d}\right)\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{Ld}\right),
and
L ​ δ L ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( L ⋅ e C B e − 0.02 + L − 1 ​ log ⁡ d − 1 d ) ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d ) . L\delta_{L}^{(t)}\leq O\left(L\cdot\frac{e^{\frac{C_{B}}{e^{-0.02}+L-1}\log d}-1}{d}\right)\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d}\right).
Thus,
Δ L ( t ) + L ​ δ L ( t ) ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d ) . \Delta_{L}^{(t)}+L\delta_{L}^{(t)}\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d}\right).
In both regimes, the conditions of Proposition B.3 are satisfied.
Therefore, applying Proposition B.3 yields the desired gradient bound.
Moreover, 𝒥 L ( t ) = 1 d ​ ( 1 ± o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) \mathcal{J}_{L}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{d}(1\pm o(1)) follows directly from Lemma B.6 .
∎
B.7 Reward Characterization
Note that ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E) is exactly the expected reward for a fixed instance ( y 0 , G L ) (y_{0},G_{L}) .
Consequently,
𝒥 L = 𝔼 Z L ​ [ ℙ ~ ​ ( E ) ] . \mathcal{J}_{L}=\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\big[\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(E)\big].
Therefore, the gradient characterization in Proposition B.2 immediately yields a corresponding characterization of the reward.
Lemma B.5 .
Given a problem of length L L , suppose that
L 2 ​ δ L Δ L = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) and p L , 2 p L , 1 = o ⁡ ( 1 ) ⋅ ( 1 − Δ L ) . \frac{L^{2}\delta_{L}}{\Delta_{L}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{p_{L,2}}{p_{L,1}}=o(1)\cdot(1-\Delta_{L}).
Then,
𝒥 L = 1 d + ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( 1 ± o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ⋅ Δ L L . \mathcal{J}_{L}=\frac{1}{d}+\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{d}\Bigr)(1\pm o(1))\cdot\Delta_{L}^{L}.
Moreover, by adapting the argument in Proposition B.3 to control the residual term ℛ E \mathcal{R}_{E} , we obtain the following coarse upper bound.
Lemma B.6 .
Given a problem of length L L , we have
| 𝒥 L − 1 d | ≤ ( 1 − 1 d ) ​ ( Δ L + σ G L ​ δ L ) L . \Big|\mathcal{J}_{L}-\frac{1}{d}\Big|\leq\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{d}\Bigr)\bigl(\Delta_{L}+\sigma_{G_{L}}\delta_{L}\bigr)^{L}.
Appendix C Learning Dynamics of Short-horizon RL
In this section, we focus on the regime L ≤ C B L\leq C_{B} . Our analysis tracks the training dynamics of the two scalar quantities q q and r r
defined in ( 21a ) and ( 21b ).
We proceed in three steps.
First, we state an induction hypothesis that is maintained throughout training.
Second, under this hypothesis, we derive one-step update bounds for q q and r r .
Finally, we close the induction by showing that the hypothesis holds for all iterations.
We will focus on the RL training dynamics; the same proof structure and bookkeeping apply to SFT training.
Accordingly, at the end of this section, we briefly list the key lemmas and the corresponding induction for SFT, and omit the details.
Induction C.1 .
Given Ω ⁡ ( 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀 ​ d ) < ϵ < 1 4 \Omega(\frac{1}{\mathsf{polylog}d})<\epsilon<\frac{1}{4} , and let T 1 T_{1} be the first iteration such that
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon .
Then for every iteration t < T 1 t<T_{1} , the following statements hold:
1.
O ⁡ ( log ⁡ L ϵ ) ≥ q ( t ) ≥ 0 O\big(\log\frac{L}{\epsilon}\big)\geq q^{(t)}\geq 0 , and q ( t ) q^{(t)} is monotonically nondecreasing in t t (starting from 0 0 );
2.
| r ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 / d 𝗉 ) ​ q ( t ) |r^{(t)}|\leq O(1/d_{\mathsf{p}})q^{(t)} .
C.1 Attention and Logit Preliminaries
We first introduce several properties of the attention scores and logits if Induction C.1 holds.
Lemma C.1 .
If Induction C.1 holds for all iterations < t <t , then we have
1.
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) = e q ( t ) − r ( t ) e q ( t ) − r ( t ) + ( L − 1 ) ≥ 1 L \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}=\frac{e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}}{e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}+(L-1)}\geq\frac{1}{L} ;
2.
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ( t ) = 1 ( L − 1 ) + e q ( t ) − r ( t ) = 1 L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ) \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}=\frac{1}{(L-1)+e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}}=\frac{1}{L-1}\big(1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\big) for k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell .
Therefore, direct calculations by combining Lemma C.1 and Lemma A.7 yield the following lemma.
Lemma C.2 .
Assume that Induction C.1 holds for all iterations < t <t . We have
p L , 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) , 1 − p L , 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( 1 d ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B − 1 ) p^{(t)}_{L,1}\geq\Omega(1),\quad 1-p^{(t)}_{L,1}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-\epsilon)C_{B}-1}}\Big)
and the following bounds
on the transition probabilities p L , 2 ( t ) p^{(t)}_{L,2} and p L , 3 ( t ) p^{(t)}_{L,3} .
1.
Regime I: if 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) < 1 − L − 1 C B \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L}<1-\frac{L-1}{C_{B}} , then
(a)
in-context distractor transition
p L , 2 ( t ) = Θ ⁡ ( d − ( 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) − 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) L − 1 ) ​ C B ) = O ⁡ ( 1 L ) ​ ( 1 − p L , 1 ( t ) ) . p_{L,2}^{(t)}=\Theta\left(d^{-\left(\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}-\frac{1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}}{L-1}\right)C_{B}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{L}\right)\Big(1-p_{L,1}^{(t)}\Big).

vocabulary distractor transition

2.
Regime II: if 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − L − 1 C B \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L}\geq 1-\frac{L-1}{C_{B}} , then
(a)
in-context distractor transition
p L , 2 ( t ) = O ⁡ ( d − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B ) = O ⁡ ( 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − p L , 1 ( t ) ) . p_{L,2}^{(t)}=O\left(d^{-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)\Big(1-p_{L,1}^{(t)}\Big).
(b)
vocabulary distractor transition
p L , 3 ( t ) = O ⁡ ( d − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B ) = O ⁡ ( 1 d ) ​ ( 1 − p L , 1 ( t ) ) . p_{L,3}^{(t)}=O\left(d^{-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)\Big(1-p_{L,1}^{(t)}\Big).
C.2 Gradient Lemma
Since the initialization is uniform, the initial step-wise probabilities satisfy p L , 1 ( 0 ) = p L , 2 ( 0 ) p^{(0)}_{L,1}=p^{(0)}_{L,2} , so the gradient characterization in Proposition B.2 is not directly applicable. We therefore need finer control of the gradients at the very beginning of training.
Lemma C.3 .
Assume that Induction C.1 holds for all iterations < t <t , when 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) 1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\geq\Omega(1) , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ​ d 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}^{(t)}_{L}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}d^{\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}-1}}\Big).
Proof.
By ( 22 ), we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}
= 1 2 ​ L ​ d 𝗉 ​ 𝔼 Z L ​ [ 𝔍 ( t ) ​ ( y 0 , G L ) ] \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2Ld_{\mathsf{p}}}\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\left[\mathfrak{J}^{(t)}(y_{0},G^{L})\right]
= 1 2 ​ L ​ d 𝗉 𝔼 Z L [ ∑ 𝒗 ∈ 𝒴 L − 1 × { y L } ( ∏ ℓ ′ = 1 L π θ ( t ) ( v ℓ ′ ∣ v ℓ ′ − 1 , G L ) ) ( ∑ ℓ = 1 L ⋅ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , ℓ 𝔊 ℓ ( 𝒗 ) ) ] . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2Ld_{\mathsf{p}}}\mathbb{E}_{Z^{L}}\Bigg[\sum_{\bm{v}\in\mathcal{Y}^{L-1}\times\{y_{L}\}}\Bigg(\prod_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{L}\pi^{(t)}_{\theta}\big(v_{\ell^{\prime}}\mid v_{\ell^{\prime}-1},G^{L}\big)\Bigg)\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\cdot\mathbf{Attn}_{{a,\ell-1}\rightarrow p,\ell}\mathfrak{G}_{\ell}(\bm{v})\Big)\Bigg].
(45)
Therefore, in the following, we will divide the trajectory 𝒗 ∈ 𝒱 L − 1 × { y L } \bm{v}\in\mathcal{V}^{L-1}\times\{y_{L}\} into three different categories:
•
𝒗 = 𝒗 ∗ ≜ ( y 1 , ⋯ , y L ) \bm{v}=\bm{v}^{\ast}\triangleq(y_{1},\cdots,y_{L}) ,
by Lemma C.2 , we can obtain

∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}
