Lemma C.4 .
Assume that Induction C.1 holds for all iterations < t <t , when 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − L − 1 C B \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\geq 1-\frac{L-1}{C_{B}} , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}
= Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ​ d 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ​ C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle=\Theta\Big(\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}d^{\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}C_{B}-1}}\Big).
Proof.
By Lemma C.2 , when 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − L − 1 C B \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\geq 1-\frac{L-1}{C_{B}} , we have Δ L ( t ) , p L , 1 ( t ) = Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) \Delta_{L}^{(t)},p_{L,1}^{(t)}=\Omega(1) , and δ L ( t ) ≤ p L , 2 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 / d ) ​ ( 1 − Δ L ( t ) ) \delta_{L}^{(t)}\leq p_{L,2}^{(t)}\leq O(1/d)(1-\Delta_{L}^{(t)}) . Hence, the condition in Proposition B.2 holds, and we can directly apply it to complete the proof.
∎
Lemma C.5 .
Assume that Induction C.1 holds for all iterations < t <t .
Then,
| ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ​ | ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) | . \bigl|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}^{(t)}_{L}\bigr|\leq O\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\bigl|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}\bigr|.
Proof.
The claim follows from Lemma A.3 , we thus omit the details.
∎
C.3 Proof of Theorem 4.1
By combining the gradient bounds in Lemmas C.3 , C.4 and C.5 , we show that the induction
hypothesis Induction C.1 is maintained throughout this stage until q ( t ) q^{(t)} reaches the target
scale Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) ) \Omega\bigl(\log(L/\epsilon)\bigr) . At that point, we obtain 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L}\geq 1-\epsilon ,
which leads to the following lemma.
Lemma C.6 (End of Constant-Length Training) .
For any Ω ⁡ ( 1 / 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀 ​ d ) < ϵ < L − 1 C B \Omega(1/\mathsf{polylog}d)<\epsilon<\frac{L-1}{C_{B}} , the induction hypothesis Induction C.1 holds for all iterations
t < T 1 = O ⁡ ( d 𝗉 ​ d ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B − 1 ⋅ log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) η ​ log ⁡ d ) . t<T_{1}=O\left(d_{\mathsf{p}}d^{(1-\epsilon)C_{B}-1}\cdot\frac{\log(L/\epsilon)}{\eta\log d}\right).
Moreover, at t = T 1 t=T_{1} we have: q ( T 1 ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) ) q^{(T_{1})}\geq\Omega(\log(L/\epsilon)) ; | r ( T 1 ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ) ​ q ( T 1 ) \bigl|r^{(T_{1})}\bigr|\leq O\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)q^{(T_{1})} .
Proof.
Assume Induction C.1 holds up to iteration t t . Then Lemmas C.3 and C.4 imply that the
policy gradient for q q is strictly positive and satisfies a lower bound of the form
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B + 1 , \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-(1-\epsilon)C_{B}+1},
where we used that along this stage 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≤ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\leq 1-\epsilon . Under policy gradient update with step size η \eta , we therefore have the per-iteration increase
q ( t + 1 ) − q ( t ) = η ​ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) ≥ η ⋅ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B + 1 . q^{(t+1)}-q^{(t)}=\eta\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}\geq\eta\cdot\Omega\left(\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-(1-\epsilon)C_{B}+1}.
Summing over iterations until q ( t ) q^{(t)} reaches Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) ) \Omega(\log(L/\epsilon)) yields
T 1 = O ⁡ ( d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ⋅ log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) ⋅ d ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B − 1 ) , T_{1}=O\left(\frac{d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\cdot{\log(L/\epsilon)}\cdot d^{(1-\epsilon)C_{B}-1}\right),
as claimed.
Finally, Lemma C.5 gives
| ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ) ​ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L ( t ) |\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)}|\leq O(\frac{1}{d})\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L}^{(t)} throughout this stage, and hence
r ( t ) r^{(t)} remains slaved to q ( t ) q^{(t)} , i.e.,
| r ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ) ​ q ( t ) |r^{(t)}|\leq O(\frac{1}{d})q^{(t)} for all t ≤ T 1 t\leq T_{1} .
∎
Theorem 4.1 follows immediately from Lemma C.6 .
C.4 Proof of Theorem 4.2
The proof follows the same template as the RL case: we (i) set up an induction hypothesis controlling the key
parameters, (ii) derive lower/upper bounds on the relevant gradients under this hypothesis, and (iii) combine these bounds to upper bound the time needed for q ( t ) q^{(t)} to reach the target scale, at which point the
attention satisfies 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L}\geq 1-\epsilon . In other words, Theorem 4.2 is obtained by
assembling the lemmas below.
By comparing Lemma A.3 and Lemma A.4 , we observe that while the gradient forms are
structurally similar, the supervised analysis is more direct. This simplification arises because we only need
to track the ground-truth trajectory defined by 𝒗 = ( y 1 , … , y L ) \bm{v}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{L}) . Consequently, we can establish an
induction hypothesis analogous to Induction C.1 .
Induction C.2 .
For any length 2 ≤ L ≤ 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀 ​ d 2\leq L\leq\mathsf{polylog}d , fix any Ω ⁡ ( 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀 ​ d ) < ϵ < min ⁡ { 1 2 ​ ( 1 − 1 C B ) , L − 1 2 ​ L } \Omega(\frac{1}{\mathsf{polylog}d})<\epsilon<\min\{\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{C_{B}}),\frac{L-1}{2L}\} , and let T 1 T_{1} be the first iteration such that
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon .
Then for every iteration t < T 1 t<T_{1} , the following statements hold:
1.
O ⁡ ( log ⁡ L ϵ ) ≥ q ( t ) ≥ 0 O(\log\frac{L}{\epsilon})\geq q^{(t)}\geq 0 , and q ( t ) q^{(t)} is monotonically nondecreasing in t t (starting from 0 0 );
2.
| r ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 / d 𝗉 ) ​ q ( t ) |r^{(t)}|\leq O(1/d_{\mathsf{p}})q^{(t)} .
Building on this induction, we characterize the gradient dynamics through the following lemmas.
Lemma C.7 .
Assume that Induction C.2 holds for all iterations < t <t , then we have
•
if 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ≤ 1 C B \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\leq\frac{1}{C_{B}} , then
− ∇ q 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ​ L ) . \displaystyle-\nabla_{q}\mathsf{Loss}^{(t)}_{L}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}L}\Big).
•
else, we have
− ∇ q 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( ϵ ​ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ​ d ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle-\nabla_{q}\mathsf{Loss}^{(t)}_{L}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{\epsilon\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}d^{(1-\epsilon)C_{B}-1}}\Big).
Proof.
The claim follows from a similar analysis as ( 46 ) in the proof of Lemma C.3 , we thus omit the details.
∎
Lemma C.8 .
Assume that Induction C.2 holds for all iterations < t <t .
Then,
| ∇ r 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ​ | ∇ q 𝖫𝗈𝗌𝗌 L ( t ) | . \bigl|\nabla_{r}\mathsf{Loss}^{(t)}_{L}\bigr|\leq O\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\bigl|\nabla_{q}\mathsf{Loss}^{(t)}_{L}\bigr|.
By combining the results above, we obtain the total training time required to reach the target attention level:
Lemma C.9 (End of Training) .
For any constant length 2 ≤ L ≤ 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀 ​ d 2\leq L\leq\mathsf{polylog}d , fix any Ω ⁡ ( 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀 ​ d ) < ϵ < min ⁡ { 1 2 ​ ( 1 − 1 C B ) , L − 1 2 ​ L } \Omega(\frac{1}{\mathsf{polylog}d})<\epsilon<\min\{\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{C_{B}}),\frac{L-1}{2L}\} , the induction hypothesis Induction C.2 holds for all iterations
t < T 1 = O ⁡ ( d 𝗉 ​ d ( 1 − ϵ ) ​ C B − 1 ⋅ log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) η ​ ϵ ​ log ⁡ d + L ​ d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ) . t<T_{1}=O\left(d_{\mathsf{p}}d^{(1-\epsilon)C_{B}-1}\cdot\frac{\log(L/\epsilon)}{\eta\epsilon\log d}+\frac{Ld_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\right).
Moreover, at t = T 1 t=T_{1} we have: q ( T 1 ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) ) q^{(T_{1})}\geq\Omega(\log(L/\epsilon)) ; | r ( T 1 ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ) ​ q ( T 1 ) \bigl|r^{(T_{1})}\bigr|\leq O\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)q^{(T_{1})} .
Theorem 4.2 follows immediately from Lemma C.9 by noting that
q ( T 1 ) = Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( L / ϵ ) ) q^{(T_{1})}=\Omega(\log(L/\epsilon)) implies 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( T 1 ) ≥ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}^{(T_{1})}_{L}\geq 1-\epsilon (by the definition of T 1 T_{1} ) and the stated bound on T 1 T_{1} matches the claimed iteration complexity.
Appendix D Learning Dynamics of Mixed-difficulty RL
In this section, we study the mixed-difficulty setting, where tasks of different lengths are interleaved.
By combining the constant-length analysis in Appendix C with the gradient characterizations from Appendix B , we analyze two regimes of the difficulty ratio R R :
(i) the large difficulty ratio regime R = ω ⁡ ( 1 ) R=\omega(1) , which gives rise to grokking-style dynamics, and
(ii) the moderate difficulty ratio regime R = O ⁡ ( 1 ) R=O(1) , which leads to smoother relay dynamics.
We begin by reviewing the mixed-difficulty setup and introducing some timestamps that will be useful for characterizing the overall learning dynamics.
Mixed-difficulty setup.
Let R > 1 R>1 denote the difficulty ratio , and set the starting (effectively short) horizon to be L 1 := C B L_{1}:=C_{B} .
Define the horizon set ℒ R = { L 1 , L 2 , … , L K } \mathcal{L}_{R}=\{L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{K}\} recursively by
L k = min ⁡ { ⌈ R ​ L k − 1 ⌉ , L max } , 2 ≤ k ≤ K , L_{k}=\min\{\lceil RL_{k-1}\rceil,L_{\max}\},\qquad 2\leq k\leq K,
where K = ⌈ log R ⁡ ( L max / L 1 ) ⌉ K=\left\lceil\log_{R}(L_{\max}/L_{1})\right\rceil , so that L K = L max L_{K}=L_{\max} .
For simplicity, we focus on the case R ≥ 2 R\geq 2 throughout.
Mastery and visible return states.
For any L i ∈ ℒ R L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{R} , we say the horizon L i L_{i} has visible return at time t t if
𝒥 L i ( t ) ≥ 0.01 . \mathcal{J}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\geq 0.01.
(56)
Denote the first iteration such that L i L_{i} has visible return as T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i T_{\mathsf{vis},i} .
We say the horizon L i L_{i} is mastered at time t t if
𝒥 L i ( t ) ≥ 0.99 . \mathcal{J}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\geq 0.99.
(57)
Denote the first iteration such that L i L_{i} is mastered as T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i T_{\mathsf{mas},i} .
Plateau between consecutive horizons.
For k ∈ { 1 , … , K − 1 } k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\} , define
𝒯 k ≜ T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k = | { t | 𝒥 L k ( t ) ≥ 0.99 , 𝒥 L k + 1 ( t ) < 0.01 } | . {\mathcal{T}}_{k}\triangleq T_{\mathsf{vis},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},k}=\left|\left\{t\middle|\mathcal{J}_{L_{k}}^{(t)}\geq 0.99,\ \mathcal{J}_{L_{k+1}}^{(t)}<0.01\right\}\right|.
(58)
In words, 𝒯 k {\mathcal{T}}_{k} counts the number of iterations during which L k L_{k} is already mastered
while L k + 1 L_{k+1} has not yet achieved a visible return.
D.1 Analysis of Large Difficulty Gap Regime
In this subsection, we analyze the large difficulty ratio regime, where R = ω ⁡ ( 1 ) R=\omega(1) . Following the similar proof strategy as in Appendix C , we start with the induction hypothesis that is expected to hold through the training process.
Induction D.1 .
Given Ω ⁡ ( 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 ​ log ⁡ d ) < ϵ < 1 4 \Omega\big(\frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}\log d}\big)<\epsilon<\frac{1}{4} , and let T ⋆ T^{\star} be the first iteration such that 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L max ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}_{L_{\max}}^{(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon . Then, for all iterations t < T ⋆ t<T^{\star} , we have the following holds:
1.
0 ≤ q ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ L max ϵ ) 0\leq q^{(t)}\leq O\Big(\log\frac{L_{\max}}{\epsilon}\Big) , and q ( t ) q^{(t)} monotonically increases.
2.
| r ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 / d 𝗉 ) ​ q ( t ) |r^{(t)}|\leq O(1/d_{\mathsf{p}})q^{(t)} .
D.1.1 Properties of the Attention Scores and Critical Thresholds
We record some properties of the attention scores and critical thresholds.
Lemma D.1 .
If Induction D.1 holds for all iterations < t <t , then we have
1.
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) = e q ( t ) − r ( t ) e q ( t ) − r ( t ) + ( L − 1 ) ≥ 1 L \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}=\frac{e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}}{e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}+(L-1)}\geq\frac{1}{L} ;
2.
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ( t ) = 1 ( L − 1 ) + e q ( t ) − r ( t ) = 1 L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ) \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}=\frac{1}{(L-1)+e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}}=\frac{1}{L-1}\big(1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\big) for k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell .
Lemma D.2 (Critical threshold of q q ) .
If Induction D.1 holds, then given L ∈ ℒ R L\in\mathcal{L}_{R} ,
the critical threshold of q q required to satisfy 𝒥 L ≥ 1 − ξ \mathcal{J}_{L}\geq 1-\xi for some constant 0 < ξ ≤ 1 0<\xi\leq 1 is given by:
q \displaystyle q
≥ log ⁡ L − 1 C B − 1 + f ⁡ ( log ⁡ L − log ⁡ log ⁡ 1 1 − ξ log ⁡ d ) \displaystyle\geq\log\frac{L-1}{C_{B}-1}+f\Big(\frac{\log L-\log\log\frac{1}{1-\xi}}{\log d}\Big)
≥ log ⁡ L − 1 C B − 1 + C B C B − 1 ⋅ log ⁡ L − log ⁡ log ⁡ 1 1 − ξ log ⁡ d + 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ​ L log 2 ​ d ) . \displaystyle\geq\log\frac{L-1}{C_{B}-1}+\frac{C_{B}}{C_{B}-1}\cdot\frac{\log L-\log\log\frac{1}{1-\xi}}{\log d}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log^{2}L}{\log^{2}d}\right).
where f ⁡ ( x ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + x 1 − x / ( C B − 1 ) ) f(x)=\log\Big(\frac{1+x}{1-x/(C_B-1)}\Big) .
Similarly, the critical threshold of q q required to satisfy 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ≥ 1 − ξ \mathbf{Attn}_{L}\geq 1-\xi for any 0 < ξ ≤ 1 0<\xi\leq 1 is given by:
q ≥ log ⁡ ( 1 − ξ ) ​ ( L − 1 ) ξ . \displaystyle q\geq\log\frac{(1-\xi)(L-1)}{\xi}.
Proof.
Given 𝒥 L ≥ 1 − ξ \mathcal{J}_{L}\geq 1-\xi , by Lemma B.5 , we have
Δ L ≥ ( 1 − ξ ) 1 / L = 1 − − log ⁡ ( 1 − ξ ) L . \displaystyle\Delta_{L}\geq(1-\xi)^{1/L}=1-\frac{-\log(1-\xi)}{L}.
Then, by Lemma A.7 , we can derive that
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ≥ 1 C B + log ⁡ L − log ⁡ ( − log ⁡ ( 1 − ξ ) ) C B ​ log ⁡ d . \mathbf{Attn}_{L}\geq\frac{1}{C_{B}}+\frac{\log L-\log(-\log(1-\xi))}{C_{B}\log d}.
Hence, applying Lemma D.1 , we have
q ≥ log ⁡ ( L − 1 ) + log ⁡ ( 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ) ≥ log ⁡ L − 1 C B − 1 + f ⁡ ( log ⁡ L − log ⁡ log ⁡ 1 1 − ξ log ⁡ d ) . \displaystyle q\geq\log(L-1)+\log\Big(\frac{\attn_L}{1-\attn_L}\Big)\geq\log\frac{L-1}{C_{B}-1}+f\left(\frac{\log L-\log\log\frac{1}{1-\xi}}{\log d}\right).
Here, we then use the first-order Taylor expansion for f ⁡ ( x ) f(x) to get the second inequality.
∎
Notice that in the large difficulty ratio regime, the changes in log ⁡ L \log L between two consecutive horizons are Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ R ) ≫ 1 \Omega(\log R)\gg 1 , which is much larger than the log ⁡ L log ⁡ d ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 ) \frac{\log L}{\log d}\leq O(1) term. Therefore, the above lemma implies that the change in q q between two consecutive horizons is dominated by Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ R ) \Omega(\log R) .
D.1.2 Warm-up Stage for L 1 L_{1}
We define the warm-up stage as the period during which the starting horizon L 1 L_{1}
reaches the mastery state, namely 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1} .
At initialization, the attention scores are essentially uniform across horizons.
We will show that, during this stage, the only non-negligible gradient contribution
comes from the effectively short horizon L 1 L_{1} .
We first record several basic properties of q q , r r , and the attention scores
throughout the warm-up stage.
Lemma D.3 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1} :
1.
0 ≤ q ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( L 1 log ⁡ d ) 0\leq q^{(t)}\leq O\left(\frac{L_{1}}{\log d}\right) , and q ( t ) q^{(t)} is monotonically increasing in t t .
2.
| r ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ​ q ( t ) |r^{(t)}|\leq O\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)q^{(t)} .
Proof.
The range of q ( t ) q^{(t)} is a direct consequence of Lemma D.2 . The monotonicity of q ( t ) q^{(t)} and the bound on r ( t ) r^{(t)} follow directly from
Induction D.1 .
∎
Lemma D.4 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1}
and for any horizon L i L_{i} with i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 , we have
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 𝖺𝗇𝗌 , ℓ − 1 → k ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 L i ) = o ⁡ ( 1 ) , ∀ ℓ ∈ [ L i ] , k ∈ [ ℓ ] . \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{\mathsf{ans},\ell-1\to k}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{L_{i}}\right)=o(1),\qquad\forall\ell\in[L_{i}],k\in[\ell].
Proof.
This follows directly from Lemma D.1 .
Moreover, since R = ω ⁡ ( 1 ) R=\omega(1) in this regime, we have L i ≥ ω ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}\geq\omega(1) for all i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 ,
so the bound is indeed o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) as d d grows.
∎
Combining the above with the same reasoning as in Proposition B.4 ,
we can verify that the condition ( 43 ) in Proposition B.3
holds for all longer horizons during warm-up, which yields the following.
Lemma D.5 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1}
and for any horizon L i L_{i} with i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 , we have
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L i ) , | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 2 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L i ) . \big|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L_{i})},\qquad\big|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L_{i})}.
Compared with Lemmas C.3 and C.4 in Appendix C ,
Lemma D.5 shows that during warm-up, the gradients contributed by
longer horizons L i L_{i} (for i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 ) are negligible relative to the shortest horizon L 1 = C B L_{1}=C_{B} .
Therefore, we can apply the constant-length analysis from Appendix C
to the warm-up stage for L 1 L_{1} , which yields the following characterization at the end of warm-up.
Lemma D.6 .
Induction D.1 holds through 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1} with
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 = O ⁡ ( K ​ L max ​ L 1 η ​ log 2 ​ d ) , T_{\mathsf{mas},1}=O\left(\frac{KL_{\max}L_{1}}{\eta\log^{2}d}\right),
and at time T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},1} we have
q ( T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ L 1 log ⁡ d ) q^{(T_{\mathsf{mas},1})}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\log L_{1}}{\log d}\right) .
D.1.3 Transition Between Mastery States
Since we have established that the initial horizon can reach the mastery state,
we next analyze how mastery propagates across consecutive horizons.
Specifically, we study the transition from horizon i i to horizon i + 1 i+1
over the time interval [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i},\,T_{\mathsf{mas},i+1}) .
Recall the definition K = ⌈ log R ⁡ ( L max / L 1 ) ⌉ K=\left\lceil\log_{R}\big(L_{\max}/L_{1}\big)\right\rceil . By construction, the horizons grow by a factor R R up to index K − 1 K-1 ,
while the last step may be truncated so that L K = L max L_{K}=L_{\max} ; consequently, L K / L K − 1 L_{K}/L_{K-1} is not necessarily equal to R R . For notational convenience, we therefore restrict attention to i ∈ { 1 , … , K − 2 } i\in\{1,\ldots,K-2\} ,
and fix an arbitrary i ⋆ ∈ { 1 , … , K − 2 } i^{\star}\in\{1,\ldots,K-2\}
for the remainder of the analysis. Moreover, we absorb the gradient term ∇ q 𝒥 L K \nabla_{q}\mathcal{J}_{L_{K}} into ∇ q 𝒥 L K − 1 \nabla_{q}\mathcal{J}_{L_{K-1}} ,
since for all times prior to T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1} , ∇ q 𝒥 L K \nabla_{q}\mathcal{J}_{L_{K}} can be upper bounded by ∇ q 𝒥 L K − 1 \nabla_{q}\mathcal{J}_{L_{K-1}} .
By the critical threshold of q q in Lemma D.2 , we have the following characterization of the attention scores:
Lemma D.7 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} :
1.
if i ⋆ > 1 i^{\star}>1 , then for any i < i ⋆ i<i^{\star} , we have
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≥ 1 − O ⁡ ( 1 R i ⋆ − i ) ⋅ ( 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ⋆ ( t ) ) = 1 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\geq 1-O\Big(\frac{1}{R^{i^{\star}-i}}\Big)\cdot(1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)})=1-o(1).
2.
for i = i ⋆ i=i^{\star} , we have
1 C B + Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ L i log ⁡ d ) < 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≤ 1 − Ω ⁡ ( 1 R ) . \displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{B}}+\Omega\Big(\frac{\log L_{i}}{\log d}\Big)<\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\leq 1-\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{R}\Big).
3.
if i ⋆ < K − 2 i^{\star}<K-2 , then for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we have
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 L i ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{L_{i}}\right).
This immediately implies the following characterization of the logits:
Lemma D.8 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} :
1.
if i ⋆ > 1 i^{\star}>1 , then for any i < i ⋆ i<i^{\star} , we have ( p L i , 1 ( t ) ) L i ≥ Ω ⁡ ( 𝒥 L i ⋆ ( t ) ) = Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) (p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)})^{L_{i}}\geq\Omega(\mathcal{J}^{(t)}_{L_{i^{\star}}})=\Omega(1) , and also
Ω ⁡ ( 1 d C B − 1 ) ≤ 1 − p L i , 1 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ( 1 − e − q ( t ) ​ R − ( i ⋆ − i ) ​ L i ⋆ ) ​ C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{d^{C_{B}-1}}\Big)\leq 1-p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}\leq O\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-{e^{-q^{(t)}}R^{-(i^{\star}-i)}L_{i^{\star}}})C_{B}-1}}\Big).
2.
for i = i ⋆ i=i^{\star} , we have
1 − p L i , 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( 1 d ( 1 − Θ ⁡ ( e − q ( t ) ​ L i ) ) ​ C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle 1-p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-\Theta({e^{-q^{(t)}}L_{i}}))C_{B}-1}}\Big).
3.
if i ⋆ < K − 2 i^{\star}<K-2 , then for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we have
p L i , 1 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ) . \displaystyle p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{d}\right).
The logit conditions imply that for any i < i ⋆ i<i^{\star} , we can invoke the gradient characterization in Proposition B.2 , and for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we can invoke the gradient characterization in Proposition B.3 . Therefore, we have the following characterization of the gradient:
Lemma D.9 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} ,
1.
if i ⋆ > 1 i^{\star}>1 , then for any i < i ⋆ i<i^{\star} , we have
Ω ⁡ ( 1 d C B − 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ≤ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ( 1 − L i ⋆ e q ( t ) ​ R i ⋆ − i ) ​ C B − 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 \displaystyle\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{d^{C_{B}-1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\leq\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\leq O\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-\frac{L_{i^{\star}}}{e^{q^{(t)}}R^{i^{\star}-i}})C_{B}-1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}
2.
for i = i ⋆ i=i^{\star} , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) = Ω ⁡ ( 1 d ( 1 − Θ ⁡ ( e − q ( t ) ​ L i ) ) ​ C B − 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}=\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-\Theta({e^{-q^{(t)}}L_{i}}))C_{B}-1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}
3.
if i ⋆ < K − 2 i^{\star}<K-2 , then for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we have
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L i ) . \displaystyle|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\Big)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L_{i})}.
Lemma D.9 immediately implies a gradient lower bound for 𝒥 mix , R \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{mix},R} during [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}) :
Lemma D.10 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ mix , R ( t ) ≥ log ⁡ d K ​ d 𝗉 ​ Ω ​ ( i ⋆ d C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}^{(t)}\geq\frac{\log d}{Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}\Omega\Big(\frac{i^{\star}}{d^{C_{B}-1}}\Big).
Proof.
By Lemma D.9 , when R ≤ o ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) R\leq o(\log d) , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) / ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( d 1 / R ) = O ⁡ ( e log ⁡ d / R ) ≫ 1 . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}/\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\leq O\big(d^{1/R}\big)=O(e^{\log d/R})\gg 1.
Thus ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)} dominates the gradient of short horizons, which leads to the following lower bound:
∇ q 𝒥 ~ mix , R ( t ) ≥ log ⁡ d K ​ d 𝗉 ​ Ω ​ ( 1 d ( 1 − 1 R ) ​ C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}^{(t)}\geq\frac{\log d}{Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-\frac{1}{R})C_{B}-1}}\Big).
On the other hand, we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ mix , R ( t ) ≥ 1 K ​ ∑ i = 1 i ⋆ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( i ⋆ ​ log ⁡ d K ​ d 𝗉 ​ d C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}^{(t)}\geq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{i^{\star}}\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{i^{\star}\log d}{Kd_{\mathsf{p}}d^{C_{B}-1}}\Big).
Further noting that i ⋆ ≤ K − 2 ≤ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) i^{\star}\leq K-2\leq O(\log d) , thus when R ≤ o ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) R\leq o(\log d) , we have d C B R ≥ i ⋆ d^{\frac{C_{B}}{R}}\geq i^{\star} , which implies that in both cases, we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ mix , R ( t ) ≥ log ⁡ d K ​ d 𝗉 ​ Ω ​ ( i ⋆ d C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}^{(t)}\geq\frac{\log d}{Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}\Omega\Big(\frac{i^{\star}}{d^{C_{B}-1}}\Big).
∎
So far, we have already controlled the gradient for the horizons before or after the current consecutive mastery state. In the following, we are going to exam ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) + ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}+\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}^{(t)} .
Lemma D.11 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) + ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d L i ⋆ + 1 ​ d 𝗉 ) \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}+\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{\log d}{L_{i^{\star}+1}d_{\mathsf{p}}}\Big)
Proof.
By the critical threshold of q q in Lemma D.2 , when t ≥ T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 t\geq T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1} , we have p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ≥ 1 − O ⁡ ( 1 L i ⋆ + 1 ) p_{L_{i^{\star}}+1,1}^{(t)}\geq 1-O(\frac{1}{L_{i^{\star}+1}}) . Hence, the conditions of Proposition B.2 are satisfied, and invoking it, we then obtain
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ) ​ ( 1 − p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}+1}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\Big)(1-p_{L_{i^{\star}}+1,1}^{(t)}).
On the other hand, since t ≤ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 t\leq T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} , again by Lemma D.2 , we have p L i ⋆ , 1 ( t ) ≤ 1 − Ω ⁡ ( 1 L i ⋆ ) p_{L_{i^{\star}},1}^{(t)}\leq 1-\Omega(\frac{1}{L_{i^{\star}}}) . Thus, we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d L i ⋆ + 1 ​ d 𝗉 ) , \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}+1}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{\log d}{L_{i^{\star}+1}d_{\mathsf{p}}}\Big),
which completes the proof.
∎
In the following, we are going to show that during the time period [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}) , there exists a major period during which the gradient is dominated by the current mastery state L i ⋆ L_{i^{\star}} .
Lemma D.12 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then during [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}) , when
q ( t ) ∈ [ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ R 0.01 ​ L i ⋆ ) , O ⁡ ( log ⁡ R 0.99 ​ L i ⋆ ) ] \displaystyle q^{(t)}\in[\Omega(\log R^{0.01}L_{i^{\star}}),\,O(\log R^{0.99}L_{i^{\star}})]
(59)
we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) + ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) = ( 1 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}+\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}^{(t)}=(1+o(1))\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}.
Moreover,
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) = Θ ⁡ ( 1 d ( 1 − e − q ( t ) ​ L i ⋆ ) ​ C B − 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}=\Theta\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-e^{-q^{(t)}}L_{i^{\star}})C_{B}-1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}.
Proof.
By the critical threshold of q q in Lemma D.2 ,
Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ L i ⋆ ) ≤ q ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ R ​ L i ⋆ ) = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ L i ⋆ + 1 ) . \Omega(\log L_{i^{\star}})\leq q^{(t)}\leq O(\log RL_{i^{\star}})=O(\log L_{i^{\star}+1}).
So the condition ( 59 ) is well-defined. Furthermore, by Lemma D.2 , when ( 59 ) holds, we have p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d ) p_{L_{i^{\star}}+1,1}^{(t)}\leq O(\frac{1}{d}) . Hence applying Proposition B.3 , and we have
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L i ⋆ + 1 ) . \displaystyle\big|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}+1}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\Big)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L_{i^{\star}+1})}.
Furthermore, ( 59 ) combined with Lemma D.2 implies that
1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ⋆ ( t ) = Θ ⁡ ( e − q ( t ) ​ L i ⋆ ) . \displaystyle 1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}=\Theta(e^{-q^{(t)}}L_{i^{\star}}).
Hence,
1 − p L i ⋆ , 1 ( t ) = Θ ⁡ ( 1 d ( 1 − e − q ( t ) ​ L i ⋆ ) ​ C B − 1 ) . \displaystyle 1-p_{L_{i^{\star}},1}^{(t)}=\Theta\Big(\frac{1}{d^{(1-e^{-q^{(t)}}L_{i^{\star}})C_{B}-1}}\Big).
Therefore, invoking Proposition B.2 , we complete the proof.
∎
Putting everything together, we can then characterize the grokking-style behaviour happening during the transition period [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}) .
Lemma D.13 .
Induction D.1 holds through [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}) , where T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 = T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + O ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 ​ log ⁡ R η ​ i ⋆ ​ log ⁡ d ) T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}=T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}+{O}\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}\log R}{\eta i^{\star}\log d}\Big)
1.
the reward of J L i ⋆ + 1 J_{L_{i^{\star}+1}} saturates below 0.01 0.01 for a time period of
𝒯 k ≥ Ω ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 i ⋆ ​ η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ log ⁡ R 1 + C B ​ R − 0.01 ​ log ⁡ d . \displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{k}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}{i^{\star}\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log R}{1+C_{B}R^{-0.01}\log d}.
2.
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 − T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ≤ O ⁡ ( L i ⋆ + 1 ​ d 𝗉 ​ K η ​ log ⁡ d ) T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}-T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}\leq O(\frac{L_{i^{\star}+1}d_{\mathsf{p}}K}{\eta\log d}) .
Proof.
The existence of T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 = T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + O ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 ​ log ⁡ R η ​ i ⋆ ​ log ⁡ d ) T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}=T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}+{O}\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}\log R}{\eta i^{\star}\log d}\Big) is guaranteed by the gradient lower bound in Lemma D.10 . Moreover, the second item is guaranteed by the gradient lower bound in Lemma D.11 . Then we focus on the first statement. We approximate the total number of iterations 𝒯 i ⋆ {\mathcal{T}}_{i^{\star}} by the integral
𝒯 i ⋆ ≳ ∫ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ R 0.01 ​ L i ⋆ ) O ⁡ ( log ⁡ R 0.99 ​ L i ⋆ ) d ​ q η ​ ∇ q ​ 𝒥 ~ mix , R . \displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{i^{\star}}\gtrsim\int_{\Omega(\log R^{0.01}L_{i^{\star}})}^{O(\log R^{0.99}L_{i^{\star}})}\frac{dq}{\eta\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}}.
By Lemma D.12 , we can have a naive upper bound on the gradient:
∇ q 𝒥 ~ mix , R ≤ i ⋆ K ⋅ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( i ⋆ ​ log ⁡ d K ​ d 𝗉 ) ⋅ 1 d ( 1 − e − q ( t ) ​ L i ⋆ ) ​ C B − 1 . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{mix},R}\leq\frac{i^{\star}}{K}\cdot\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}\leq O\Big(\frac{i^{\star}\log d}{Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{d^{(1-e^{-q^{(t)}}L_{i^{\star}})C_{B}-1}}.
Plugging this into the integral, we have
𝒯 i ⋆ \displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{i^{\star}}
≥ Ω ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 i ⋆ ​ η ​ log ⁡ d ) ​ ∫ log ⁡ R 0.01 log ⁡ R 0.99 d − C B ​ e − q ​ 𝑑 q = Ω ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 i ⋆ ​ η ​ log ⁡ d ) ​ ∫ R − 0.99 R − 0.01 e − ( C B ​ log ⁡ d ) ​ u u ​ 𝑑 u \displaystyle\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}{i^{\star}\eta\log d}\Big)\int_{\log R^{0.01}}^{\log R^{0.99}}d^{-C_{B}e^{-q}}dq=\Omega\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}{i^{\star}\eta\log d}\Big)\int_{R^{-0.99}}^{R^{-0.01}}\frac{e^{-(C_{B}\log d)u}}{u}du
≥ Ω ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 i ⋆ ​ η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ e − ( C B ​ R − 0.01 ​ log ⁡ d ) ∫ R − 0.99 R − 0.01 1 u d u \displaystyle\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}{i^{\star}\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot e^{-(C_{B}R^{-0.01}\log d)}\int_{R^{-0.99}}^{R^{-0.01}}\frac{1}{u}du
≥ Ω ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 i ⋆ ​ η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ e − ( C B ​ R − 0.01 ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ log ⁡ R \displaystyle\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}{i^{\star}\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot e^{-(C_{B}R^{-0.01}\log d)}\cdot\log R
≥ Ω ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 i ⋆ ​ η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ log ⁡ R 1 + C B ​ R − 0.01 ​ log ⁡ d , \displaystyle\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}{i^{\star}\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log R}{1+C_{B}R^{-0.01}\log d},
where we use the Taylor expansion for the last inequality.
∎
D.1.4 Proof of Theorem 5.1 and Corollary 5.1
Proof.
For any k ∈ { 1 , … , K − 2 } k\in\{1,\ldots,K-2\} , Lemma D.13 implies
𝒯 k \displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{k}
≥ Ω ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 k ​ η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ log ⁡ R 1 + C B ​ R − 0.01 ​ log ⁡ d \displaystyle\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}\,K\,d_{\mathsf{p}}}{k\,\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log R}{1+C_{B}R^{-0.01}\log d}
≥ Ω ~ ​ ( d C B − 1 ​ d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ) , \displaystyle\geq\widetilde{\Omega}\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}\,d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\Big),
where the last inequality uses
log ⁡ R 1 + C B ​ R − 0.01 ​ log ⁡ d = Ω ⁡ ( 1 / log ⁡ d ) \frac{\log R}{1+C_{B}R^{-0.01}\log d}=\Omega(1/\log d)
and K / k = Ω ~ ​ ( 1 ) K/k=\widetilde{\Omega}(1) .
Moreover,
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , k + 1 ≤ O ⁡ ( L k + 1 ​ d 𝗉 ​ K η ​ log ⁡ d ) ≤ O ⁡ ( d 𝗉 2 ​ K η ​ log ⁡ d ) ≪ 𝒯 k , T_{\mathsf{mas},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{vis},k+1}\leq O\Big(\frac{L_{k+1}\,d_{\mathsf{p}}\,K}{\eta\log d}\Big)\leq O\Big(\frac{d_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}\,K}{\eta\log d}\Big)\ll{\mathcal{T}}_{k},
since d 𝗉 = d c x d_{\mathsf{p}}=d^{c_{x}} and c x < C B − 1 c_{x}<C_{B}-1 .
Therefore,
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k ≥ Ω ~ ​ ( d C B − 1 ​ d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ) . \displaystyle T_{\mathsf{mas},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},k}\geq\widetilde{\Omega}\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}\,d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\Big).
On the other hand, Lemma D.13 also gives the matching upper bound
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k + 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , k ≤ O ~ ​ ( d C B − 1 ​ d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ) . \displaystyle T_{\mathsf{mas},k+1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},k}\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}\,d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\Big).
Summing over k ∈ { 1 , … , K − 2 } k\in\{1,\ldots,K-2\} , we obtain
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 = Θ ~ ​ ( d C B − 1 ​ d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ) . \displaystyle T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},1}=\widetilde{\Theta}\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}\,d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\Big).
Finally, by Lemma D.6 , the time spent in the warm-up stage
is negligible compared to T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},1} .
Moreover, the final step can be bounded as
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 ≤ O ⁡ ( T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 2 ) . T_{\mathsf{mas},K}-T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}\leq O\big(T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},K-2}\big).
This completes the proof.
∎
D.2 Analysis of Moderate Difficulty Ratio Regime
In this subsection, we analyze the moderate difficulty ratio regime, where 2 ≤ R = O ⁡ ( 1 ) 2\leq R=O(1) . Our overall proof strategy follows that of the large difficulty ratio regime, with several adjustments to account for the smaller gap between two consecutive scales.
We begin by stating the induction hypothesis, which we expect to remain valid throughout training.
Induction D.2 .
Given Ω ⁡ ( 1 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 ​ log ⁡ d ) < ϵ < 1 4 \Omega\big(\frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}\log d}\big)<\epsilon<\frac{1}{4} , and let T ⋆ T^{\star} be the first iteration such that 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L max ( t ) ≥ 1 − ϵ \mathbf{Attn}_{L_{\max}}^{(t)}\geq 1-\epsilon . Then, for all iterations t < T ⋆ t<T^{\star} , we have the following holds:
1.
0 ≤ q ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( log ⁡ L max ϵ ) 0\leq q^{(t)}\leq O\Big(\log\frac{L_{\max}}{\epsilon}\Big) , and q ( t ) q^{(t)} monotonically increases.
2.
| r ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 / d 𝗉 ) ​ q ( t ) |r^{(t)}|\leq O(1/d_{\mathsf{p}})q^{(t)} .
D.2.1 Properties of the Attention Scores and Critical Thresholds
We record several basic properties of the attention scores and the critical thresholds.
Lemma D.14 .
If Induction D.2 holds for all iterations < t <t , then:
1.
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) = e q ( t ) − r ( t ) e q ( t ) − r ( t ) + ( L − 1 ) ≥ 1 L ; \mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}=\frac{e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}}{e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}+(L-1)}\ \geq\ \frac{1}{L};
2.
for any k ≠ ℓ k\neq\ell ,
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 a , ℓ − 1 → p , k ( t ) = 1 ( L − 1 ) + e q ( t ) − r ( t ) = 1 L − 1 ​ ( 1 − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ( t ) ) . \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{a,\ell-1\to p,k}=\frac{1}{(L-1)+e^{q^{(t)}-r^{(t)}}}=\frac{1}{L-1}\big(1-\mathbf{Attn}_{L}^{(t)}\big).
Lemma D.15 (Critical threshold of q q ) .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for any L ∈ ℒ R L\in\mathcal{L}_{R} ,
a sufficient threshold on q q for 𝒥 L ≥ 1 − ξ \mathcal{J}_{L}\geq 1-\xi ,
where 0 < ξ ≤ 1 0<\xi\leq 1 is a constant, is
q ≥ log ⁡ L − 1 C B − 1 + f ⁡ ( log ⁡ L − log ⁡ log ⁡ 1 1 − ξ log ⁡ d ) , \displaystyle q\ \geq\ \log\frac{L-1}{C_{B}-1}+f\Big(\frac{\log L-\log\log\frac{1}{1-\xi}}{\log d}\Big),
where f ⁡ ( x ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + x 1 − x / ( C B − 1 ) ) f(x)=\log\Big(\frac{1+x}{1-x/(C_B-1)}\Big) .
Similarly, a sufficient threshold on q q for 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L ≥ 1 − ξ \mathbf{Attn}_{L}\geq 1-\xi is
q ≥ log ⁡ ( 1 − ξ ) ​ ( L − 1 ) ξ . q\ \geq\ \log\frac{(1-\xi)(L-1)}{\xi}.
The above lemmas mirror their counterparts in the large-difficulty regime.
However, to track the variation of the critical threshold when L L increases
only by a constant factor R R , we need a more careful comparison than the
coarse Taylor-expansion argument used for widely separated scales.
Lemma D.16 .
If Induction D.2 holds and 0 < ξ ≤ 1 0<\xi\leq 1 is a constant,
let q ξ ​ ( L ) q_{\xi}(L) denote the critical threshold of q q required to ensure
𝒥 L , 1 ≥ 1 − ξ \mathcal{J}_{L,1}\geq 1-\xi .
Then for any L k , L k + 1 ∈ ℒ R L_{k},L_{k+1}\in\mathcal{L}_{R} ,
q ξ ​ ( L k + 1 ) − q ξ ​ ( L k ) = log ⁡ R ⋅ ( 1 + O ⁡ ( 1 / log ⁡ d ) ) . q_{\xi}(L_{k+1})-q_{\xi}(L_{k})=\log R\cdot\Big(1+O(1/\log d)\Big).
Proof.
By Lemma D.15 , we have
d ​ q ξ ​ ( L ) d ​ L = 1 L ​ [ 1 + 1 log ⁡ d ⋅ C B ( 1 + X ⁡ ( L ) ) ​ ( C B − 1 − X ⁡ ( L ) ) ] , \frac{dq_{\xi}(L)}{dL}=\frac{1}{L}\left[1+\frac{1}{\log d}\cdot\frac{C_{B}}{(1+X(L))(C_{B}-1-X(L))}\right],
where X ⁡ ( L ) = log ⁡ L − log ⁡ log ⁡ 1 1 − ξ log ⁡ d X(L)=\frac{\log L-\log\log\frac{1}{1-\xi}}{\log d} .
Since 0 ≤ X ⁡ ( L ) ≤ 1 + O ⁡ ( 1 / log ⁡ d ) 0\leq X(L)\leq 1+O(1/\log d) , it follows that
d ​ q ξ ​ ( L ) d ​ L = 1 L ​ ( 1 + O ⁡ ( 1 / log ⁡ d ) ) \frac{dq_{\xi}(L)}{dL}=\frac{1}{L}\big(1+O(1/\log d)\big) .
Therefore,
q ξ ​ ( L k + 1 ) − q ξ ​ ( L k ) \displaystyle q_{\xi}(L_{k+1})-q_{\xi}(L_{k})
= ∫ L k L k + 1 1 L ​ ( 1 + O ⁡ ( 1 / log ⁡ d ) ) ​ 𝑑 L \displaystyle=\int_{L_{k}}^{L_{k+1}}\frac{1}{L}\big(1+O(1/\log d)\big)\,dL
= log ⁡ R ⋅ ( 1 + O ⁡ ( 1 / log ⁡ d ) ) . \displaystyle=\log R\cdot\Big(1+O(1/\log d)\Big).
∎
D.2.2 Warm-up Stage for L 1 L_{1}
We define the warm-up stage as the period during which the starting horizon L 1 L_{1}
reaches the mastery state, namely 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1} . The analysis is similar to the large difficulty ratio regime, but with some modifications since L i L_{i} with i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 could be relatively small and still at the constant-length regime.
Lemma D.17 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then for all iterations 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1} :
1.
0 ≤ q ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( L 1 log ⁡ d ) 0\leq q^{(t)}\leq O\left(\frac{L_{1}}{\log d}\right) , and q ( t ) q^{(t)} is monotonically increasing in t t .
2.
| r ( t ) | ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ​ q ( t ) |r^{(t)}|\leq O\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)q^{(t)} .
Lemma D.18 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then for all iterations 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1} , and for any L i ∈ ℒ R L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{R} with i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 , we have
1.
if L i = O ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}=O(1) , then 1 − C B ​ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≥ 1 − 1 0.99 ​ R i − 1 + 1 1-C_{B}\mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L_{i}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{0.99R^{i-1}+1} ;
2.
else, 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 L i ) = o ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L_{i}}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{L_{i}}\right)=o(1) .
Proof.
The second item is similar to the large difficulty ratio regime. For the first item, by Lemma D.17 , we have
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≤ 1 ( L i − 1 ) ⋅ e − O ⁡ ( L 1 / log ⁡ d ) + 1 = 1 R i − 1 ​ C B ⋅ e − O ⁡ ( L 1 / log ⁡ d ) + 1 . \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L_{i}}\leq\frac{1}{(L_{i}-1)\cdot e^{-O(L_{1}/\log d)}+1}=\frac{1}{R^{i-1}C_{B}\cdot e^{-O(L_{1}/\log d)}+1}.
Hence,
1 − C B ​ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≥ 1 − 1 0.99 ​ R i − 1 + 1 . \displaystyle 1-C_{B}\mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L_{i}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{0.99R^{i-1}+1}.
∎
Similarly as Lemma D.5 , the condition ( 43 ) in Proposition B.3 holds for L i = ω ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}=\omega(1) , which yields the following.
Lemma D.19 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then for all iterations 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1}
and for any horizon L i = ω ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}=\omega(1) , we have
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L i ) , | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 2 ) ⋅ d − Ω ⁡ ( L i ) . \big|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L_{i})},\qquad\big|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}}\right)\cdot d^{-\Omega(L_{i})}.
Lemma D.18 shows that even some longer horizons L i L_{i} are still at the constant-length regime, its target attention scores are still below 1 C B \frac{1}{C_{B}} , which means p L i , 1 ( t ) ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) p^{(t)}_{L_{i},1}\leq d^{-\Omega(1)} is still close to 0 0 . However, directly applying Proposition B.3 to L i = O ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}=O(1) only gives a bound of d − Ω ⁡ ( L i ) d^{-\Omega(L_{i})} , which may be too loose in the constant-length regime. Thus we use a variant of Proposition B.3 and precise characterization of 1 − C B ​ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) 1-C_{B}\mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L_{i}} to get a more precise bound.
Lemma D.20 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then for all iterations 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1}
and for any horizon L i = O ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}=O(1) with i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 , we have
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 ) ⋅ d − L i ​ ( 1 − 1 0.99 ​ R i − 1 + 1 ) , | ∇ r 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ O ~ ​ ( 1 d 𝗉 2 ) ⋅ d − L i ​ ( 1 − 1 0.99 ​ R i − 1 + 1 ) . \big|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\cdot d^{-L_{i}(1-\frac{1}{0.99R^{i-1}+1})},\qquad\big|\nabla_{r}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{d_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}}\right)\cdot d^{-L_{i}(1-\frac{1}{0.99R^{i-1}+1})}.
Since L i ≥ R i − 1 ​ C B L_{i}\geq R^{i-1}C_{B} , we have L i ​ ( 1 − 1 0.99 ​ R i − 1 + 1 ) ≥ C B + Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}(1-\frac{1}{0.99R^{i-1}+1})\geq C_{B}+\Omega(1) . Compared with Lemma C.3 in Appendix C ,
Lemma D.19 and Lemma D.20 show that during warm-up the gradients contributed by
longer horizons L i L_{i} (for i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 ) are negligible relative to the shortest horizon L 1 = C B L_{1}=C_{B} .
Therefore, we can apply the constant-length analysis from Appendix C
to the warm-up stage for L 1 L_{1} , which yields the following characterization at the end of warm-up.
Lemma D.21 .
Induction D.2 holds through 0 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 0\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},1} with
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 = O ⁡ ( K ​ L max ​ L 1 η ​ log 2 ​ d ) , T_{\mathsf{mas},1}=O\left(\frac{KL_{\max}L_{1}}{\eta\log^{2}d}\right),
and at time T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},1} we have
q ( T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ L 1 log ⁡ d ) q^{(T_{\mathsf{mas},1})}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\log L_{1}}{\log d}\right) .
D.2.3 Transition between mastery states
In this part, we analyze the transition of the mastery state across consecutive horizons.
Concretely, we focus on the time interval
[ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i},\,T_{\mathsf{mas},i+1})
for i ∈ { 1 , … , K − 2 } i\in\{1,\dots,K-2\} .
As before, we fix an arbitrary i ⋆ ∈ { 1 , … , K − 2 } i^{\star}\in\{1,\dots,K-2\} for the remainder of the analysis (The restriction i ≤ K − 2 i\leq K-2 excludes the final truncated step where
L K / L K − 1 L_{K}/L_{K-1} is not necessarily R R .)
By the critical threshold of q q in Lemma D.15 , we have the following characterization of the attention scores:
Lemma D.22 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} :
1.
if i ⋆ > 1 i^{\star}>1 , then for any i < i ⋆ i<i^{\star} , we have
𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) − 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ⋆ ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}-\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}\geq\Omega(1).
2.
for i = i ⋆ i=i^{\star} , we have
1 C B + Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ L i log ⁡ d ) < 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≤ 1 − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{B}}+\Omega\Big(\frac{\log L_{i}}{\log d}\Big)<\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\leq 1-\Omega(1).
3.
if i = i ⋆ + 1 i=i^{\star}+1 , we have
1 R ​ C B ≤ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≤ 1 C B + O ⁡ ( log ⁡ L i log ⁡ d ) . \displaystyle\frac{1}{RC_{B}}\leq\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\leq\frac{1}{C_{B}}+O\Big(\frac{\log L_{i}}{\log d}\Big).
4.
if i ⋆ < K − 2 i^{\star}<K-2 , then for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we have
•
if L i = O ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}=O(1) , then 1 − C B ​ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≥ 1 − 1 0.99 ​ R i − i ⋆ − 1 + 1 1-C_{B}\mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L_{i}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{0.99R^{i-i^{\star}-1}+1} ;
•
else, 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 L i ) = o ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathbf{Attn}^{(t)}_{L_{i}}\leq O\left(\frac{1}{L_{i}}\right)=o(1) .
This immediately implies the following characterization of the logits:
Lemma D.23 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} :
1.
if i ⋆ > 1 i^{\star}>1 , then for any i < i ⋆ i<i^{\star} , we have ( p L i , 1 ( t ) ) L i ≥ Ω ⁡ ( 𝒥 L i ⋆ ( t ) ) = Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) (p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)})^{L_{i}}\geq\Omega(\mathcal{J}^{(t)}_{L_{i^{\star}}})=\Omega(1) , and also
1 − p L i , 1 ( t ) ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ ( 1 − p L i ⋆ , 1 ( t ) ) . \displaystyle 1-p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}\leq d^{-\Omega(1)}\Big(1-p_{L_{i^{\star}},1}^{(t)}\Big).
2.
for i = i ⋆ i=i^{\star} , we have
1 − p L i , 1 ( t ) = Θ ⁡ ( d − ( 1 − 1 e q ( t ) / ( L i − 1 ) + 1 ) ​ C B + 1 ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( d − ( 1 − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ C B + 1 ) . \displaystyle 1-p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}=\Theta\Big({d^{-\big(1-\frac{1}{e^{q^{(t)}}/(L_{i}-1)+1}\big)C_{B}+1}}\Big)\geq\Omega(d^{-(1-\Omega(1))C_{B}+1}).
3.
if i = i ⋆ + 1 i=i^{\star}+1 , we have
p L i , 1 ( t ) / p L i , 2 ( t ) ≥ d Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) . \displaystyle p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}/p_{L_{i},2}^{(t)}\geq d^{\Omega(1)}.
4.
if i ⋆ < K − 2 i^{\star}<K-2 , then for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we have
•
if L i = O ⁡ ( 1 ) L_{i}=O(1) , then p L i , 1 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( d − ( 1 − 1 0.99 ​ R i − i ⋆ − 1 + 1 ) ) p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}\leq O\bigg(d^{-\big(1-\frac{1}{0.99R^{i-i^{\star}-1}+1}\big)}\bigg) ;
•
else, p L i , 1 ( t ) ≤ O ⁡ ( 1 / d ) p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)}\leq O\Big(1/d\Big) .
The logit conditions also guarantee that for any i ≤ i ⋆ + 1 i\leq i^{\star}+1 , we can invoke the gradient characterization in Proposition B.2 , and for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we can invoke the gradient characterization in Proposition B.3 and the variant in Lemma D.20 . Therefore, we have the following characterization of the gradient:
Lemma D.24 .
If Induction D.1 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} ,
1.
if i ⋆ > 1 i^{\star}>1 , then for any i < i ⋆ i<i^{\star} , we have
log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ⋅ 1 d C B − 1 ≤ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) . \displaystyle\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\cdot\frac{1}{d^{C_{B}-1}}\leq\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}\leq d^{-\Omega(1)}\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}.
2.
for i = i ⋆ i=i^{\star} , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) = Θ ⁡ ( 1 − p L i , 1 ( t ) ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}=\Theta(1-p_{L_{i},1}^{(t)})\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}.
3.
if i = i ⋆ + 1 i=i^{\star}+1 , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) = Θ ⁡ ( ( 1 − p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) ​ ( p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) L i ⋆ + 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}^{(t)}=\Theta\Bigg(\Big(1-{p_{L_{i^{\star}+1},1}^{(t)}}\Big)\Big(p_{L_{i^{\star}+1},1}^{(t)}\Big)^{L_{i^{\star}+1}}\Bigg)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}.
4.
if i ⋆ < K − 2 i^{\star}<K-2 , then for any i > i ⋆ + 1 i>i^{\star}+1 , we have
| ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ( t ) | ≤ d − Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) . \displaystyle|\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i}}^{(t)}|\leq d^{-\Omega(1)}\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}.
Thus, to control the gradient of 𝒥 mix , R \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{mix},R} during [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}) , we only need to focus on the gradient of L i ⋆ L_{i^{\star}} and L i ⋆ + 1 L_{i^{\star}+1} . Similar to Lemma D.11 , we have the following lower bound for the period that the reward of L i ⋆ + 1 L_{i^{\star}+1} becomes visible.
Lemma D.25 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then for all iterations T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ≤ t < T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}\leq t<T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1} , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) + ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ d L i ⋆ + 1 ​ d 𝗉 ) . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}+\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{\log d}{L_{i^{\star}+1}d_{\mathsf{p}}}\Big).
Now we turn to the period [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}) . The main difference from the large difficulty ratio regime is that in this stage, L i ⋆ L_{i^{\star}} and L i ⋆ + 1 L_{i^{\star}+1} will jointly decide a gradient lower bound for 𝒥 mix , R \mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{mix},R} , which is significantly larger than the one in the long-plateau stage in the large difficulty ratio regime.
Lemma D.26 .
If Induction D.2 holds, then during [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}) , we have
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) + ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( d − R ​ C B R + C B + 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}+\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big({d^{-\frac{RC_{B}}{R+C_{B}}+1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}.
Proof.
Notice that during [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}) , by Lemma D.24 , ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)} is dominated by the term 1 − p L i ⋆ , 1 ( t ) 1-p_{L_{i^{\star}},1}^{(t)} . On the other hand, for L i ⋆ + 1 L_{i^{\star}+1} , by Lemma D.22 and Lemma D.24 , we have ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) \nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}+1}^{(t)} firstly dominated by the term ( p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) L i ⋆ + 1 (p_{L_{i^{\star}+1},1}^{(t)})^{L_{i^{\star}+1}} , which will increase as q q increases, and then by the term ( 1 − p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) (1-p_{L_{i^{\star}+1},1}^{(t)}) when
( p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) L i ⋆ + 1 (p_{L_{i^{\star}+1},1}^{(t)})^{L_{i^{\star}+1}} reaches the constant level, and ( 1 − p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) (1-p_{L_{i^{\star}+1},1}^{(t)}) is lower bounded by Ω ⁡ ( 1 / L i ⋆ + 1 ) \Omega(1/L_{i^{\star}+1}) . Therefore, to lower bound the gradient summation, we only need to find the time t t when ( p L i ⋆ + 1 , 1 ( t ) ) L i ⋆ + 1 (p_{L_{i^{\star}+1},1}^{(t)})^{L_{i^{\star}+1}} reaches the same level as 1 − p L i ⋆ , 1 ( t ) 1-p_{L_{i^{\star}},1}^{(t)} . Thus, consider
C B ⋅ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ⋆ − 1 = L i ⋆ + 1 ​ ( 1 − C B ⋅ 𝐀𝐭𝐭𝐧 L i ⋆ + 1 ) , C_{B}\cdot\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i^{\star}}}-1=L_{i^{\star}+1}(1-C_{B}\cdot\mathbf{Attn}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}),
which can be rewritten as
C B ​ e q e q + L i ⋆ − 1 − 1 = L i ⋆ + 1 ​ ( 1 − C B ​ e q e q + L i ⋆ + 1 − 1 ) \displaystyle C_{B}\frac{e^{q}}{e^{q}+L_{i^{\star}}-1}-1=L_{i^{\star}+1}\left(1-C_{B}\frac{e^{q}}{e^{q}+L_{i^{\star}+1}-1}\right)
(60)
which is a quadratic equation in e q e^{q} . Denoting W L ​ ( x ) = C B ​ x x + L − 1 W_{L}(x)=C_{B}\frac{x}{x+L-1} , then solving( 60 ) is equivalent to finding the solution x ⋆ x^{\star} of W L i ⋆ ​ ( x ⋆ ) − 1 = L i ⋆ + 1 ​ ( 1 − W L i ⋆ + 1 ​ ( x ⋆ ) ) W_{L_{i^{\star}}}(x^{\star})-1=L_{i^{\star}+1}(1-W_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}(x^{\star})) . Consider the point x 0 = L i ⋆ + 1 − 1 C B − 1 x_{0}=\frac{L_{i^{\star}+1}-1}{C_{B}-1} . Note that 1 − W L i ⋆ + 1 ​ ( x 0 ) = 0 1-W_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}(x_{0})=0 and W L ​ ( x ) W_{L}(x) is monotonically increasing. Thus x ⋆ < x 0 x^{\star}<x_{0} . Hence,
W L i ⋆ ​ ( x ⋆ ) − 1 ≤ W L i ⋆ ​ ( x 0 ) − 1 = C B ​ L i ⋆ + 1 − 1 L i ⋆ − 1 L i ⋆ + 1 − 1 L i ⋆ − 1 + ( C B − 1 ) − 1 . \displaystyle W_{L_{i^{\star}}}(x^{\star})-1\leq W_{L_{i^{\star}}}(x_{0})-1=\frac{C_{B}\frac{L_{i^{\star}+1}-1}{L_{i^{\star}}-1}}{\frac{L_{i^{\star}+1}-1}{L_{i^{\star}}-1}+(C_{B}-1)}-1.
Hence,
∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ ( t ) + ∇ q 𝒥 ~ L i ⋆ + 1 ( t ) ≥ Ω ⁡ ( d W L i ⋆ ​ ( x ⋆ ) − 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 ≥ ( d − C B ​ ( 1 − C B R + C B ) − 1 ) ⋅ log ⁡ d d 𝗉 . \displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}}}^{(t)}+\nabla_{q}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{L_{i^{\star}+1}}^{(t)}\geq\Omega\Big({d^{W_{L_{i^{\star}}}(x^{\star})-1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}\geq\Big({d^{-C_{B}(1-\frac{C_{B}}{R+C_{B}})-1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\log d}{d_{\mathsf{p}}}.
∎
Putting everything together, we can then characterize the relay behaviour happening during the transition period [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}) .
Lemma D.27 .
Induction D.1 holds through [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}) , where T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 = T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + O ⁡ ( d C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 ​ log ⁡ R η ​ i ⋆ ​ log ⁡ d ) T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}=T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}}+{O}\Big(\frac{d^{C_{B}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}\log R}{\eta i^{\star}\log d}\Big)
1.
the reward of J L i ⋆ + 1 J_{L_{i^{\star}+1}} only saturates below 0.01 0.01 for a time period of at most
𝒯 k ≤ O ⁡ ( d R ​ C B R + C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ log ⁡ R . \displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{k}\leq O\Big(\frac{d^{\frac{RC_{B}}{R+C_{B}}-1}Kd_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot\log R.
2.
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 − T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ≤ O ⁡ ( L i ⋆ + 1 ​ d 𝗉 ​ K η ​ log ⁡ d ) T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}-T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}\leq O(\frac{L_{i^{\star}+1}d_{\mathsf{p}}K}{\eta\log d}) .
Proof.
The proof is straightforward by Lemma D.25 and Lemma D.26 and the fact that q ( t ) q^{(t)} changes Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ R ) \Theta(\log R) during [ T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ , T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}},T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1}) and O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) during [ T 𝗏𝗂𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 , T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , i ⋆ + 1 ) [T_{\mathsf{vis},i^{\star}+1},T_{\mathsf{mas},i^{\star}+1}) due to Lemma D.2 .
∎
D.2.4 Proof of Theorem 5.2 and Corollary 5.2
Proof.
Theorem 5.2 follows immediately from Lemmas D.25 and D.27
together with the bound K = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) K=O(\log d) .
For Corollary 5.2 , we apply Lemma D.27 iteratively
for K − 2 K-2 transitions (from horizon 1 1 up to horizon K − 1 K-1 ):
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 \displaystyle T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},1}
≤ O ⁡ ( d R ​ C B R + C B − 1 ​ K ​ d 𝗉 η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ ( K − 2 ) ​ log ⁡ R + O ⁡ ( d 𝗉 ​ K η ​ log ⁡ d ) ⋅ L 1 ​ ( R K − 1 − 1 R − 1 ) \displaystyle\leq O\Big(\frac{d^{\frac{RC_{B}}{R+C_{B}}-1}\,K\,d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot(K-2)\log R\;+\;O\Big(\frac{d_{\mathsf{p}}K}{\eta\log d}\Big)\cdot L_{1}\Big(\frac{R^{K-1}-1}{R-1}\Big)
≤ O ~ ​ ( d 𝗉 η ) ⋅ d R ​ C B R + C B − 1 + O ~ ​ ( d 𝗉 η ) ⋅ L max , \displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot d^{\frac{RC_{B}}{R+C_{B}}-1}\;+\;\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot L_{\max},
where the last inequality uses K = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) K=O(\log d) and R K − 1 = O ⁡ ( L max ) R^{K-1}=O(L_{\max}) . Combining this with the condition L max = O ⁡ ( d c x ) L_{\max}=O(d^{c_{x}}) and
c x < 2 ​ C B 2 + C B ≤ R ​ C B R + C B c_{x}<\frac{2C_{B}}{2+C_{B}}\leq\frac{RC_{B}}{R+C_{B}} ,
we obtain
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 ≤ O ~ ​ ( d 𝗉 η ) ⋅ d R ​ C B R + C B − 1 . \displaystyle T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},1}\leq\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{d_{\mathsf{p}}}{\eta}\Big)\cdot d^{\frac{RC_{B}}{R+C_{B}}-1}.
Finally, by Lemma D.21 , the time spent in the warm-up stage
is negligible compared to T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , 1 T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},1} .
Moreover, we can bound the final step by
T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 ≤ O ⁡ ( T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 1 − T 𝗆𝖺𝗌 , K − 2 ) T_{\mathsf{mas},K}-T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}\leq O\big(T_{\mathsf{mas},K-1}-T_{\mathsf{mas},K-2}\big) .
This completes the proof.
∎
